Al-Ṭabarī explains the instruction as changing the throne by adding some things and removing others. Al-Qurṭubī records a simpler version: turning it upside down. Either way the object remains hers and stops looking like hers, which is the precise condition a test of recognition requires. Too small a change and the question is trivial; too large and it is unanswerable. What is being prepared is an examination with a correct answer, and the design of it tells us something about the examiner. Sulaymān is not staging a spectacle. He has taken the most impressive object he could obtain and deliberately made it less impressive in order to learn something.

**What Exactly Is Being Tested**

The stated purpose is to see whether she is guided or not — *a-tahtadī am takūnu min alladhīna lā yahtadūn*. The verb is the same one the Qur'an uses for religious guidance throughout, including in the hoopoe's report three verses earlier, where Sheba's people were described as left unguided.

That shared vocabulary creates a deliberate double meaning that the Arabic carries better than English. On the surface the question is whether she can identify a piece of furniture. Underneath, it is whether she can be guided at all. The two senses are connected by a real inference: recognising a familiar thing in unfamiliar form is exactly the cognitive act that religious guidance requires of her, since she is being asked to recognise the God she has been worshipping in the sun under a description she has not met before. Al-Ṭabarī reads the clause as waiting to see whether she recognises the throne, and Ibn Kathīr calls it a test set for her. Both are right at their own level.

**A Test Designed to Be Passed**

There is a reading of this episode in which Sulaymān is humiliating her — seizing the seat of her power and then playing games with it. The design of the test argues against it.

He does not ask her to identify the throne cold. He asks whether it is *like* hers, which supplies the comparison and invites a judgment rather than demanding certainty. He alters it partially rather than beyond recognition. And he asks before revealing what he has done, so that her answer is her own. Every choice here lowers the difficulty and preserves her standing. A ruler intending to shame a rival would have unveiled the throne in its original state and let the silence do the work. What Sulaymān constructs is closer to a question a good teacher asks: hard enough to be informative, fair enough to be answerable, and phrased so that a wrong answer costs the answerer little.

**The King Keeps Testing**

This is the third time in the chapter that Sulaymān responds to a situation by devising a test. He tested the hoopoe's report by sending a letter and watching the reply. He tested his court's capability by asking who could bring the throne. Now he tests the queen's judgment by altering it.

Three tests, three different subjects, one method. What the pattern establishes is that this king does not form conclusions from impressions, about a bird's honesty, his subordinates' powers, or a foreign monarch's capacity for truth. He builds situations that produce evidence. The chapter opened by describing people whose deeds are made to look good to them so that they wander blindly, and every one of Sulaymān's tests is a defence against exactly that condition — the condition of a person whose beliefs are never checked against anything outside himself. His kingdom includes jinn who can cross continents, and his characteristic act is still to ask a question and wait.

**Both Rulers Now Have Tested the Other**

The symmetry with what came before is complete. She sent a gift to discover what kind of man he was; he alters a throne to discover what kind of woman she is. Neither acted on assumption, and neither relied on the reports of others.

That mutual testing is what makes the meeting that follows a genuine encounter rather than a submission scene. Two rulers have each independently gathered evidence about the other, and each has run an experiment capable of returning an unwelcome result. Her test found a man who could not be bought; his is about to find a woman who can tell the truth under pressure. The Qur'an has spent fifteen verses on this exchange without either party seeing the other's face, and the whole of it has been conducted by inference from behaviour. She arrives in the next verse, and the question is put to her immediately.

**Recognition Requires Something to Recognise With**

There is an assumption behind the test worth making explicit. To identify an altered object you must hold a reliable memory of the original and be able to compare against it under interference. A person with no clear idea of their own throne would fail regardless of intelligence.

Sulaymān is therefore testing two things at once: whether she pays attention to what is hers, and whether she can hold that attention when the object in front of her contradicts it. Both are transferable capacities. The religious question she faces requires exactly the same operation — she has an inherited account of how the world is governed, and she is being asked to hold it against evidence that does not fit. A ruler who cannot tell an altered throne from an unaltered one is unlikely to manage the larger comparison. The test is practical, and its result is directly informative about the thing Sulaymān actually wants to know.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:42

> So when she arrived, it was said ˹to her˺, “Is your throne like this?” She replied, “It looks to be the same. We have ˹already˺ received knowledge ˹of Solomon’s prophethood˺ before this ˹miracle˺, and have submitted ˹to Allah˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Journey Is Skipped Entirely**

She travelled from southern Arabia to Jerusalem, and the Qur'an gives it four words: *fa-lammā jā'at*, so when she came. No account of the caravan, the distance, the escort, or what she thought on the way.

The omission is consistent with how the chapter has handled her throughout. Her name is withheld, her city is unnamed, the contents of her gift are not described, and now her journey is compressed to a subordinate clause. What the text preserves is every word she speaks and every decision she makes. This is a portrait built entirely from speech and choice, which is why the question put to her on arrival carries so much weight — it is one of only four occasions in the chapter where we hear her think.

**The Question Is Carefully Unfair**

*A-hākadhā ʿarshuk* — is your throne like this? The phrasing does not ask whether this is her throne. It asks about likeness, and the object in front of her has been altered.

Al-Qurṭubī reports the observation that the question was a trap for the intellect. A plain yes would be wrong, because the throne has been changed and is not simply as she left it. A plain no would also be wrong, because it is in fact hers. The correct answer lies between the two, and Arabic has to be handled with some care to reach it. Nothing in her situation helps. She has arrived after a long journey in a foreign court, is being questioned without preamble by a king who refused her gift and threatened her kingdom, and is shown an object that should be a thousand miles away. The pressure is considerable and the question is a puzzle.

**"It Looks to Be the Same"**

Her answer is *ka-annahu huwa* — as though it were it. Three words, and they thread the needle exactly.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the reply as expressing doubt, and that is the surface of it. But al-Qurṭubī's reading is the better one: she assessed the object correctly. It was neither entirely the same nor entirely different, and she said so with a particle of comparison rather than a verb of identification. That is not evasion. It is a precise report of an uncertain perception, which is the hardest kind of honesty to produce under pressure, because both confident answers are socially easier. She could have claimed recognition and looked perceptive; she could have denied it and looked unshakeable. She stated what she actually saw. Everything the chapter has shown of her — the fair judgment of the letter, the consultation, the test by gift — comes to a point in these three words.

**Who Speaks the Second Sentence?**

The verse's second half is disputed among the commentators, and the two readings change its meaning considerably. On the reading this translation follows, the queen continues speaking: knowledge came to her before this, and she had already submitted. Al-Zamakhsharī records this as a minority position — that she is acknowledging the power of God and the truth of Sulaymān's prophethood before the throne and the pavilion were shown to her.

The majority took the words as Sulaymān's, spoken to his court: we were given knowledge before her and we were submitters. Al-Ṭabarī reads the following verse as continuing that speech. Both are grammatically available, since Arabic supplies no speech markers here. The minority reading has a real advantage: it explains why she is described as arriving at all, since a woman travelling to a king who demanded her submission has already conceded something, and it makes her later declaration in the palace a completion rather than a reversal. The majority reading has the advantage of the following verse, which speaks of her in the third person as one hindered by what she worshipped — an awkward thing for her to say of herself.

**Knowledge Before the Miracle**

Whichever speaker is intended, the sentence makes the same claim in substance: the knowledge preceded the wonder. Either Sulaymān's court had it before she arrived, or she had it before the throne was shown.

That ordering is the theological point and it runs against the obvious reading of the scene. A displaced throne looks like the sort of thing that would compel belief, and the chapter is at pains to say it did not do the work. Miracles in the Qur'an consistently confirm what reasoning has already made available rather than substituting for it; Pharaoh's court saw nine of them and remained where it was. If the words are hers, she is saying she had worked out enough before arriving that the spectacle in front of her is confirmation rather than cause. If they are his, he is saying the same about his own people. Either way the sentence declines to credit the miracle with the conversion, and the narrative bears that out — her declaration of faith comes two verses later, prompted by something far smaller than a flying throne.

**"It Was Said" — the Questioner Goes Unnamed**

The verse uses a passive: *qīla*, it was said. Sulaymān gave the order to alter the throne in the previous verse, but the question itself is attributed to nobody in particular.

The construction recurs in this passage — she is told to enter the palace in the same impersonal form two verses later — and its effect is to take the king out of the frame at the moments that matter most for her. She is not being interrogated by a rival monarch; she is being presented with a question and a doorway. Whatever she concludes, she concludes without a face to concede to. That anonymity is consistent with how the whole encounter has been managed, from a letter that put God's name before Sulaymān's to a correction about the floor delivered as a flat statement of fact. The Qur'an is arranging a sequence in which nothing she does can be read as deference to the man in the room.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:43

> But she had been hindered by what she used to worship instead of Allah, for she was indeed from a disbelieving people.

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Was Blocking Her**

The verse names the obstacle precisely: *mā kānat taʿbudu min dūni Allāh* — what she used to worship instead of God. Not her pride, not her throne, not her advisors. The sun.

Al-Ṭabarī's reading is the plain one: her worship of the sun prevented her from worshipping God properly. The verb *ṣaddahā*, she was barred, is the same verb the hoopoe used in verse 24 when he said Satan had barred them from the way. The chapter is consistent about the mechanism. An existing object of worship does not leave a vacancy; it occupies the position, and the occupation is what has to be cleared before anything else can be placed there. This is why Sulaymān's letter demanded submission rather than argument, and why the hoopoe's speech began by denying before it affirmed.

**A Past Tense That Matters**

The Arabic construction *mā kānat taʿbudu* describes a habitual practice in the past — what she used to worship. By the time this sentence is spoken, the worship is being described as something behind her.

That tense is doing quiet work in the narrative. The verse is not saying she is currently blocked; it is explaining why she had not arrived sooner. Read alongside the previous verse, where knowledge is said to have come before the miracle, the two statements fit together into an account of a slow approach: she had reasoned her way toward something, and an inherited religion had held her short of it. The Qur'an is describing a delay rather than a refusal, which is precisely what distinguishes her from Pharaoh's court. They knew and refused. She was hindered, and the hindrance is named as an external inheritance rather than an act of will.

**"From a Disbelieving People"**

The verse's final clause locates the problem in her origin: she was of a people who did not believe. This is the only place in her narrative where the Qur'an offers an explanation of her condition, and the explanation is social rather than personal.

Nobody chooses the religion they are born into. A queen of Sheba inherited sun worship the way she inherited the throne, as the established fact of her world, and the verse says so without softening and without excusing. The Qur'an holds people responsible for what they do with what reaches them, and it is consistently willing to name the starting position as a starting position. The same Book records the Quraysh objecting that they found their fathers on a path and were following it — there the plea is rejected, because a messenger had come and been refused. Here the circumstance is stated as the cause of a delay that ends the moment the case is properly put to her.

**Her Failing Is Named Without Contempt**

Compare how the chapter has spoken of its two failing courts. Pharaoh's people were *corruptors* who denied wrongfully and arrogantly, having known the truth. She is described as hindered, and the thing that hindered her is given the active verb while she takes the passive.

That grammatical distribution is not accidental. Nothing in this verse calls her arrogant, deceitful or corrupt, and the chapter has just spent four verses displaying her judgment, her consultation and her honesty under questioning. What it faults is one thing, stated once, in a single clause. The Qur'an is capable of severity toward its characters and applies it where it applies; the restraint here is a judgment of its own. She had one error, it was serious, and it was the only one — which is the precondition for a narrative that ends in her own voice rather than in her destruction.

**Two Ways to Read This Verse's Speaker**

If the previous verse's second half was Sulaymān speaking, this verse continues him, and al-Ṭabarī reads it that way. If the queen was speaking there, then this verse is the Qur'an's own narration, breaking in to explain her position.

The second option is the more natural for this particular sentence. The description is in the third person, it assesses her from outside, and it supplies a causal account of her past that a speaker would not ordinarily produce about herself in a foreign court. Al-Zamakhsharī's minority reading of the previous verse and this narratorial reading of the present one fit together into a coherent whole: she states what she has come to know, and the text explains what had kept her from it. Either way the content is the same, and the narrative is now positioned for its final move. The obstacle has been named. What remains is the moment that removes it, and it arrives in the next verse through something as ordinary as a floor.

**Worship as an Occupied Position**

The image behind *ṣadda* is physical obstruction — something standing in a road. Applied to worship, it produces a claim about how belief actually works that the chapter has been making since its fourth verse.

A person is not a blank surface waiting for a true doctrine to be written on it. Whatever place in the self is reserved for ultimate loyalty is already filled, by the sun, by ancestry, by wealth, by the state, or by the self. This is why the Qur'an's creedal formula begins with a denial and why Sulaymān's letter demanded submission rather than assent to a proposition. Nothing can be installed until the previous occupant is removed. Her case is the clean illustration: an intelligent, fair-minded ruler who had reasoned her way to the edge of the truth and could not cross, because the position was taken. What finally clears it is not an argument at all.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:44

> Then she was told, “Enter the palace.” But when she saw the hall, she thought it was a body of water, so she bared her legs. Solomon said. “It is just a palace paved with crystal.” ˹At last˺ she declared, “My Lord! I have certainly wronged my soul. Now I ˹fully˺ submit myself along with Solomon to Allah, the Lord of all worlds.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Floor Does What a Flying Throne Could Not**

The throne crossed a continent in an instant and produced from her a careful, measured answer about resemblance. A glass floor produces a declaration of faith. The chapter has arranged these two events in this order deliberately.

What changes between them is not the scale of the marvel but its relation to her. The throne was a wonder performed in front of her, impressive and external. The floor is a mistake she makes herself, in her own body, in public. She looks at a surface, concludes it is water, and lifts her skirts to wade — and is wrong. That is a different kind of evidence entirely. A person can admire a miracle and remain unchanged; a person who has just been shown that her own eyes reported something that was not there has learned something about herself rather than about the performer.

**The Physics of the Illusion**

The Arabic is *ṣarḥun mumarradun min qawārīr* — a structure paved smooth with glass. Commentators note that part of the effect came from fish swimming beneath the crystal, which al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī all mention, and which would make the reading of water not merely plausible but nearly forced.

The detail changes the character of her error. She was not being careless. Confronted with a transparent surface, moving shapes underneath, and a flat expanse stretching away, any competent observer would conclude there was water. Her inference was sound and her premises were wrong. The Qur'an is describing the most respectable kind of mistake — the one produced by reasoning correctly from what appears — which is precisely the mistake her whole nation had made about the sun. They looked at the greatest visible power and drew the obvious conclusion. Here, in miniature and in a single second, she is shown the shape of her error without anyone having to name it.

**Sulaymān Corrects Her Immediately**

The moment she acts on the mistake, he tells her the truth. *Innahu ṣarḥun mumarradun min qawārīr* — it is a hall paved with glass. No pause, no enjoyment of the moment, no lesson drawn aloud.

The speed is the courtesy. A host who wanted to make a point would have let the misunderstanding run; Sulaymān ends it in the next breath and states the fact flatly, without adding an interpretation. The absence of any moral commentary from him is conspicuous. He does not say *as you were mistaken about this, so you were mistaken about the sun*, and he does not need to. She draws the conclusion herself in the same verse, which is the only way the conclusion could have been worth drawing. A realisation handed to someone is an assertion; a realisation reached is a conviction.

**The Folklore Around This Verse, and Why It Should Go**

A body of legend attached itself to this scene. The stories hold that Sulaymān's jinn told him the queen's mother was a jinn and that her legs were deformed, and that the glass floor was contrived to expose them; further tales have him marrying her afterwards. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī all record versions of this material.

Ibn Kathīr's judgment on it is the correct one. He sees little value in the folklore and traces it to Arab legend and to oral traditions carried in by converts from Judaism and Christianity — the category the scholars call *isrāʾīliyyāt*. Nothing in the Qur'an supports any of it. The text says she saw a surface, judged it water, and acted; it says nothing about her legs beyond the ordinary act of lifting a garment to cross water, and it reports no marriage. Ibn Kathīr's own handling confirms the classification. Where he transmits the jinn-mother material he names ath-Thaʿlabī as its channel, and where a report is attached to the Prophet he flags the weakness of the narrator; on the marriage he records a version from Wahb ibn Munabbih and states that it is not authenticated. A body of narrative whose most sympathetic transmitter marks it as unauthenticated at each point where it touches a chain is not a competing account of the episode. The episode as the Qur'an tells it is about perception and it ends in a declaration of faith.

**"I Have Wronged My Soul"**

Her confession uses a formula the Qur'an gives to others at their turning points. *Rabbi innī ẓalamtu nafsī* — my Lord, I have wronged myself. Mūsā says almost exactly this after the killing in Egypt: *“My Lord! I have definitely wronged my soul, so forgive me.”* (28:16).

The construction places the injury on herself. She does not say she was deceived by her people, misled by her advisors, or trapped by an inherited religion — all of which the previous verse had, in effect, said on her behalf. The Qur'an supplied her excuse and she declines to use it. That refusal is what makes the confession a real one. There is also something exact in the verb: *ẓulm* is putting a thing where it does not belong, and misdirected worship is precisely that, a right given to the wrong recipient. She names her error by its correct technical description without any prompting.

**Submitting "Along With Solomon," Not to Him**

Her final words are the most carefully constructed sentence anyone speaks in the chapter: *aslamtu maʿa Sulaymān li-Llāhi rabb al-ʿālamīn* — I submit, with Sulaymān, to God, the Lord of all worlds.

Three things are settled at once. She places herself beside him rather than beneath him, which the letter's wording had always permitted. She names the recipient of her submission as God, not the king who summoned her. And she uses the widest possible title, Lord of all worlds — the same phrase spoken at the fire in verse 8. Ibn ʿArabī drew the comparison that makes this land: her submission was superior to Pharaoh's, because she submitted to the Lord of all worlds without qualification, whereas he, at the moment the sea closed over him, submitted to the Lord of Mūsā and Hārūn. Pharaoh conceded a God attached to two men. She names God as He is. The chapter's two royal narratives end in the same act performed at two entirely different depths, and Sheba's story closes here, with the last word hers.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:45

> And We certainly sent to the people of Thamûd their brother Ṣâliḥ, proclaiming, “Worship Allah,” but they suddenly split into two opposing groups.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A New People, a Familiar Opening**

Sheba's story ended in a declaration of faith. The chapter now moves to Thamūd, and the shift in register is immediate. Where the Sulaymān narrative ran for thirty verses with dialogue, deliberation and tests, this one opens with a formula the Qur'an uses again and again: We sent to them their brother, saying worship God.

The compression signals what kind of story this is. Thamūd's account appears in several chapters — al-Aʿrāf, Hūd, ash-Shuʿarāʾ, al-Qamar — and readers of the Qur'an knew it. An-Naml is not retelling it but selecting from it, and what this chapter takes is the part about a plot and its unravelling. The people were an ancient Arabian nation, remembered in the Qur'an for carving dwellings out of rock, and their ruins in northwestern Arabia stood on routes the first audience used. This is not distant legend for them. It is a place they could visit.

**"Their Brother" Is a Deliberate Word**

Ṣāliḥ is called *akhāhum*, their brother. The Qur'an applies the same term to Hūd with ʿĀd, to Lūṭ with his people, to Shuʿayb with Madyan. The word means one of them by descent, sharing their lineage and their speech.

This detail forecloses a standard objection. A messenger who arrives from outside can be dismissed as a foreigner who does not understand the community, its history or its arrangements. Ṣāliḥ has no such distance available to him, and neither do his people. He is their kinsman, raised among them, speaking their dialect, and whatever he is telling them cannot be filed under the customs of some other nation. The Qur'an makes the same point about Muḥammad ﷺ to the Quraysh, who had known him for forty years before he recited anything. Sending a brother removes the easiest excuse before the message is delivered.

**The Whole Message in Two Words**

What Ṣāliḥ proclaims is *an uʿbudū Allāh* — worship God. That is the entire content of his mission as this verse reports it. No law, no ritual, no social programme, no argument against the existing gods.

The Qur'an's prophetic missions are consistently summarised this way, and the summary is not laziness. Everything else these messengers said — and Thamūd's story elsewhere includes warnings about a she-camel, about corruption in the land, about the treatment of the poor — follows from that one instruction and is unintelligible without it. Placing worship first also identifies where the conflict actually lies. Thamūd did not reject Ṣāliḥ over an argument about ethics; they rejected him over a question of who is owed worship, and the social consequences followed. A chapter that has just shown a queen redirecting her worship and a court refusing to redirect theirs states the demand here in its barest form.

**The Split Is Instantaneous**

The verb is *fa-idhā hum farīqāni yakhtaṣimūn* — and at once they were two parties, disputing. The particle *idhā* in this construction marks something sudden and unlooked-for, the narrative equivalent of a jump.

Al-Ṭabarī identifies the two groups plainly: those who believed Ṣāliḥ and those who denied him. What the grammar adds is the speed. Two words of proclamation, and a unified nation is a divided one before anything else has happened. This is a general observation the Qur'an makes about revelation in several places: a message that requires a decision cannot be received by a community without dividing it, because assent and refusal are the only available responses and both will be taken. The division is not the messenger's fault and it is not presented as a failure of his mission. It is what happens when a real demand reaches people who were previously agreed only because nothing had been asked of them.

**Two Parties Quarrelling, Not Two Opinions Coexisting**

The participle is *yakhtaṣimūn*, from a root meaning to litigate, to contend as adversaries. It is not the word for holding different views. It describes active conflict, each side pressing against the other.

That escalation is what the rest of the Thamūd narrative in this chapter will trace. Within four verses the dispute will have produced a conspiracy to murder Ṣāliḥ and his household by night. The Qur'an is showing the sequence rather than asserting it: a proclamation, an immediate split, a hardening into factions, and then violence planned in secret by nine men. Nothing in the progression requires an unusual amount of wickedness at any single step, which is why it is worth tracing. The chapter's earlier verses described people whose deeds are made to look good to them, and a faction that has spent enough time contending will find that murder has come to look like self-defence. Ṣāliḥ's first recorded words to them come next, and they are a question about why they are in such a hurry.

**Where Thamūd's Story Stands in the Chapter**

The chapter's four narratives are arranged with care. Mūsā before a court that knew and refused; Sulaymān and a queen who did not know and accepted; now Thamūd, and after them the people of Lūṭ. Two prophets sent to foreign powers, then two sent to their own kin.

That second pair changes the terms. Mūsā walked into Egypt as an outsider with a demand, and the hostility he met is at least intelligible. Ṣāliḥ speaks to his own nation, in their language, about the God they nominally acknowledged, and the response is a plot to kill him in the night. The chapter is closing off the explanations that let a reader keep the lesson at a distance. It is not foreignness that produces the refusal, and it is not the strangeness of the message. The Quraysh hearing this sūrah had a brother of their own reciting it to them, and the parallel needs no drawing.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:46

> He urged ˹the disbelieving group˺, “O my people! Why do you ˹seek to˺ hasten the torment rather than grace? If only you sought Allah’s forgiveness so you may be shown mercy!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**He Still Calls Them His People**

The nation has split into two hostile factions and Ṣāliḥ addresses the hostile one as *yā qawmi* — O my people. The possessive is not a formality. It is the same word a man uses of the tribe he belongs to, and he keeps using it after they have declared against him.

The Qur'an gives this vocative to every prophet sent to his own kin, and it survives escalation in each case. Nūḥ uses it through centuries of rejection; Shuʿayb uses it while being threatened with stoning. What the address preserves is the relationship the message is meant to save. A messenger who began saying *you people* would have conceded the separation his opponents were trying to create, and the separation is the thing he is arguing against. Within three verses a group of them will be plotting to kill him and his household in the dark, and he is at this moment still claiming them as his own.

**The Strange Charge of Being in a Hurry**

His question is not the one a reader expects. He does not ask why they disbelieve, why they mock, or why they threaten. He asks why they are rushing: *lima tastaʿjilūna bi'l-sayyi'ati qabla al-ḥasanah* — why do you seek to hasten the evil before the good?

Al-Ṭabarī reads the evil as God's punishment and the good as His mercy, which makes the question literal. They had been demanding the torment Ṣāliḥ warned of, in the manner the Qur'an records of several rejecting peoples: bring it on, if you are telling the truth. Al-Qurṭubī adds a second layer, that their deeds themselves were the evil they were hastening — they were performing the acts that earn punishment instead of those that earn reward, and so hurrying toward one outcome while a better one stood available. The two readings work together. Demanding the punishment verbally and earning it practically are the same impatience expressed twice.

**Why Anyone Would Demand Their Own Destruction**

The behaviour looks irrational until its function is understood. A man who challenges a prophet to produce the threatened disaster is not seeking death; he is seeking the discomfort's end. Warnings that hang unfulfilled are harder to live with than verdicts, because a verdict can be absorbed and a warning cannot.

There is also a rhetorical calculation. If the punishment does not arrive, the challenger has publicly exposed the warner, and every day of delay after the challenge is counted as evidence. The Qur'an records the same tactic from the Quraysh, who asked for stones from the sky if the recitation were true, and it answers such demands consistently by pointing at the delay itself as mercy rather than weakness. Ṣāliḥ's question exposes what the demand actually costs. They are using the interval — the only thing standing between them and the outcome — as ammunition against the man warning them about it.

**Forgiveness Is Offered With No Preconditions**

The second half of his speech is the alternative: *lawlā tastaghfirūna Allāh laʿallakum turḥamūn* — why not seek God's forgiveness, so that you may be shown mercy. Nothing is attached to the offer. No compensation, no penance, no probationary term.

The construction is worth noticing. *Lawlā* with a present verb functions in Arabic as an urging rather than a question — closer to *why not simply* than to a demand for an explanation. And the outcome clause uses *laʿalla*, a particle of hope rather than guarantee, which keeps the promise honest: seeking forgiveness is what opens the possibility, and the granting remains God's. Ṣāliḥ is not selling a transaction. He is telling people standing at one end of a road that the other end is still reachable, and the only thing required to start is to ask. Set against the length of the punishment they are demanding, the cost of the alternative is nothing at all.

**Two Directions Available From the Same Position**

The verse describes a fork. From exactly where Thamūd is standing, two things can be hastened, and they have chosen the one that ends them. That is the structure Ṣāliḥ is trying to make visible.

The Qur'an uses the same pairing elsewhere in a near-identical formula, describing people who would have a prophet hasten the evil before the good although examples of earlier nations had already passed. The repetition across chapters indicates a recognised pattern rather than one nation's peculiarity. What makes it durable is that hastening the evil never feels like hastening anything; it feels like standing firm, refusing to be intimidated, declining to be manipulated by a threat. Every quality the faction would have claimed for itself — courage, scepticism, loyalty to the ancestral way — is engaged in the choice. Their answer to Ṣāliḥ comes in the next verse, and it does not address his question at all.

**Seeking Forgiveness Is the Whole Requirement**

The word Ṣāliḥ uses for the remedy is *istighfār*, a verbal form that means to request pardon. Arabic builds this pattern for asking: the same shape gives the verbs for seeking help and seeking refuge. What he prescribes is an act of asking, not an act of repair.

That is significant given who he is addressing. These are people who have hamstrung a she-camel in other accounts of this story, who have divided their city, and who are shortly going to plan a murder. Nothing in his offer requires them to undo any of it first, and nothing establishes a minimum standard for eligibility. The gate is asking. The Qur'an keeps this threshold low with striking consistency, promising elsewhere that those who have exceeded all limits against themselves should not despair. Ṣāliḥ is putting the same offer to a faction currently in open dispute with him, and he puts it as a question rather than a condition, which is the least coercive form available to him.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:47

> They replied, “You and your followers are a bad omen for us.” He responded, “Your omens are destined by Allah. In fact, you are ˹only˺ a people being tested.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Reply That Changes the Subject**

Ṣāliḥ asked why they were hurrying toward punishment when forgiveness was available. Their answer addresses none of it. They tell him he is bad luck.

The evasion is total and it is the most revealing thing they say. A counter-argument would have engaged the question; an accusation of ill omen relocates the discussion entirely, from what they should do to what he is. It also converts a demand into a grievance. Once he and his followers are classified as a source of misfortune, everything that has gone wrong in the community since he started speaking becomes his doing, and the community becomes the injured party. The Qur'an records other peoples reaching for the same move — Pharaoh's people blamed Mūsā and those with him for their bad fortune, and the townsfolk in Yā Sīn told their messengers the same thing. The tactic recurs because it requires no evidence.

**What Augury Actually Was**

The word they use is *iṭṭayyarnā*, built on the root *ṭ-y-r*, birds. The vocabulary preserves the practice it came from. Pre-Islamic Arabs read the future by releasing or startling a bird and watching which way it flew: to the right, a good sign; to the left, a bad one.

Knowing this makes the exchange sharper in a chapter titled after an insect and largely concerned with a hoopoe. Sheba's fate turned on a bird that carried real information across a real distance, and Thamūd is using birds as a random oracle. One is observation; the other is the appearance of observation. The linguistic fossil also explains why the Qur'an treats augury as a matter of speech rather than of science — the whole apparatus consists of assigning a meaning to something that carries none, and then acting on the meaning. Islam later prohibited the practice explicitly, and the prohibition is usually traced to exactly this reasoning rather than to a rival system of divination.

**"Your Omens Are With God"**

Ṣāliḥ's reply is compressed: *ṭā'irukum ʿinda Allāh* — your bird, your omen, is with God. The commentators unfold it in slightly different ways that add up to one position.

Al-Qurṭubī takes it to mean that the things they predict from omens are with God; al-Ṭabarī, that such matters are known to Him alone; al-Rāzī, that whatever good or evil reaches them originates with God rather than with any augury. The common claim is that causation has been misassigned. Something does determine what befalls them, and it is not the direction a startled bird flies, and it is not the presence of a preacher in their town. The answer is economical because the error is simple. They have taken a real question — why are things going badly — and attached it to a mechanism that cannot answer it.

**The Diagnosis: You Are Being Tested**

The second half of his reply is the substantive correction: *bal antum qawmun tuftanūn* — rather, you are a people being tried. The particle *bal* cancels what preceded it. Not an omen; a test.

Al-Ṭabarī's reading is that the trial consisted precisely in Ṣāliḥ being sent to them. That turns their accusation inside out. They said his arrival was their misfortune; he says his arrival is their examination. Both statements agree that his presence is the decisive new factor in their community's life, and they disagree completely about what that means. The difference is between being a victim of circumstances and being a candidate under assessment, and only one of those descriptions leaves anything to do. A cursed people can only complain. A tested people can pass.

**Superstition as an Escape From Responsibility**

Behind the exchange lies a general claim about why augury appeals. An omen explains misfortune without implicating anyone who holds it. If the cause of your troubles is a bird, or a stranger, or a preacher, then nothing about your own conduct requires examination.

That is the function Ṣāliḥ's reply attacks. His correction does not merely deny their metaphysics; it returns the question to them. A test has a subject, and the subject is the one being tested. This is why the Qur'an's objection to divination is moral before it is theological — the practice is condemned not chiefly because it is inaccurate but because it moves the locus of responsibility out of the person and into the sky. Thamūd's nine leading men are introduced in the next verse, and what they do with their responsibility is the last stage of this story.

**What They Actually Accused Him Of**

Their charge is worth stating precisely, because it is not a claim that he is lying. They do not say his message is false, his God does not exist, or his warnings are invented. They say he brings bad luck — *iṭṭayyarnā bika wa biman maʿak*, we take you and those with you as an evil sign.

That formulation concedes a great deal. It accepts that something has changed in their situation since he began speaking and that the change is real enough to need explaining. What it denies is only the explanation he offers. In place of a test they substitute a curse, which keeps the facts and reverses the responsibility. They also extend the charge to his followers, which is how factional thinking works: the believing party from verse 45 is now a category of person rather than a set of positions, and misfortune is attributed to their presence rather than to anything they have done. Ṣāliḥ's reply refuses both halves in one sentence.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:48

> And there were in the city nine ˹elite˺ men who spread corruption in the land, never doing what is right.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Precise Count**

The Qur'an gives a number: nine. Not a faction, not a majority, not the people of the city — nine men, in one settlement, named by their count and their conduct.

The specificity narrows the story considerably. Thamūd had split into two parties in verse 45, and the disputing group was presumably large. Yet what follows — the conspiracy, the oath, the plan to murder Ṣāliḥ's household by night — is the work of this small number. Al-Ṭabarī identifies them as the ones who acted together to hamstring the she-camel and then conspired against the prophet. The Qur'an is describing how a nation's destruction is actually organised, and the answer is that it does not require many people. It requires nine, in the right positions, and a population that does not stop them.

**They Were Notables, Not Outcasts**

Al-Qurṭubī records that these men were among the city's leading figures, and lists among their offences corrupt lending practices and a habit of dishonouring and humiliating people. That places them at the top of the social order rather than at its margins.

The detail matters for how the corruption is to be understood. A city's nine most powerful men do not spread *fasād* by petty crime; they do it through the instruments they control — credit, contracts, and the informal machinery by which some people are made small in front of others. The lending charge is concrete and recognisable. A creditor with standing can ruin a family legally, and repeated often enough the practice reorganises a whole society around debt and obligation. Ṣāliḥ's message to them was to worship God, and the Qur'an's other accounts of Thamūd add warnings about how they treated the weak. A demand for worship and a demand for justice arrive together, and the men with most to lose from the second are the ones who organise against the first.

**Corruption and Repair Are the Qur'an's Standard Pair**

The verse defines them twice, positively and negatively: they work *fasād* in the land and do not *iṣlāḥ*. The two roots are paired throughout the Qur'an as opposites — spoiling and setting right, breaking and mending.

The negative half is not filler. It closes an excuse that would otherwise be available, since a man who causes damage can always point to something he also built. These nine are described as doing no repair at all, which turns a description of acts into a description of a settled direction. The pairing also defines corruption usefully. *Fasād* in the Qur'an is not primarily personal vice; the root refers to something going rotten, a functioning thing being spoiled. A society is a working arrangement, and corruption is what makes it stop working — which is exactly what predatory lending and routine humiliation do to the bonds that let people live together.

**The Word for City and What It Implies**

They are located *fī al-madīnah*, in the city. Thamūd's dwellings are described elsewhere in the Qur'an as carved into rock, and the ruins in northwestern Arabia — monumental tomb façades cut into sandstone outcrops — were known to the Prophet's contemporaries and are visible today.

This was a settled, wealthy, technically accomplished civilisation, not a wandering tribe. That context bears on the charges. Corrupt lending requires a monetary economy; a class of notables requires an established hierarchy; a city requires organisation that can be captured. The Qur'an's destroyed nations are consistently advanced ones, and the pattern is part of the argument it makes to the Quraysh, themselves a prosperous merchant community. The chapter has already condemned Egypt and corrected Sheba, both sophisticated states. Thamūd is the third, and its ruins were on the road north.

**Nine Men and a Silent Majority**

The verse raises a question it does not answer: where was everyone else? A city contained these nine. The believing party existed, the disputing party existed, and the plot proceeded.

The Qur'an's destruction narratives generally fall on whole peoples while identifying particular instigators, and the tension between those two facts is the uncomfortable part. What the structure implies is that a community is answerable for what its leading men are permitted to do in it. Nine notables ruining a city by lending and humiliation could be stopped by a population that declined to accept the arrangement; they are not stopped, and the arrangement holds. That is not an accusation the verse makes explicitly, and it should not be pressed further than the text allows. What the text does make explicit is that these nine were in the city and were doing this, stated as a fact everybody in the story would have known. Their private conversation is reported next, and it shows what men with that much standing thought they could get away with.

**Why the Number Is Given at All**

The Qur'an rarely counts the wicked. It names peoples, cities and courts, and it identifies Pharaoh and Hāmān; it does not usually say how many men were in a conspiracy. Here it does, and the precision has an effect.

A number makes the group finite and therefore resistible. Nine men are not a tide of history or an inevitable social force; they are nine individuals who could be identified, opposed, and refused. Stating the figure removes the excuse that the corruption was simply how things were. It also fixes responsibility with a kind of legal exactness, distinguishing the instigators from the disputing faction at large and from the city that housed them. The chapter has been careful about attribution throughout — the hoopoe blamed Satan for adorning Sheba's deeds, Ṣāliḥ blamed nothing on omens — and this verse continues the pattern by naming the actual agents rather than gesturing at a climate.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:49

> They vowed, “Let us swear by Allah that we will take him and his family down by night. Then we will certainly say to his ˹closest˺ heirs, ‘We did not witness the murder of his family. We are definitely telling the truth.’”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Swearing by God to Commit Murder**

The conspirators bind themselves with an oath, and the oath is *taqāsamū bi-Llāh* — by God. They invoke the name of the deity whose messenger they are planning to kill, as the guarantee of their agreement with one another.

This is the sharpest irony in the chapter and it is not accidental. Thamūd were not atheists. They acknowledged God in the way their society acknowledged many things, as a name to swear by, and Ṣāliḥ's demand that they worship Him is what they are conspiring against. An oath in His name is the strongest bond available in their culture, and they reach for it instinctively to secure a murder. What the detail exposes is the difference between recognising a name and submitting to what it refers to. Verse 14 described a court that knew and refused; this is a smaller version, where the knowledge has been reduced to a formula for making promises binding.

**The Plan Is Made at Night for a Reason**

The verb is *nubayyitannahu* — we shall come upon him by night. Arabic has a specific term for a night attack, and it names a recognised form of tribal raid: fall on a household in the dark, when defence is impossible and witnesses are asleep.

Two purposes are served by the timing. The practical one is that the target cannot resist. The other is concealment, and it is the one the rest of the verse elaborates. Al-Ṭabarī notes that they swore to kill Ṣāliḥ and to provide alibis for one another afterward, so the darkness is part of an evidentiary strategy rather than merely a tactic. Men who intend to lie about their whereabouts choose an hour when nobody can contradict them. The plan is thus two plans — an attack and a cover — and the second is discussed at greater length in this verse than the first.

**The Family Is Included**

The target is not Ṣāliḥ alone: *nubayyitannahu wa ahlahu*, him and his household. The Qur'an states it flatly and adds no comment.

Extending the attack to a man's family had a logic in a tribal society, since a surviving son or brother carries the obligation of retaliation, and a killing that leaves claimants alive is unfinished business. That logic is what turns an assassination into a massacre. Whatever these nine told themselves about Ṣāliḥ deserving what he got — the accusation of ill omen from two verses earlier supplies a candidate justification — no version of it covers the household. The chapter's fourth verse described people whose deeds are made appealing to them, and a plan that has grown from silencing a preacher to killing his family in their sleep is that process completed. Nobody in the room objects.

**The Alibi Is Prepared in Advance**

The second half of the verse is a rehearsed statement: we shall tell his heir that we did not witness the destruction of his family, and we are telling the truth. They are drafting testimony before the event it concerns.

Al-Qurṭubī explains the target of the lie: they wanted an excuse ready for the person on whom retaliation would fall — the *walī*, the heir who inherits the right to demand redress. This is a legal calculation, made inside a functioning system of blood justice. The conspirators are not planning to escape law; they are planning to satisfy it with a false statement. That tells us what kind of men they are more precisely than the murder does. They expect to be questioned, they expect the questioning to matter, and they have decided that a coordinated lie will hold. Nine notables who own the city's credit and its dignity have reason to think their word will be accepted.

**"We Are Definitely Telling the Truth"**

The rehearsed speech ends with an assertion of honesty: *wa innā la-ṣādiqūn*. They are composing, in advance, the sentence with which they will vouch for a lie, and they are adding the emphatic particles that Arabic reserves for insistence.

There is a dark precision in this. The most heavily emphasised claim to truthfulness in the chapter comes from men constructing a perjury, and the emphasis is itself the tell — a truthful witness does not usually need to certify himself. The Qur'an has been building a vocabulary around truth and certainty since verse 3, where the believers were marked by *yaqīn*, and verse 14, where Pharaoh's court was certain and denied anyway, and verse 27, where Sulaymān said he would see whether the hoopoe was truthful. Here the word arrives in the mouths of conspirators as decoration on a fabrication. The verse leaves them at the moment the plan is complete, and the next verse says what happened to it.

**The Timing in the Wider Account**

The Qur'an's other chapters supply the surrounding events. Thamūd had demanded a sign, been given a she-camel, and hamstrung it; Ṣāliḥ then told them they had a fixed number of days left. The number of days is not left to the commentators. Hūd states it: *“But they killed her, so he warned ˹them˺, ‘You have ˹only˺ three ˹more˺ days to enjoy life in your homes— this is an unfailing promise!’”* (11:65). Several commentators accordingly place this conspiracy inside that three-day window.

If that placement is right, it makes the plot considerably stranger. These men had been told a deadline and are using the time inside it to arrange a murder — hastening the evil before the good, exactly as verse 46 described. They are also attempting to kill a man whose prediction of their destruction has not yet been falsified, which is a poor use of a countdown. Two things should be kept apart here. The three days are the Qur'an's own datum, stated in Hūd and not an inference. What the commentators add is the placement of *this particular meeting* inside those days, which an-Naml does not date; the sūrah reports the plot without a calendar. The reconstruction is reasonable and the deadline behind it is scriptural, but only the deadline is.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:50

> And ˹so˺ they made a plan, but We too made a plan, while they were unaware.

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Verb, Used Twice**

The Arabic repeats the same word deliberately: *wa makarū makran wa makarnā makran*. They plotted a plot, and We plotted a plot. The verb and its cognate object appear on both sides of the sentence, and the mirroring is the whole point.

The construction is a known rhetorical device and the Qur'an uses it in several places where human scheming is answered. It produces an effect no paraphrase can reproduce: the divine response is described in precisely the vocabulary the conspirators used for their own act, so that their word comes back at them unchanged. Whatever they thought they were doing, something of the same name was being done about them at the same time. The verse's last clause seals it — *wa hum lā yashʿurūn*, while they did not perceive. They had a plan and no idea they were inside one.

**Does God Plot?**

The word *makr* in Arabic normally carries deception, and applying it to God raises an obvious difficulty. The Qur'an nonetheless uses it, here and elsewhere, and the solution the commentators reached is a linguistic one rather than an evasion.

*Makr* at root means a covert stratagem — a plan whose workings are hidden from the one it concerns. Deceit is a frequent feature of human stratagems, not the meaning of the word itself. When the Qur'an says God devised a plot, the hidden quality is what transfers: the conspirators could not see what was being arranged. Nothing false was told to them, and no promise was broken. The Book elsewhere calls God the best of planners and states that all planning belongs to Him. Read together, these passages describe sovereignty over outcomes rather than a divine talent for lying. The rhetorical repetition here is what forces the reading — the word is chosen because they chose it.

**The Response Is Simultaneous, Not Subsequent**

Both verbs are in the past tense and the sentence presents them as concurrent. God's plan is not described as a reaction arriving after theirs matured. It was running while theirs was being sworn.

That simultaneity is what makes the closing clause devastating. The nine had spent their meeting arranging a night attack and rehearsing an alibi, confident that the only parties to the matter were themselves, Ṣāliḥ, and an heir they intended to deceive. The verse says a third party was present throughout. Their secrecy was real with respect to the city and worthless with respect to what actually determined the outcome. The hoopoe's speech in verse 25 had already named the attribute at work — God knows what is concealed and what is disclosed — and here the abstract claim is cashed out in a specific conspiracy.

**What Their Plot Cost Them**

The Qur'an does not describe the mechanism in this chapter. The next verse states the result, and the other accounts of Thamūd supply the destruction that fell on them. What this verse establishes is the shape of the reversal rather than its physics.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the commentators offer various versions of how the plot was foiled and how the nine met their end, and that none of them is definitive. That admission from within the tradition is the resolution, and it is worth repeating because the gap invites filling. The variants — a collapsing rock, an angel's blow, and others — are mutually exclusive, which settles the matter without needing to assess any of them individually: they cannot all be reports of what happened, and nothing distinguishes one as the report. The Qur'an, for its part, does not assign the nine a separate death at all. The chapter's next verse destroys them with their people. What the text gives is sufficient for its purpose: they planned, something else was planned, and they did not know. The absence of detail keeps the attention on the structure rather than on the spectacle.

**Secrecy Is the Assumption Being Attacked**

Strip the episode to its logic and the conspirators' error is a single premise. They believed that an act performed in darkness, agreed under oath, and covered by coordinated testimony would be, in the relevant sense, unobserved.

That premise is the practical form of disbelief in the Hereafter, which the chapter's fourth verse identified as the root of everything else. A man certain of a final accounting cannot plan an unwitnessed crime, because the category does not exist for him; a man who is not can plan one, and will, whenever the balance of advantage recommends it. The nine are the demonstration the chapter set up eighty verses earlier. Their oath by God, their alibi, and their emphatic claim to truthfulness are all the behaviour of people managing an audience they believe is the only audience. The result of the plan, and what remains of their city, is stated next.

**The Qur'an's Broader Language for This**

The idea in this verse recurs across the Book in related vocabulary. God is called the best of planners in two places, and elsewhere it is stated that all planning belongs to Him entirely. A parallel set of passages uses the language of *kayd*, scheming, and asserts that the divine scheme outmatches the human one.

Collected together, these verses form a consistent position rather than a scattering of images. Human beings genuinely plan, and their plans genuinely operate; the Qur'an never suggests the conspirators here were doing nothing. What it denies is that any such plan is the final determinant of what happens. The formula in this verse is the compressed version, and the structure of the destroyed-nation narratives is the extended one: in each, a scheme is laid, proceeds, and arrives somewhere other than its intended destination. Thamūd's nine are the clearest case in the chapter, because their plan is reported in full detail exactly one verse before its collapse is announced.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:51

> See then what the consequences of their plan were: We ˹utterly˺ destroyed them and their people all together.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Look, Used a Second Time**

The verse opens with the same imperative the chapter applied to Pharaoh's court in verse 14: *fa-anẓur kayfa kāna* — see then how it was. Thirty-seven verses apart, two nations are handed to the reader with the same instruction.

The formula recurs throughout the Qur'an at the close of destruction narratives, and its function is to convert a story into evidence. What the reader is told to examine is not the wickedness but the *ʿāqibah*, the end, the upshot. A story judged by its ending is being treated as a completed experiment, and experiments can be compared. The repetition within a single chapter turns two separate accounts into a series, and a series with two matching results is the beginning of a pattern. That is the argument an-Naml has been assembling since its opening verses, and here is where it starts to close.

**The Plot Is What Fared Badly**

The grammar is exact about the subject. It is not *see how they fared*; it is *see how the consequence of their plot was*. The plan itself is the thing whose outcome the reader is invited to inspect.

That precision matters because the previous verse established the frame. They plotted, and a plot was made about them, and they did not perceive it. The question now on the table is whose plan produced its intended result. Theirs was designed to remove Ṣāliḥ, protect their standing, and satisfy an heir with a coordinated lie. What it produced was the annihilation of the people it was meant to serve. Nothing in the verse suggests the conspirators were prevented from acting or that their scheme was clumsy. It was a competent plan by powerful men, and its consequence was the opposite of its purpose, which is a stronger claim than saying it failed.

**"Them and Their People All Together"**

The destruction extends past the nine. *Dammarnāhum wa qawmahum ajmaʿīn* — We destroyed them and their people, all of them. A conspiracy of nine ends a nation.

This is the hardest sentence in the passage and it should not be softened. The Qur'an has just taken care to identify the instigators by number and by conduct, distinguishing them from the city at large, and then reports a destruction that covers everyone. The believers are excepted two verses later, which is the text's own qualification and the place where the moral accounting is done. What remains is the claim that a community which allows nine men to run it into this is not a bystander to what they do. The chapter had shown the alternative in Sheba, where a court was consulted, spoke honestly, and was heard. Thamūd's population is not recorded objecting to anything.

**Destroyed Utterly**

The verb is *dammara*, and it is stronger than the general words for punishment or seizure. The root denotes ruin that leaves nothing standing, and the Qur'an reserves it for the most complete destructions it describes.

Its force here is confirmed by the next verse, which points at empty houses still standing in the landscape. The people are gone and the architecture remains, which is the specific form this ruin took. Nothing is said in this chapter about the mechanism — other accounts of Thamūd describe a cry, a quaking, a thunderbolt — and an-Naml withholds all of it. The omission keeps the emphasis on the reversal rather than the spectacle. What the reader is asked to look at is a result, not a disaster.

**A Warning Placed Between Two Rescues**

Sequence is doing work here. Verse 44 ended with a queen declaring faith. Verse 53 will record the deliverance of those who believed. This verse sits between them, and it is the only one of the three that ends badly.

The arrangement prevents either extreme reading. A chapter of rescues would make the warnings decorative; a chapter of destructions would make the invitations empty. An-Naml alternates, and the alternation is the argument: the same message reached Sheba and Thamūd, and the outcomes diverged entirely according to what was done with it. Nothing in the two situations made the results inevitable. A sun-worshipping queen with every political reason to resist submitted; a nation warned by its own brother, with a believing faction already among them, ended as empty stone. The next verse tells the reader where those stones are.

**What the Nine Achieved**

Trace the conspirators' intentions against their result. They wanted Ṣāliḥ silenced, their standing in the city preserved, the she-camel affair closed, and an heir satisfied with a coordinated lie. Every one of those objectives concerned the continuation of normal life in their city.

What the plan produced was the end of the city. Not a setback, not an exposure, not a reversal of fortune for the nine alone — the removal of the entire community whose arrangements they were protecting. That is the specific irony the imperative points at. The plot was rational on its own terms, and its terms were wrong, because they had left out the only factor that would decide the outcome. The chapter had already named that omission in verse 25, where the hoopoe described God as knowing what is concealed and what is disclosed, and again in verse 50, where the plot they could not see was running alongside the one they made. This verse is where the two threads meet and the account is settled.

**Nothing Is Said About How**

An-Naml gives no mechanism. Other chapters describe a cry, a shaking of the earth, a thunderbolt falling on Thamūd; this one reports only that the destruction happened and directs the reader to inspect the consequence.

The silence is consistent with the chapter's method throughout. It withheld the contents of Sheba's gift, the name of her queen, the identity of the man with knowledge of the Book, and the mechanism by which a throne crossed a continent. In each case what is kept is the part that carries the argument and what is dropped is the part that would satisfy curiosity. A reader who wants to know what Thamūd heard in their last hour is asking a question the chapter has decided is not the point. What it wants examined is the gap between a plan and its consequence, and the mechanism of the destruction adds nothing to that.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:52

> So their homes are there, ˹but completely˺ ruined because of their wrongdoing. Surely in this is a lesson for people of knowledge.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Evidence Is Still Standing**

The demonstrative is present tense and pointing: *fa-tilka buyūtuhum* — those are their houses. Not *those were*. The Qur'an directs its first audience toward structures they could go and look at.

Thamūd's dwellings survive in northwestern Arabia, monumental façades cut directly into sandstone outcrops, and the site lies on the caravan route between Mecca and Syria that the Quraysh used for trade. Merchants passing north saw them. That geography converts the preceding verse's claim from a story into a checkable assertion, which is exactly what the imperative in verse 51 demanded — look at the outcome. The chapter is not asking for trust in a report about ancient peoples. It is pointing out of the window at a ruin whose emptiness is the whole argument.

**Houses That Outlasted Their Builders**

There is a specific irony in what survived. Thamūd are remembered in the Qur'an for carving their homes out of rock, and elsewhere the Book records them doing it with confidence, as people securing themselves. The stone held. The people did not.

This is the point the ruins make without any commentary. A civilisation that invests in permanence is making a bet about what lasts, and the bet was answered in the most literal way available: the architecture is intact and the nation is gone. The Qur'an makes the same observation about other peoples who built to endure. What distinguishes Thamūd's case is that the evidence is unusually durable, since rock-cut tombs do not collapse the way mud-brick cities do. Two and a half thousand years after the fact, the houses are still there and still empty, which is the condition the verse describes.

**"Because of Their Wrongdoing"**

The verse assigns a cause: *bimā ẓalamū*, on account of the wrong they did. Al-Ṭabarī identifies the wrong as their idolatry and their denial of their prophet.

Naming the cause blocks the natural alternative explanation. Ruins invite the reading that a civilisation failed for the reasons civilisations usually fail — drought, invasion, trade routes shifting, a dynasty exhausting itself. The verse forecloses that reading for this case and states a moral cause instead. Whether a modern reader accepts the claim is a separate question from what the text is doing, which is refusing to let the evidence be absorbed into a purely material account. The word chosen is *ẓulm*, putting something where it does not belong, which covers both halves of al-Ṭabarī's gloss precisely: worship given to the wrong recipient, and a messenger given the wrong reception.

**The Lesson Is Restricted to Those Who Know**

The verse ends by limiting its audience: *inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li-qawmin yaʿlamūn* — in this is a sign for a people who know. Not for everyone who passes, and not for everyone who looks.

The restriction recalls the chapter's second verse, where the Book was called guidance and good news specifically for the believers. The same logic applies here. A ruin is publicly available and the sign in it is not, because reading a sign requires bringing something to it. Caravans passed these façades for centuries and most travellers saw architecture. Extracting a lesson from an empty city requires already believing that outcomes carry meaning, which is the disposition the chapter has been arguing for since it named the Book's verses *āyāt*, signs. The word used here is the same one. Thamūd's houses and the Qur'an's verses are placed in one category.

**An Inward Reading of the Ruin**

Alongside the plain sense, a strand of commentary read the desolate houses as an image of the people rather than of their property. The interpretation takes the hardened, emptied dwelling as a figure for a heart made rigid by disobedience — the structure intact, the life gone.

This is allegory, and it should be labelled as such rather than presented as the verse's meaning. The sentence is about buildings, and its evidential force depends on the buildings being real and locatable. But the extension is not arbitrary, because it works from something the plain sense already contains: the mismatch between a durable exterior and an absent interior. A person can maintain every outward form of a functioning life while the thing the forms existed for has departed, and there is no visible difference from outside. Whether the commentators were right to find that in this verse, the observation stands on its own, and it belongs to a chapter that has spent fifty verses on people who looked at surfaces and drew the wrong conclusion. Those who were not destroyed are accounted for next.

**A Sign Placed in the Landscape**

The word for what the ruins constitute is *āyah*, the same word the chapter's first verse used for the verses of the Qur'an. Two very different things are put in one category: sentences that are recited and stones that are passed on a trade road.

That shared term is a claim about how evidence works. A verse and a ruin both point beyond themselves to something that placed them there, and both require a reader willing to follow the pointing. Neither compels. The Qur'an uses *āyah* in a third sense as well, for the natural world — rain, the alternation of night and day, the growth of a seed — so the category covers scripture, history and creation together. Thamūd's empty houses belong to the middle term, history preserved in stone. They are what happened, preserved, available for inspection, and carrying a meaning that the chapter has just spelled out for anyone who would otherwise miss it. Nothing in the description asks the reader to take the outcome on trust; the evidence is placed where anyone travelling that road could inspect it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:53

> And We delivered those who were faithful and were mindful ˹of Allah˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Completes the Accounting**

Verse 51 said the destruction covered them and their people all together. This verse states the exception, and without it the previous claim would describe an indiscriminate catastrophe. The believers were taken out.

The placement is deliberate. The Qur'an states the destruction first, in the strongest available terms, and supplies the qualification two verses later — long enough for the weight of the ruin to land, close enough that the accounting is completed within the same passage. Every destruction narrative in the Book follows this structure: a people ends, and those who believed among them do not. The pattern is what distinguishes these accounts from stories of divine temper. A punishment that fell on everyone present would be a disaster; one that sorts is a judgment.

**Two Conditions, Not One**

The deliverance is granted to those who *believed* and who *were mindful* — *alladhīna āmanū wa kānū yattaqūn*. Two verbs, and the second is not a restatement of the first.

*Īmān* is the inward assent: accepting that what Ṣāliḥ brought was true. *Taqwā* is the resulting carefulness, the conduct of someone who lives as though the assent were real. Arabic builds the word from a root meaning to guard oneself, and the Qur'an uses it for a practical vigilance rather than a feeling. Pairing them here does the same work the chapter's third verse did, where believers were identified by prayer, alms and certainty. A claim about the inside is given an outside. In a city run by nine notables whose business was corrupt lending and public humiliation, *taqwā* would have had an obvious daily content, and the people who practised it would have been visible.

**The Verb of Rescue**

*Najjaynā* comes from a root meaning to pull something clear, to bring it out of a place where it would have been lost. The same verb is used of Nūḥ on the ark, of Lūṭ leaving his city, and of Mūsā's people crossing the sea.

What the word describes is extraction rather than protection. The believers were not made immune where they stood; they were removed from where the destruction fell. That distinction matters for how the Qur'an presents divine rescue throughout. It generally involves motion, timing and a warning acted upon — Lūṭ's household leaves at night, Nūḥ builds for years before the water. Deliverance in these accounts is something people participate in, and the participation is the evidence that the belief was real. The verse's two conditions and its verb of extraction fit together: those who believed and were careful were the ones in a position to be pulled clear.

**A Believing Minority Existed Throughout**

This verse confirms retrospectively what verse 45 reported: the nation split into two parties, and one of them accepted Ṣāliḥ. That party existed through everything that followed — the demand for punishment, the accusation of ill omen, the conspiracy of the nine.

Their situation is worth registering. They lived in a city whose leading men were organising a murder, held a position that made them a named source of misfortune, and had no power to alter any of it. The Qur'an does not record them arguing, resisting or being heroic. What it records is that they believed and were careful, and that this was sufficient. There is a realism in that which the destruction narratives are often read past. Most people inside a failing society cannot change its direction; what remains available to them is their own position, and the verse says that their own position was enough.

**Where They Went**

The Qur'an says nothing about what became of the survivors, and the commentators filled the silence in different ways. Al-Ṭabarī transmits that those saved with Ṣāliḥ settled in Palestine. Al-Qurṭubī records an alternative, that some four thousand went to Ḥaḍramawt in southern Arabia, where Ṣāliḥ died, and that the place is named from that event since the word can be read as *death has come*.

That etymology can be settled, and it is false. The name is far older than the event it is supposed to commemorate. It appears as *ḥḍrmt* in Old South Arabian inscriptions from the middle of the first millennium BC, where it names a kingdom with its capital at Shabwa on the incense route; Theophrastus transcribes it into Greek as Ἁδρραμύτα in the late fourth or early third century BC, and Strabo later calls its people the Chatramotitai. The Hebrew Bible already lists it as Hazarmaveth among the sons of Joktan. A place attested under that name across three languages, centuries before the Arabic reading was proposed, did not acquire it from a sentence meaning *death has come*. The derivation is a folk etymology of the familiar kind — an existing name resolved into two words of the later language that sound right. The rest of the material is likewise a filling of silence: the destinations, and the figure of four thousand, come from the commentators, and the Qur'an gives no address at all. The Book's reticence is consistent with how it has handled every detail in this chapter that does not bear on the argument — Sheba's queen has no name, her gift no contents, and now her counterpart's survivors no address. What is recorded is that they were brought out. The chapter turns to its fourth and last prophetic narrative in the following verse.

**The Rescue Is Stated in a Single Line**

Seven verses went to the plot and its consequence. One goes to the deliverance, and it names no individuals, no numbers and no circumstances. The Qur'an spends its words on the warning and summarises the outcome it actually wants.

That imbalance is characteristic of the destroyed-nation narratives, and it follows from who they were told to. The stories are told to people who have not yet decided, and what such an audience needs at length is the sequence of refusals that led to the end — the demand for punishment, the accusation of ill omen, the nine men and their oath. The rescue needs only to be certain, not detailed. There is also a structural effect. A single short verse of deliverance placed after a long account of ruin leaves the reader with the ruin's weight and the rescue's fact, which is the proportion the chapter has maintained since it described the Book as guidance and good news for those who take it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:54

> And ˹remember˺ Lot, when he rebuked ˹the men of˺ his people, “Do you commit that shameful deed while you can see ˹one another˺?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth and Shortest Narrative**

Lūṭ's story occupies five verses. Mūsā received eight, Sulaymān thirty, Ṣāliḥ nine. The chapter is closing its historical section, and the compression is a sign that the pattern is by now established rather than being built.

What an-Naml keeps is minimal: a question, a second question, the people's reply, the rescue, the destruction. No negotiation, no angels at the door, no bargaining over the city — all of which appear in Hūd and al-Ḥijr. The selection tells us what this chapter wants from the episode. It wants the exchange in which a prophet names a practice and its practitioners answer by expelling the people who object. That exchange is the one that matches what has come before, and everything else is left to the other accounts.

**The Word for the Act**

Lūṭ names the deed *al-fāḥishah*, with the definite article — the indecency, the one everybody present knows he means. The root denotes something excessive and grossly improper, and the Qur'an applies it to a small set of sexual offences.

The commentators identify what is meant here consistently: anal intercourse, chiefly between men, which the next verse makes explicit by contrasting men with women. Al-Ṭabarī notes that some extended the term to the same act with women. The article is doing the work of a euphemism, and the euphemism is itself informative. Lūṭ does not describe the act; he points at it with a word for what it is, and his audience understands immediately. A community that has a definite article for a practice has a practice that is established, public and unembarrassed.

**"While You Can See"**

The question's second half is the strange part: *wa antum tubṣirūn* — while you see. Commentators divided over what the seeing refers to, and the readings are not exclusive.

Al-Rāzī takes it to mean they knew their deeds were indecent — seeing in the sense of understanding, with the eyes of the mind open. On that reading the charge is the same one levelled at Pharaoh's court in verse 14, where certainty and denial coexisted. A second reading takes the seeing literally: they did it in each other's sight, openly, without concealment.
