Al-Rāzī elsewhere presses this point too, observing that part of their offence was the openness, the refusal to keep the matter from view. Both readings identify the same aggravating feature. A wrong done in private admits that it is wrong; the concealment is a kind of testimony. What Lūṭ confronts is a community that has stopped producing that testimony.

**Openness as a Separate Charge**

The distinction between an act and its public performance deserves stating, because the verse turns on it. Lūṭ's question is not *do you commit this*; it is *do you commit this while seeing*. The clause is what the interrogative hangs on.

A practice performed in the open has been normalised, and normalisation changes what it does to a society. It no longer merely involves those who perform it; it becomes the standard against which others are measured, which is precisely what the next verses show — the objectors are the ones marked out for expulsion. The Qur'an elsewhere treats public sin as a distinct category, and reports from the Prophet ﷺ speak of the whole community being pardoned except those who publicise what they have done. The principle is not that secrecy makes a wrong acceptable. It is that a wrong kept private leaves the standard intact, and a wrong displayed replaces it.

**A Rebuke Framed as a Question**

Lūṭ does not open with a prohibition or a threat. He asks. Both this verse and the next put his objection in interrogative form, and the form is a choice — Arabic had every resource for a flat command.

A question addressed to people about their own conduct invites them to supply the answer, which means it assumes they have the materials to see the problem. That assumption is the substance of Lūṭ's approach, and it fits his position: the Qur'an elsewhere calls him their brother in the same sense as Ṣāliḥ, one of the community speaking to his own. Rhetorical questions of this kind run through the Qur'an's prophetic speech from Ibrāhīm asking his father why he worships what cannot hear to Mūsā before Pharaoh, and their function is consistent. They locate the argument inside the listener rather than between the speaker and the listener. His second question, in the next verse, sharpens the first by naming what the practice displaces.

**"And Lot" — the Formula That Opens Each Account**

The verse begins *wa Lūṭan idh qāla li-qawmih* — and Lot, when he said to his people. The construction matches the chapter's other narrative openings: *wa laqad arsalnā ilā Thamūd*, and *idh qāla Mūsā li-ahlih*. Each prophet is introduced by a name and a moment of speech.

The pattern tells the reader what kind of history this is. These are not biographies; they are records of what was said and how it was answered. Mūsā is introduced speaking to his family about a fire, Sulaymān addressing his people about a gift, Ṣāliḥ proclaiming two words, Lūṭ asking a question. In every case the narrative begins at the point where a message enters a community, and it ends at the point where the community's answer has produced its result. Everything in between is the exchange. An-Naml is a chapter about how claims are received, and its narrative formula is built to isolate exactly that, stripping away everything that would turn an exchange into an adventure. The question is put to them directly rather than reported about them, which keeps the exchange in the present tense of a confrontation.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:55

> Do you really lust after men instead of women? In fact, you are ˹only˺ a people acting ignorantly.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection Is Stated Plainly**

Where the previous verse used a definite article as a euphemism, this one names the matter directly: approaching men with desire *instead of* women. Lūṭ has moved from pointing at the practice to describing it.

The phrase that carries the argument is *min dūni al-nisāʾ* — to the exclusion of women, in place of women. The same construction appeared in verse 24, where Sheba's people prostrated to the sun *min dūni Allāh*, instead of God. The Qur'an uses this formula for substitution: something has been put in the position belonging to something else. That is a consistent way of framing what the Book objects to, and it recurs across quite different subjects. The complaint is not that a desire exists but that an arrangement has been displaced.

**Al-Rāzī's Argument From Design**

Al-Rāzī states the reasoning the tradition generally rests on: the practice runs against the wisdom of God, who did not create men sexually for men. The argument is teleological — it reads a purpose off the structure of the thing.

Whether that reasoning persuades a given reader depends on premises the Qur'an assumes and does not argue for in this passage, chiefly that creation is purposive and that its purposes are legible. Within those premises the argument is straightforward and it is the one the classical commentators make. It is worth being clear about what kind of claim it is. Lūṭ's objection as the Qur'an frames it is not primarily about harm to others or about social order; it is about a design being set aside. That is also why the word chosen for the people's condition is *ignorance* rather than cruelty or injustice, which are the charges laid against Thamūd's nine.

**"A People Acting Ignorantly"**

The verdict is *qawmun tajhalūn* — a people who are ignorant, or who behave ignorantly. The root *j-h-l* is important in Qur'anic vocabulary and it does not mean a simple lack of information.

*Jahl* in Arabic usage is the opposite of *ḥilm*, forbearance and self-possession, as much as it is the opposite of knowledge. The pre-Islamic period is called the *Jāhiliyyah* not because the Arabs of that era lacked learning — they had poetry, genealogy and astronomy — but because their conduct was governed by impulse, pride and unchecked appetite. Applied here, the word names a way of acting rather than a gap in what is known, a failure of self-government rather than of education, which is consistent with the previous verse's charge that they did this *while seeing*. They are not uninformed. They are operating without the restraint that information should produce.

**Two Charges That Fit Together**

Put the two verses side by side and the accusation has a shape. Verse 54 said they did it openly, in each other's sight, knowing. This verse says they are ignorant. Those look contradictory and are not.

Knowing something and being governed by it are different capacities, and the Qur'an keeps them apart throughout this chapter. Pharaoh's court was certain the signs were true and denied them anyway. Thamūd's nine swore by God while planning murder. Lūṭ's people know the practice is indecent — that is what *while you see* asserts — and the knowledge does not reach their conduct. *Jahl* names precisely that disconnection. It is the condition of a person whose information and whose behaviour have come apart, which is a more accurate description of most human failure than simple error is.

**A Question Asked Rather Than a Sentence Passed**

Lūṭ's second question ends the part of the exchange that belongs to him. He has asked twice and characterised once, and there is no threat in either verse, no announcement of punishment, and no demand backed by a consequence.

The restraint is worth registering because of what happens next. His people's answer is not an argument but a motion to expel him and those with him from the city — the response of a community that regards an objection as an unbearable presence. The disproportion between his two questions and the reply they draw is the narrative's whole point, and it is a pattern the chapter has established. Ṣāliḥ asked why they were hurrying and was told he was bad luck; nine men then planned to kill his household. Sheba's queen received a demand and answered with a test. The chapter has been measuring communities by what they do with an objection, and the people of Lūṭ answer in the next verse.

**Where This Sits Among the Qur'an's Accounts**

Lūṭ's people appear in several chapters and the emphases differ. Hūd gives the scene at his door and his desperate attempt to protect his guests. Al-Ḥijr supplies the angels and the timing of the departure. Ash-Shuʿarāʾ places his story in a sequence of five prophets with a repeating refrain.

An-Naml keeps only the exchange, and reading the versions together shows how deliberate that selection is. This chapter has no angels, no guests, no negotiation and no dawn deadline. What it takes is the two questions and the answer they received, because the chapter's subject is the reception of a message rather than the machinery of a rescue. The same editorial logic governed its treatment of Mūsā, where years of confrontation in Egypt were compressed into one clause about signs arriving and being called sorcery. Each narrative in an-Naml is cut to the moment where a community decides, and the material that does not bear on the deciding is left to the chapters that want it. The result is four episodes that can be read as one argument. What the phrasing isolates is the deliberateness of the act, and deliberateness is what the rest of the passage goes on to judge.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:56

> But his people’s only response was to say, “Expel Lot’s followers from your land! They are a people who wish to remain chaste!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Answer They Had**

The verse marks the reply as the whole of what they produced: *fa-mā kāna jawāba qawmihi illā an qālū* — the answer of his people was nothing but that they said. The construction is restrictive. Out of everything available to them, this is what came.

Lūṭ had asked two questions. A community with an argument would have made one; a community that disagreed could have said so. What they generate instead is a motion of expulsion, which is not a reply to either question. The Qur'an uses this same restrictive formula for other peoples confronted by their prophets, and it functions each time as a verdict on the quality of the response. Nothing was answered. The objection was treated as a presence to be removed rather than a claim to be met, and the formula records that the removal was the entirety of their contribution.

**Purity Turned Into an Accusation**

The charge is astonishing when read closely. They call for the expulsion of Lūṭ's household on the grounds that *innahum unāsun yataṭahharūn* — they are people who keep themselves pure.

The verb is *taṭahhara*, and the Qur'an uses it elsewhere as unambiguous praise. God is said to love those who purify themselves, and a mosque is commended because men in it love to be purified. Here the same word is used as an indictment. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both gloss the sense as a sneer: they are trying to keep themselves clean of what we do. The people of Lūṭ have not disputed that his household is pure; they have agreed, and made the purity the offence. That inversion is the most complete moral reversal recorded in this chapter, and it is delivered in a single word. The Qur'an reports the same sneer from these people in another chapter, in almost identical wording, which indicates it was their settled formulation rather than a remark made once in anger.

**When Restraint Becomes an Insult**

The mechanism behind the insult is worth setting out, because it is not peculiar to this city. A community that has normalised a practice finds abstention intolerable in a way that mere disagreement is not.

Disagreement can be argued with and dismissed. Abstention is a standing demonstration that the practice is optional, performed daily by people living next door, and it therefore functions as a judgment regardless of whether the abstainers say anything. Lūṭ's household is not accused of preaching, obstructing or organising. They are accused of being clean. That is enough to require their removal, because their existence is the argument. The chapter's earlier verses described people whose deeds were made to look good to them; this is what such a condition does when it meets a counter-example — it cannot leave the counter-example in place.

**"From Your Land"**

The expulsion is framed possessively: *min qaryatikum*, from your town. The speakers address one another, and the town belongs to them.

The pronoun does the political work. Lūṭ is a prophet sent to his own people and has lived among them, and the phrasing writes him out of the community he belongs to. Once his household is *them* and the town is *yours*, the removal becomes tidying rather than exile. This is the same operation Thamūd performed when they told Ṣāliḥ he and his followers were an ill omen, converting an internal disagreement into an external contamination. The Qur'an records the move twice in ten verses, and the repetition marks it as a recognised pattern: communities that will not answer an objection reclassify the objector as a foreign body.

**The Sentence They Passed on Themselves**

There is a grim accuracy in their proposal. They ask for the righteous to be removed from the city, and within two verses exactly that happens — Lūṭ and his household are taken out, and what follows falls on those who remain.

The Qur'an does not point this out, and the restraint is more effective than a comment would be. Their demand is granted in form and reversed in meaning. The same request that was meant to purge the town of an irritant becomes the description of its rescue operation, with the difference that the people leaving are the ones who survive. This is the second time in the chapter that a plan has arrived at the opposite of its intention; Thamūd's nine swore an oath that ended their nation. Both cases make the same structural point without stating it. The rescue is reported in the next verse, along with the one exception to it.

**A Response With No Content**

Strip the reply to its components and nothing remains but a motion and a justification. The motion is expulsion. The justification is that the people to be expelled are clean. There is no denial, no counter-claim about the practice, no defence of it, and no attack on Lūṭ's standing to speak.

That emptiness is what the restrictive opening formula was marking. The Qur'an could have reported an argument and refuted it; what it reports is that no argument was offered. This matters for the chapter's larger case, which has been measuring communities by the quality of their response to evidence. Sheba's queen received a demand and built a test around it. Sulaymān received a report and verified it. Pharaoh's court produced at least a classification, however dishonest — they called the signs sorcery, which is a claim capable of being wrong. Lūṭ's people produce nothing that can be assessed at all, which places them at the bottom of the chapter's scale. The reply is recorded in their own words, so the assessment that follows rests on what they said rather than on a characterisation of it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:57

> So We delivered him and his family, except his wife. We had destined her to be one of the doomed.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception Inside the Rescue**

The deliverance is granted and immediately qualified. Him and his household — except his wife. The Qur'an states it without elaboration and moves on, and the flatness of the report is part of its force.

This is the counterpart to what happened at Thamūd, where the destruction was total and the believers were excepted from it. Here the rescue is the general case and one person is excepted from that. The two constructions together describe a single principle operating in both directions: the sorting is by the individual, and neither proximity to the condemned nor proximity to the saved transfers. A believer inside a doomed city came out; the wife of a prophet, inside the rescued household, did not.

**Marriage to a Prophet Saved Nobody**

The verse is the Qur'an's clearest statement that religious standing is not inherited or acquired by association. The woman lived in the house of a messenger of God, shared his table, and was part of the household the rescue covered.

The Book presses this point elsewhere with the same example. Sūrah at-Taḥrīm sets the wives of Nūḥ and Lūṭ side by side as a parable for those who disbelieve — both married to righteous servants, both of no avail to their husbands — and then places against them the wife of Pharaoh, who believed inside the house of the chapter's worst tyrant. The pairing is deliberate and it cuts both ways. Neither the best nor the worst household determines what the person inside it becomes. Lūṭ's wife appears in five other chapters of the Qur'an, and in every one of them the same exception is recorded. That consistency across passages of very different length and emphasis indicates how central the point was held to be.

**"Among Those Who Lagged Behind"**

The word describing her fate is *al-ghābirīn*, from a root meaning to remain behind after others have gone. It is not the ordinary word for the destroyed or the punished.

The choice is precise and it identifies the form her failure took. She stayed. When the household was brought out, she did not come, or did not come fully, and other accounts of the story add detail about a backward glance and a delay. What the term names is a relation to the departure rather than a crime committed in the city. The Qur'an's other accounts of her never specify what she believed; what they specify, consistently, is that she was of those who lagged. Her offence, in the way the Book chooses to record it, was an attachment to the place that was being left.

**"We Destined Her"**

The verb is *qaddarnāhā*, we decreed or measured it for her, and the phrasing raises the old question about foreknowledge and choice. If her outcome was decreed, in what sense was it hers?

The Qur'an's consistent practice is to state both the decree and the responsibility without reconciling them philosophically, and this verse follows it. Verse 4 of this chapter attributed the adornment of evil deeds to God, and the whole surrounding argument held the deniers accountable; the same structure appears here in miniature. What the classical positions generally maintain is that divine decree includes the choice rather than bypassing it — what was measured for her was the outcome of what she would do. Whether that satisfies as an answer is a question the Qur'an does not take up here. What the verse asserts is that her fate was known and fixed, and that she was one of a household given every advantage and did not use it.

**Nothing Is Said About Why**

This chapter gives no account of her conduct. No statement of hers is quoted, no act is described, and no motive is supplied. She appears in a subordinate clause and disappears.

The reticence matches everything else an-Naml has withheld — the name of Sheba's queen, the contents of her gift, the identity of the man with knowledge of the Book. What the chapter preserves in each case is the part that bears on its argument, and here the argument needs only the exception itself. A reader who wants to know what she did is reaching for a story the Qur'an is not telling. The story it is telling is about who comes out of a city and who does not, and the sorting principle is the point rather than the biography. What fell on those who remained is described next.

**The Household Is Rescued as a Household**

The rescue covers *him and his family*, a unit rather than a list of qualifying individuals. That framing is worth holding alongside the exception, because the two together describe how the Qur'an handles collective and individual fate.

Ordinarily the household travels as one. Nūḥ's family boards the ark, Ibrāhīm's leaves with him, Lūṭ's is brought out. The Book does not treat faith as a purely private matter with no social shape; a believing household is a real thing and its members are extracted together. Sharing a roof with people who believe exposes a person to the argument daily and makes the choice easier than it would otherwise be, and the Qur'an counts that as a genuine advantage. What the exception establishes is that the unit is not a container that protects whatever is inside it. Membership is the normal channel of rescue and it is not a guarantee, and the difference between those two statements is the whole content of this verse. Nūḥ's son, refused a place on the ark in another chapter, makes the same point from the other side.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:58

> And We poured upon them a rain ˹of brimstone˺. How evil was the rain of those who had been warned! Questions to the Pagans:1) Who Is the Creator?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rain Used as a Punishment**

The Qur'an's ordinary vocabulary for rain is one of mercy. Water comes down and dead ground revives; gardens grow; the sign of God's provision is the shower that arrives when it is needed. This verse takes the same category and inverts it.

The verb is *amṭarnā*, we rained down, and what fell is not specified in this chapter. Other passages describe stones of baked clay falling on the cities of Lūṭ's people, and the translation's bracketed gloss follows that reading. What an-Naml keeps is the bare structure: something came down from the sky on a city, in the way water comes down on a field, and it destroyed rather than revived. The inversion is the point being made, and it is made again in the second half of the verse by the exclamation that follows.

**"How Evil Was the Rain"**

The second clause is an exclamation of the kind Arabic reserves for emphasis: *fa-sā'a maṭaru al-mundharīn* — evil was the rain of those who had been warned. The construction is compressed and the genitive is unusual.

The rain is called *theirs* — the rain of the warned. It belongs to them in the way a sentence belongs to a convict, produced by their situation rather than arriving from outside it. And the group is identified not as the sinners or the deniers but as *al-mundharīn*, those who had received warning. That is the description the verse chooses at the moment of destruction, and it is the one that makes the destruction intelligible. What fell on them fell on people who had been told.

**Warned, Not Ambushed**

The chapter has been building this point across all four of its narratives and states it here in one word. Pharaoh's court received nine signs. Thamūd received a prophet who was their own brother and a specific question about why they were in a hurry. Sheba received a letter. Lūṭ's people received two questions and a household living visibly differently in their midst.

In none of the cases does destruction arrive without notice. The Qur'an is consistent about this to the point of making it a rule, stating elsewhere that no community is destroyed until a messenger has been sent and the case put. That principle is what the word *mundharīn* invokes at this moment. The rain is described as belonging to the warned because the warning is what makes it a judgment rather than a catastrophe. Remove the notice and the event becomes weather. The Qur'an's insistence on the warning is therefore not a detail of the narrative but the condition that makes the whole category of divine punishment coherent, and it recurs in every one of these accounts without exception.

**The Cities Were Real and Locatable**

As with Thamūd's carved houses, the Qur'an treats these ruins as available for inspection. Elsewhere it says of the destroyed cities of Lūṭ's people that they lie on a road still travelled, and that they are on a clear highway — addressed to an audience whose caravans went north through the region east of the Dead Sea.

The geographical reference performs the same function it did nine verses earlier. It converts a story into a claim that can be checked against something in the landscape, which is what the chapter's repeated imperative to look at outcomes requires. The Qur'an's destroyed nations are consistently placed in known locations on known routes rather than in an unspecified antiquity, and the Quraysh merchants hearing this chapter had travelled both roads — north past the cities of Lūṭ's people and past the rock-cut façades of Thamūd. Two ruins, two warnings, one trade route. A merchant who made the northern journey and the southern one had passed both pieces of evidence in a single year of business.

**Where the Narrative Section Ends**

This verse closes the chapter's historical half. Fifty-one verses of narrative, four peoples, two rescues and three destructions, and the last word is an exclamation about warning.

What follows is entirely different in form. The chapter turns from stories to a sequence of questions addressed to the living — who created the heavens and the earth, who answers the distressed, who guides in the darkness — each ending with the same refrain. The transition is not arbitrary. Everything the narratives demonstrated about how communities receive evidence is about to be applied directly to the audience rather than to ancient peoples, and the switch from third-person history to direct interrogation is how the application is made. The first of those questions begins in the next verse with an instruction to the Prophet about what to say.

**The Heading in the Text**

The translation line carries an editorial heading that belongs to the printed edition rather than to the verse: a label announcing the section of questions addressed to the pagans that begins immediately afterwards. It is reproduced here because the translation is quoted exactly as it stands.

The label is not part of the revelation, but it does identify something real about the chapter's structure. An editor reading straight through felt the need to mark a break at precisely this point, which confirms what the passage itself indicates: verse 58 closes the narrative half and verse 59 opens something formally different. Modern printed Qur'ans frequently carry such headings, added by translators and publishers to help readers navigate; they vary between editions and carry no authority. Readers of this commentary should treat everything after the exclamation about rain as an editorial insertion. The sentence is short and the brevity is functional, since nothing further needs to be established once the outcome has been named.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:59

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Praise be to Allah, and peace be upon the servants He has chosen.” ˹Ask the disbelievers,˺ “Which is better: Allah or whatever ˹gods˺ they associate ˹with Him˺?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Changes Direction**

Fifty-eight verses of narrative end, and this one opens with an instruction: *qul*, say. The Prophet is told what to recite, and what follows is a series of questions aimed at the living rather than reports about the dead.

The hinge is well placed. Four peoples have just been shown receiving evidence and deciding, three of them badly. The chapter now stops describing decisions and demands one. Everything in the remaining verses is either a question or an answer to a question, and the questions are addressed to people in Mecca who worship beside God what cannot hear them. The narratives were the argument's evidence; this section is the argument. What changes is not the subject but the grammar, from reports about others to questions put to the reader.

**Praise and Peace Before the Challenge**

Before the question is put, two things are said. Praise belongs to God, and peace be upon the servants He has chosen. Only then does the interrogation begin.

The order is not decorative. A polemical question that opens with praise of God establishes that the speaker's position is not primarily against anything, and the salutation on the chosen servants ties the challenge to the line of messengers the chapter has just recounted — Mūsā, Dāwūd, Sulaymān, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ. Those five were the *chosen servants* of the preceding narratives, and greeting them here links the history to the argument that follows. There is also a formal effect. The section that is about to ask five times whether there is another god beside God begins by giving God what is owed to Him, which is the practical answer to its own question.

**The Question That Frames the Rest**

*Ā-Llāhu khayrun ammā yushrikūn* — is God better, or what they associate? This is the general form of which the following verses are instances, and the question is a strange one on its face.

Taken literally it invites a comparison, and a comparison implies commensurability — two items on one scale. The Qur'an does not usually permit that. What makes the question effective is that it accepts, for the sake of argument, the framework its opponents live by. They treat their deities as beneficial: sources of rain, intercession, protection, good fortune. Very well, says the question: compare. The verses that follow supply the terms of the comparison — creation, water, gardens, guidance, answering the distressed — and each one belongs entirely to one side. The argument is won not by refusing the comparison but by making it.

**"Better" Is a Deliberately Mild Word**

The comparative is *khayr*, better, and its mildness is calculated. The verse does not ask whether the idols are false, powerless or imaginary, though the Qur'an asserts all three elsewhere. It asks which is better.

That framing makes the question difficult to refuse. A Meccan could decline to debate whether his gods existed; declining to say which is better forces him either to name his gods as superior, which nobody in that religious system claimed, or to concede the point and keep worshipping anyway. The mildness also matches how the Qur'an characterises Meccan paganism generally. These were not people who denied God; they acknowledged Him as creator and attached intermediaries. A question about relative rank is therefore aimed precisely at the actual belief, which was never that the idols outranked God but that they were worth addressing alongside Him.

**A Section Built on Repetition**

What begins here runs for several verses with a fixed shape. Each states something God does — creating the heavens and earth, sending water, fixing the earth, answering distress, guiding through darkness, originating creation — and each ends with the same refrain asking whether there is a god beside Him.

The repetition is the method. A single instance could be argued with; six instances covering cosmology, agriculture, geography, prayer, navigation and provision leave no domain in which the associated deities have work to do. The construction is also grammatically pointed. Each question begins with a relative clause — *He who created*, *He who made* — of a kind that sets up a comparison and then does not complete it, arriving instead at the refrain. The implication carried by that incompleteness is that no explicit comparison should be offered at all. The first of the six arrives in the next verse and begins with the largest claim available.

**Who the Chosen Servants Are**

The greeting falls on *ʿibādihi alladhīna iṣṭafā* — His servants whom He has chosen. The verb *iṣṭafā* means to select out, to take the best part of something, and the Qur'an uses it of prophets and of certain communities.

Nothing in the phrase identifies which servants are meant, and the commentators reasonably read it as covering the messengers named in the chapter just concluded. That reading fits the placement exactly. Five prophets have been described in the preceding verses, and the salutation arrives at the moment their stories end. There is also a quiet argument in giving them peace at this point. The narratives showed three of them rejected, one of them threatened with expulsion, another accused of bringing bad luck; the greeting states the actual verdict on men whose own communities delivered a different one. Before the chapter asks the Meccans to compare God with their gods, it corrects the record on the people who first put the question to them. The men their own societies expelled, accused and plotted against are the ones on whom peace is pronounced here. The structure of the verse moves from a command to speak, through a statement of praise, to a question, and each step narrows the ground available to the audience.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:60

> Or ˹ask them,˺ “Who created the heavens and the earth, and sends down rain for you from the sky, by which We cause delightful gardens to grow? You could never cause their trees to grow. Was it another god besides Allah?” Absolutely not! But they are a people who set up equals ˹to Allah˺!

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Largest Thing to the Smallest**

The question begins with the creation of the heavens and the earth and ends with a tree. That movement is the verse's design, and it is more persuasive than either end would be alone.

Cosmic origins are easy to concede and easy to keep at a distance; a man can agree that God made the universe and continue his religious practice unchanged, because nothing in his week depends on the answer. The verse therefore does not stop there. It follows the chain down — heavens and earth, then rain from the sky, then gardens grown by that rain, then the specific trees in them, then the flat statement that the listener could not grow one. By the last clause the argument has arrived in the orchard the audience owns, and the concession made at the top has become a claim about their own property.

**Rain Placed Immediately After Creation**

The second item in the chain is water sent down from the sky. Placing it directly after the creation of the heavens and the earth gives it extraordinary weight, and the placement makes sense for the audience addressed.

Mecca is a dry valley. Agriculture in Arabia depended on rainfall that arrived unpredictably and could not be compelled, which is precisely why rain-making featured in the religious practices of the region. A question about who sends the water is not abstract theology in that setting; it concerns the single variable that decided whether a season ended in surplus or in famine. The Qur'an returns to rain constantly as its standard example of a sign, and the reason is this combination: it is universally necessary, entirely outside human control, and visibly arriving from somewhere else.

**"Delightful Gardens" and What the Words Mean**

The Arabic is *ḥadā'iqa dhāta bahjah*. A *ḥadīqah* is not a garden in the general sense; al-Ṭabarī defines it as an enclosed, walled garden, and al-Qurṭubī specifies gardens of date palms. The word denotes cultivated ground with a boundary around it — a property.

*Bahjah* is beauty of the kind that gladdens. Al-Ṭabarī connects it here to what is edible, the flowering and fruiting from which people and animals eat, so the delight is not purely visual. Taken together the phrase names something an Arabian audience knew as the highest form of agricultural wealth: a walled palm garden in fruit. The verse's argument is then addressed to the owner of exactly such a property. He built the wall, dug the channels, planted the offshoots and pruned the crowns, and the verse is about to tell him what he did not do.

**"You Could Never Cause Their Trees to Grow"**

The clause is emphatic in Arabic: *mā kāna lakum an tunbitū shajarahā* — it was not for you to make their trees grow. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is exact and practical: if God did not send the water, these trees would not flourish.

Al-Rāzī presses the point further, arguing that the verse removes the idea that a human being grows anything at all, since every aspect of a tree's existence and survival originates elsewhere. That is the harder claim and the verse supports it. A farmer's contribution is real and it is entirely rearrangement — he moves a seed, directs water, removes competitors. Nothing he does makes the seed open. The Qur'an puts the same argument about crops in another place, asking whether the sower or the one who sows is responsible for what is reaped. Both passages isolate the identical gap between the work a person genuinely performs and the outcome he takes credit for.

**"Was It Another God?" — the Refrain Begins**

The question closes with *a-ilāhun maʿa Allāh* — is there a god along with God? This is the refrain that will end each of the questions in this section, and its form is worth attention.

The preceding clause set up a comparison and never delivers one. The grammar leads the listener to expect something placed against God and then arrives at a question about whether anything can be placed there at all. That structural refusal is itself the argument: the power and generosity just described are of an order that should not be offered a comparison. The answer follows immediately as *bal*, a particle that cancels what came before — no, rather, they are a people who set up equals. And the verb chosen, *yaʿdilūn*, is ambiguous in a way the commentators noticed. Without a preposition to fix it, it can mean to make equal or to swerve away. Both fit. A person who assigns God an equal has, in the same act, turned aside from Him.

**The Argument Uses Only Agreed Facts**

Nothing in this verse asks the audience to accept a new premise. The heavens exist, rain falls, gardens grow, and no farmer has ever made a tree come up by willing it. Every element was available to a Meccan merchant without any revelation at all.

That restraint is what makes the question hard to answer. The Qur'an is not appealing to a miracle, a scripture or a private experience; it is pointing at the shared world and asking who is responsible for it. Pharaoh's court refused signs that were specially produced for them. The audience here is given nothing special, only the ordinary furniture of their lives, arranged so that the question becomes unavoidable. This is the method the chapter opened with when it called the Book's verses *āyāt*, signs, using the same word it uses for rain and growth. The evidence was always the world; the Book claims only to be pointing at it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:61

> Or ˹ask them,˺ “Who made the earth a place of settlement, caused rivers to flow through it, placed firm mountains upon it, and set a barrier between ˹fresh and salt˺ bodies of water? Was it another god besides Allah?” Absolutely not! But most of them do not know. 2) Who Is the Most Gracious?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Features, All of Them Underfoot**

The previous question looked up — heavens, sky, rain. This one looks down, and lists four things about the ground people stand on: it is habitable, it has rivers, it has mountains that hold it, and its waters are kept apart.

The shift is deliberate. Having established the argument at cosmic scale, the chapter now runs it at human scale, naming the conditions that make settled life possible. Each item on the list is something a person in seventh-century Arabia depended on daily and had no part in producing. The word for the earth's condition is *qarāran*, a place of settling — from a root meaning to come to rest. The claim is not merely that the earth exists but that it is stable enough to build on, which is a different and more specific assertion.

**Rivers in a Chapter Addressed to a Desert Audience**

Naming rivers to Meccans is pointed. Mecca had no river; its water came from a well, and the surrounding region was defined by the absence of permanent surface water. The audience knew rivers from travel and from the agricultural regions to the north and south.

That makes the item an argument rather than an observation. A community that lives on a well knows exactly what a river is worth, and the knowledge is the kind that comes from lacking something. The Qur'an's questions in this section consistently name provisions that are unevenly distributed, which prevents the audience from treating them as automatic features of the world. Rain that sometimes does not fall, rivers that exist elsewhere, gardens that fail — the argument works because these things are visibly contingent, and contingency is what points to a giver.

**Mountains Described as Holding the Earth Steady**

The word is *rawāsī*, from a root meaning to be firmly anchored; a ship at anchor uses the same root. The Qur'an repeatedly describes mountains this way, as stabilisers placed on the earth.

What the image asserts is a functional relationship: mountains are not merely large objects but are doing something for the ground they stand on. The verse pairs them with the earth's habitability, which is the order the argument requires — first the ground is a place of settlement, then the reason it holds. Commentators have long connected this to the practical experience of solid terrain versus shifting sand, and the modern reader may connect it to what is known about crustal structure. Neither reading is required by the verse. What the text asserts is that the arrangement is an arrangement, made rather than happened.

**The Barrier Between Two Waters**

The fourth item is the strangest: a barrier set between two bodies of water. The Qur'an returns to this image in several places, sometimes calling the divider *ḥājiz* and sometimes *barzakh*, and the pairing is usually understood as fresh water and salt.

The phenomenon is observable. Where a large river meets the sea, the two waters remain visibly distinct for a considerable distance, differing in colour, density and salinity, and they mix slowly rather than at once. Estuaries maintain a measurable boundary layer between outflowing fresh water and intruding salt. What the verse names is therefore something a traveller could see at any major river mouth on the coasts the Arabs traded along. Al-Rāzī also records an inward reading, in which the two seas are the soul's faith and its rebellion, with a barrier held between them by God's grace for as long as the soul does not turn insolent. That is allegory rather than exegesis, and it is worth reporting as his and not as the verse's.

**"Most of Them Do Not Know"**

The refrain is answered here with a statement about knowledge: *bal aktharuhum lā yaʿlamūn* — rather, most of them do not know. The previous verse ended by calling them people who set up equals. This one is gentler and more diagnostic.

Two things follow from the wording. The first is *most*, not all, which keeps the judgment honest — the Qur'an's polemic against Meccan paganism regularly leaves room for exceptions, and this chapter has just spent thirty verses on a queen who was wrong and correctable. The second is that ignorance is named rather than malice. Pharaoh's court in verse 14 knew and refused, which is the worst case; these are people who have not put the question to themselves. That diagnosis explains the form of this whole section. You do not answer ignorance with a threat. You answer it with a question, repeated in enough different domains that the answer becomes hard to avoid, which is exactly what the chapter is doing.

**The Heading in the Translation Line**

The quoted translation ends with an editorial heading that belongs to the printed edition rather than to the revelation — a label announcing the next of the questions in this sequence. It is reproduced because the blockquote follows the translation exactly.

Such headings are added by translators and publishers to help a reader find their place, and they differ from edition to edition. They carry no authority and are not recited. What this one does register accurately is a real feature of the passage: the questions in this section are distinct units, each self-contained, each ending on the same refrain, and an editor working through the chapter felt the need to number them. The verse itself ends at *most of them do not know*, and everything after that belongs to the apparatus of the book rather than to the text.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:62

> Or ˹ask them,˺ “Who responds to the distressed when they cry to Him, relieving ˹their˺ affliction, and ˹Who˺ makes you successors in the earth? Is it another god besides Allah? Yet you are hardly mindful!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Moves Inside**

The previous questions named public facts: heavens, rain, gardens, rivers, mountains, the sea. This one names something private. Who answers a desperate person who calls out.

That shift changes the kind of evidence being offered. Cosmology can be conceded intellectually and kept at a distance; the experience of calling for help and being answered cannot. Nearly everyone in the audience had been in extremity at some point — illness, debt, a lost caravan, a child not breathing — and had called out. The question asks them what they did in that moment and to whom. The Qur'an makes the observation explicitly elsewhere, that when a wave engulfs a ship its passengers call on God alone and forget their intermediaries. This verse puts the same fact as a question rather than an accusation.

**Who the "Distressed" Are**

The word is *al-muḍṭarr*, a passive form meaning one who has been driven to necessity — pushed into a corner by circumstances rather than having walked into one. Al-Qurṭubī glosses it as the helpless and powerless; al-Rāzī specifies someone in a bad situation not of their choosing, afflicted by poverty or illness.

The grammar is the point. The person described did not choose distress and cannot choose their way out; the form of the word denies them agency. That is a specific and unflattering condition, and the verse makes it the one God is named as responding to. There is no mention of the righteous, the deserving, or those with a record. The qualification for the response described here is having nothing left, which is the opposite of how access to a patron normally works in any human system.

**The Supplication With No Veil**

Al-Qurṭubī connects this verse to a report about the prayer of the wronged, and the report is well attested. When the Prophet ﷺ sent Muʿādh ibn Jabal to Yemen he instructed him at length and ended with a warning: beware the supplication of the oppressed, for there is no veil between it and God (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1496; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 19).

The context of that instruction sharpens its application here. Muʿādh was being sent as a collector of the obligatory charity, with authority over people's property, and the warning was against taking the best of their wealth. The prayer that passes without a veil is therefore the prayer of the person your own power has placed in distress. Read alongside this verse, the two texts describe the same access from two angles: the Qur'an says God answers the one driven to necessity, and the report tells those with power that this is a fact about their own position. The chapter has already shown nine notables in a city who did not think so.

**Made Successors on the Earth**

The second half of the question shifts register entirely: *wa yajʿalukum khulafā'a al-arḍ* — and who makes you successors in the earth. The word *khalīfah* means one who comes after another and takes their place.

Pairing this with the answering of the distressed produces a deliberate contrast. Human beings are, in one sentence, utterly helpless and entrusted with the earth. Both are true and the Qur'an sets them side by side without softening either. The succession also carries a warning inside it, because a successor occupies a place someone else has vacated. The chapter has just spent fifty verses on the peoples who vacated it — Thamūd's houses standing empty, Pharaoh's army gone, Lūṭ's cities buried. Whoever is being addressed holds the earth on the same terms those nations did.

**"Hardly Mindful"**

The refrain closes with *qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn* — little do you remember, or reflect. The verb is from *dhikr*, recollection, and it implies the material was already possessed.

That is the accurate charge for what this verse describes. Nobody needs to be informed that they called out in their worst hour; they need to remember it. The experience of being answered is one of the most common in human life and one of the most quickly filed away once the crisis passes, because acknowledging it has consequences for the rest of the week. The verse's complaint is therefore not against an absence of evidence but against the handling of evidence already held — the same complaint the chapter made against Pharaoh's court, who knew, and against Sheba, whose people were barred by what they already worshipped. Note also the switch to the second person: the previous verse said *most of them do not know*, and this one says *you* hardly reflect. The audience has stopped being discussed and started being addressed.

**The Response Is Described, Not Promised**

Read the grammar closely and the verse is not making a pledge about the future. It asks who is the one that responds — *amman yujību* — using a form that describes a standing characteristic rather than issuing a guarantee about any particular prayer.

The distinction matters for how the verse is used. Taken as a promise that every cry is granted as requested, it would be falsified daily by ordinary experience, and the Qur'an is not careless enough to make such a claim. What it asserts is that the one who answers the desperate is God, and that nothing else occupies that role. The question at issue is identity, not throughput. That reading also fits the polemical setting: the audience is being asked who they turned to in their worst hour, not whether they got what they asked for. Their own behaviour under pressure is the evidence being cited.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:63

> Or ˹ask them,˺ “Who guides you in the darkness of the land and sea, and sends the winds ushering in His mercy? Is it another god besides Allah? Exalted is Allah above what they associate ˹with Him˺!

**Expanded Commentary**

**Finding the Way When Nothing Is Visible**

The question names a practical problem the audience knew intimately: moving through darkness on land or at sea without losing the road. Arabia's trade depended on it, and travel across open desert or open water at night is navigation without landmarks.

Al-Qurṭubī reads the darkness as suggesting a wilderness without guideposts or signs. That is exactly the condition. A caravan crossing sand at night and a ship out of sight of shore face the same difficulty — nothing in the immediate surroundings tells you where you are. What made both possible was reading the stars, which the Qur'an elsewhere names as their purpose: landmarks set in the sky for people to find their way by. The question asks who arranged that a world without paths should nonetheless contain a means of crossing it.

**Two Kinds of Darkness**

The commentators take the phrase both literally and as an image, and both readings are defensible. Physical darkness is the plain sense and the practical danger. Beyond it, the same words describe a person who cannot see how to proceed and has no marker to steer by.

The extension is not arbitrary, because the Qur'an itself uses light and darkness for guidance and its absence throughout, and it uses *hidāyah* — the verb in this verse — for both kinds of finding the way. What makes the double sense work here is that the two share a structure. In each case the traveller is competent, the destination is real, and the missing element is a reference point outside himself. That is precisely the argument the chapter has been making about religious knowledge since its second verse, where the Book was called guidance. A man who can read stars can cross a desert; a man with no stars cannot, however skilled.

**Winds That Arrive Before the Rain**

The second item is the wind sent *bushran bayna yaday raḥmatih* — as good news before His mercy. The mercy is rain, and the wind is what comes first.

Anyone who has watched a storm gather knows the sequence: the air changes, the wind picks up and turns cool, and then the water arrives. The Qur'an describes this several times and always in the same terms, with the wind cast as a herald. What the image asserts is that the world is arranged to give notice. Relief is preceded by a sign of relief, and the sign is legible to anyone paying attention. The word for that good news is *bushrā*, the same word verse 2 used when it called the Book good news for the believers. Scripture and weather are given the same description, and the parallel is the chapter's standing method.

**The Refrain Answered With Exaltation**

Each of these questions closes differently. Verse 60 answered with a charge of setting up equals; verse 61 with ignorance; verse 62 with failure to reflect. This one answers with *taʿālā Allāhu ʿammā yushrikūn* — exalted is God above what they associate.

The escalation is worth following. The first three answers describe the people; this one describes God, and it does so by distance. *Taʿālā* means to be high above, and the preposition sets Him above the comparison itself rather than at the top of it. This is the section's most direct refusal of the framing its own questions employ. Having invited a comparison five times, the refrain here states that the comparison was never available. That is not a contradiction. The questions were addressed to people who believed a comparison was possible, and they work by allowing the attempt until the attempt collapses.

**Mercy Named as the Thing Being Announced**

The verse calls rain *His mercy* directly, not a sign of it or a result of it. That identification runs through the Qur'an, which repeatedly uses the word *raḥmah* for water arriving on dry ground.

For an agricultural society the equivalence is not metaphorical. Rain is the difference between a year of food and a year of hunger, and it comes from nowhere the farmer can reach. Naming it mercy therefore attaches an abstract divine attribute to something with a date and a measurable quantity, which is the same operation this whole section performs — taking claims about God and cashing them out in the ordinary world. Note also what the sequence implies about the two halves of this verse. Guidance through darkness and rain after wind are both cases of something needed arriving in advance of the need becoming fatal. The chapter is describing a pattern rather than listing favours, and the next question moves from what sustains life to where life came from.

**Guidance Is the Chapter's Oldest Theme**

The verb in this question, *yahdī*, has been running through an-Naml from the beginning. Verse 2 called the Book guidance; verse 4 said the deniers wander blindly; verse 24 said Sheba's people were left unguided; verse 41 asked whether the queen would be guided.

Placing the same verb here, in a question about crossing a desert at night, is the chapter's most economical connection between its two halves. The narratives were about people finding or failing to find their way with evidence in front of them. This question asks about people finding their way with stars in front of them. Both are cases of a traveller who cannot generate direction from inside himself and must read something placed outside. The Qur'an does not argue the analogy; it uses one word for both operations and lets a reader who has come this far make the link.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:64

> Or ˹ask them,˺ “Who originates the creation then resurrects it, and gives you provisions from the heavens and the earth? Is it another god besides Allah?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Show ˹me˺ your proof, if what you say is true.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Splits the Audience**

The preceding questions named things the Meccans largely agreed about. Rain, mountains, guidance through darkness, answering the distressed — a pagan Arab would have attributed all of it to God, with the idols serving as intermediaries. This question contains something they did not accept.

The two halves are *yabda'u al-khalq*, He originates creation, and *thumma yuʿīduh*, then He brings it back. The first was common ground; the Qur'an records elsewhere that if you ask them who created them they will say God. The second was the point of rupture. Resurrection was the specific claim the Meccans found absurd, and the objection they raised repeatedly was that bones, once crumbled, do not return. The verse slides the disputed item in beside the agreed one and lets the grammar do the work.

**Origination Implies Return**

Al-Rāzī states the argument the construction is making: the message of the prophets is not only that God begins everything, which many idolaters accepted, but that all things return to Him — and the origin points toward the return.

The reasoning is an argument from consistency. Whoever brought something into being from nothing faces no greater difficulty in restoring what already existed once. The Qur'an makes this explicit in other places, asking whether the one who created the heavens and the earth is unable to create their like, and answering that He certainly is. Placing origination and return in a single clause performs the same argument compactly. A listener who has conceded the first half has, without noticing, conceded the harder half, because the only ground for accepting one and rejecting the other would be that the second is more difficult, and by the terms of the first it cannot be.

**Provision From Both Directions**

The third item is *yarzuqukum min al-samā'i wa'l-arḍ* — provides for you from the heavens and the earth. Rain from above, growth from below, and the audience lives off the meeting of the two.

Al-Rāzī takes the origination-and-return clause as encompassing every other blessing named in this section, which makes the provision here a summary rather than an addition. There is also a reading, transmitted in the spiritual commentaries, that the provisions from heaven and earth are respectively the spiritual and the material. That reading is an extension and should be marked as one, though it has a foothold in the Qur'an's own habit of calling revelation something sent down. What the plain sense establishes is comprehensiveness: the sources of what keeps a person alive are named in both available directions, leaving nothing outside the account.

**"Show Me Your Proof"**

The refrain is followed by a demand that has not appeared in the previous questions: *qul hātū burhānakum in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — bring your proof, if you are truthful. The burden shifts.

This is the section's sharpest move. Five questions have put the case for one position; now the other side is asked to produce an argument of its own. The word *burhān* means a demonstrative proof, evidence that compels rather than persuades, and it is the same register as the *sulṭān mubīn* Sulaymān demanded of the hoopoe in verse 21. The Qur'an uses this demand repeatedly against those who assert partners with God, and the demand is always unanswered in the text, because the position it addresses was never argued for. Meccan paganism was inherited practice, not a set of claims defended with reasons, and asking for proof exposes that in one sentence. The Qur'an records their actual justification in other places, and it is always the same: we found our fathers doing this.

**The Condition Attached to the Demand**

The clause *in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — if you are truthful — is doing more than adding emphasis. It ties the request for evidence to the speaker's own honesty rather than to the strength of his case.

That connection has been running through the chapter. Sulaymān told the hoopoe he would see whether he was truthful or among the liars, and the bird answered with sure news from Sheba. Thamūd's nine rehearsed a false statement and certified themselves as truthful. Here the same standard is applied to the audience: a person who asserts something is claiming to know it, and a claim to know carries an obligation to be able to say how. The chapter has consistently treated the willingness to be tested as the mark of an honest position. What cannot be known by anyone at all, including the Prophet, is the subject of the verse that follows.

**The First and the Last**

The pairing of origination and return has a wider home in the Qur'an's language about God. He is named the First and the Last, the Outward and the Inward, in a formula that places Him at both ends of everything rather than at one.

That framing explains why this question sits where it does in the sequence. The preceding questions described God acting within the world — sending rain, holding mountains, answering the distressed, guiding through darkness. This one steps outside the world's duration and names the two boundaries. Nothing further can be added after it, which is why the demand for counter-evidence follows here rather than after any of the earlier questions. Having covered the span from origin to return and the provision that fills it, the section has left no domain in which a second god could be doing anything, and the challenge to produce a proof is issued into that emptiness.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:65

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “None in the heavens and the earth has knowledge of the unseen except Allah. Nor do they know when they will be resurrected.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Limit the Prophet Announces About Himself**

The instruction is *qul*, say, and what the Prophet is told to say is a statement about the boundaries of his own knowledge. Nobody in the heavens or the earth knows the unseen — a phrase that includes him, and includes the angels.

Al-Qurṭubī reports that the verse came in response to the idolaters asking when the Day of Resurrection would arrive. If that occasion is right, the answer is remarkable for what it declines to do. A man building a following on claims of special access has an obvious interest in appearing to know; the reply here is a public admission that he does not, framed as a general principle rather than as a personal apology. The Qur'an has the Prophet make this kind of disclaimer repeatedly, and the consistency of it is part of the case for the book's honesty.

**What "the Unseen" Means**

*Ghayb* in the Qur'an is not simply what happens to be out of sight. It names what belongs to a different order of reality — not a thing too distant or too small to observe, but a category no observation reaches.

The term also covers events placed beyond reach by time: the remote past nobody witnessed and the future that has not occurred. Both senses are present in this verse, since the resurrection is unseen in both ways at once. The distinction matters because it separates the Qur'an's claim from a claim about current ignorance. Better instruments do not reduce the *ghayb*; a telescope extends what is witnessed and does not touch the other category at all. What is being described is a partition in the structure of knowledge rather than a frontier that research moves.

**Knower of the Absent and the Present**

The Qur'an pairs *ghayb* with *shahādah* in a formula it uses about a dozen times, describing God as Knower of the unseen and the witnessed. The pair could equally be rendered as what is absent and what is present.

Setting the two together is what makes the attribute meaningful. A being who knew only hidden things would be a specialist; the formula asserts that the same knowledge covers both categories without difference, so that the distinction between hidden and apparent is a fact about creatures rather than about God. This chapter already used the claim in the hoopoe's argument to Sheba, where God was described as knowing what is concealed and what is disclosed. Verse 50 cashed it out in narrative when nine conspirators made a plan they believed nobody could see. The abstract attribute, the historical example and this general statement are three presentations of one claim.

**Even the Angels Are Included**

The scope is *man fī al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — whoever is in the heavens and the earth. The heavens are where the Qur'an locates the angels, and they are therefore covered by the denial.

That inclusion closes off a route the surrounding argument needs closed. Meccan religion treated certain beings as intermediaries with privileged access, and the jinn in particular were credited by soothsayers with information from above. This chapter has already shown a jinn of extraordinary power volunteering in Sulaymān's court, and it will not have escaped the audience that such a creature could carry a throne across a continent. The verse says that none of these beings knows the unseen. Power and knowledge are separated cleanly: an *ʿifrīt* can move a throne and cannot tell you when the Hour comes. Sūrah Sabaʾ makes the same point about Sulaymān's jinn labourers, who kept working after his death because they had no way of perceiving that he had died.

**Why the Hour Is Kept Hidden**

The verse specifies one item of the unseen above all others: when they will be resurrected. That particular ignorance is universal and it is not accidental.

The Qur'an elsewhere has the Prophet asked directly about the Hour and told to say its knowledge is with his Lord alone. What follows from the concealment is a way of living. A known date would convert religion into scheduling, with repentance deferred until the final year and conduct calculated against a countdown. An unknown date that is nonetheless certain requires readiness now, which is the practical result the Qur'an wants. There is also a connection to the chapter's opening. Verse 3 marked the believers as those with *certainty* about the Hereafter — certainty that it will come, paired with complete ignorance of when. Those two conditions together are what produce the disposition the whole chapter has been describing, and the next verse turns to those who have neither.

**A Claim That Cost Something to Make**

Consider the position this statement puts its speaker in. The Prophet is being publicly asked for a date, the questioners are hostile, and the answer he is instructed to give is that nobody knows and he is included in nobody.

An inventor of a religion does not write that sentence. The whole value of a claimed channel to the unseen lies in what it can produce on demand, and a movement's credibility in seventh-century Arabia was measured against soothsayers who did produce, however unreliably. Declining the question forfeits that ground entirely. The Qur'an makes the Prophet decline it repeatedly and in the most exposed circumstances, and it does the same with other things he was asked and could not answer. The pattern is one of the internal features of the text that later Muslim scholars pointed to when arguing that its author was not the man reciting it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:66

> No! Their knowledge of the Hereafter amounts to ignorance. In fact, they are in doubt about it. In truth, they are ˹totally˺ blind to it.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Statements That Get Worse**

The verse describes one group three times, and each description is more severe than the one before. Their knowledge amounts to nothing. They are in doubt. They are blind. Arabic marks the escalation with *bal* before each clause, a particle that cancels the previous statement and replaces it with something stronger.

That structure is the verse's argument. It begins by addressing them as people who claim knowledge about the Hereafter — the previous verse had them demanding a date for the resurrection — and it strips the claim in stages. First the knowledge is emptied, then the emptiness is named as doubt, then the doubt is named as an inability to see at all. By the last clause the starting position has been dismantled entirely. Someone who began the passage asking a confident question ends it described as sightless.

**A Verb the Reciters Read Differently**

The opening phrase is *bal iddāraka ʿilmuhum fī al-ākhirah*, and the classical readers vocalised the consonants in more than one way, producing genuinely different senses. Al-Rāzī sets out the options.

Read as a question, it becomes a reproach: has their knowledge reached to the Hereafter? — meaning of course it has not. Read as a statement, al-Ṭabarī reports the sense that their knowledge *will* be complete in the Hereafter, when the thing they denied is in front of them and knowing it is worth nothing. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note a third possibility, taking the verb as a contracted form of *tadāraka*, which yields the sense that their knowledge is completed there. The readings converge on one point and differ in tone. Nobody holds that these people currently know anything about the matter; the question is whether the verse mocks the claim now or predicts the moment it collapses.

**Doubt Named as the Actual Condition**

The second clause is the most precise: *bal hum fī shakkin minhā*, they are in doubt about it. This is the honest description of the position the previous verses were arguing against.

Meccan resistance to the resurrection was not a worked-out denial supported by reasons. It was an inability to take the prospect seriously, which is a different mental state and a more common one. The Qur'an names it accurately here rather than inflating it into a doctrine, and the accuracy matters because doubt and denial call for different responses. A denier has a position to be refuted. A doubter has no position at all, which is why this section of the chapter asks questions instead of mounting refutations. What the sequence of six questions was doing was giving a doubtful person something to stand on, one domain at a time.

**Blindness as the Final Term**

The third clause goes further than doubt: *bal hum minhā ʿamūn*, they are blind to it. A doubter has seen something unclearly. A blind man has not seen it at all.

The word connects to the chapter's fourth verse, where those who deny the Hereafter were described as wandering blindly with their deeds made attractive to them. Eighty-two verses later the same condition is named again with a related term, and the two passages frame everything between them. In the intervening narratives the chapter has shown what the condition produces: a court that called signs sorcery, a people who called a prophet bad luck, nine men who swore by God and planned a murder. Blindness here is not an excuse offered on their behalf. It is the accumulated result of the refusals the chapter has been documenting.

**Why the Hereafter Is the Fault Line**

Of all the disputed doctrines, an-Naml keeps returning to this one. The believers in verse 3 were marked by certainty about it; the condemned in verse 4 by disbelief in it; the questions of verse 64 by origination and return; this verse by knowledge, doubt and blindness concerning it.

The concentration is deliberate, because this is the belief with practical teeth. A person's view about the origin of the universe changes nothing about how he treats a debtor. His view about whether he will be questioned changes everything, which is why the chapter's villains are always people acting in the confidence that nothing follows. Thamūd's nine plotted at night. Pharaoh's court refused what they knew because conceding was expensive. In each case the operative assumption was that the ledger closes at death. What these three clauses describe is the epistemic state that makes such a life possible, and the objection the deniers themselves raise comes in the next verse.

**Knowledge Arriving Too Late**

One of the readings al-Ṭabarī transmits deserves separate attention, because it says something the other readings do not. On that vocalisation the clause is a statement rather than a question: their knowledge *does* reach the Hereafter — meaning it arrives there, at the point where knowing is useless.

The picture is grim and the Qur'an draws it elsewhere at length. The Book describes the deniers at the resurrection asking to be sent back, admitting what they had refused, seeing clearly at the one moment when clear sight purchases nothing. Knowledge in that setting is no longer a basis for decision; the decisions are over. What the reading adds to this verse is a completion of its three-stage description. Empty now, doubtful now, blind now — and then, at the end, full knowledge delivered to people who have nothing left to do with it. The value of understanding something depends entirely on when it arrives, and the chapter has been arguing since its opening verses that the interval available for acting on it is the thing being spent.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:67

> The disbelievers ask, “When we and our fathers are reduced to dust, will we really be brought forth ˹alive˺?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Their Own Words, Quoted**

The chapter now lets the opposition speak. For two verses the Qur'an reports the Meccan objection in the first person, without paraphrase, and the objection is a question about dust.

Quoting an opponent accurately is a choice, and the Qur'an makes it consistently — Pharaoh's speeches are recorded, Iblīs is given his arguments, and the councils of the destroyed nations are reported in their own words. The practice has an effect on how the refutation lands. A reader who has heard the objection stated at full strength is in a different position from one handed only the answer. This particular question appears in near-identical form in half a dozen chapters, which indicates it was the standing Meccan reply rather than a remark made once.

**The Objection Is About Physical Bodies**

What they find incredible is specific. Not that God exists, not that He created them, but that a person reduced to *turāb*, dust, could be brought out again as himself. The verb they use, *mukhrajūn*, means taken out — the image is of extraction from a grave.

That specificity tells us the dispute was never theological in the broad sense. The Meccans conceded a creator; the Qur'an says so elsewhere. What they could not accept was a bodily resurrection, and the reason is visible in their phrasing. They are reasoning from an observation: decomposition is total, the material disperses, and nothing in ordinary experience reverses it. As an inference from available evidence it is entirely sound. It is also, the chapter has just argued, an inference drawn from the wrong evidence base, since the question is not what usually happens but what the one who began the process can do.

**"We and Our Fathers"**

They include their ancestors in the objection, and the inclusion is not incidental. It appears again in the next verse, where the promise is said to have been made to their fathers before them.

Two things are being done by the phrase. The first is rhetorical amplification: generations have gone into the ground and none has come out, which strengthens the empirical case. The second is social. Accepting the resurrection means accepting that the men whose way of life they inherited are also subject to it, and the Qur'an records elsewhere that the fear of implicating the fathers was a real obstacle to Meccan conversion. A doctrine that condemns your ancestors is harder to accept than one that merely inconveniences you. Their own wording exposes the pressure they were under.

**Al-Rāzī on What the Doubt Is Really About**

Al-Rāzī identifies the root of the objection: doubt about the resurrection comes from doubt about the power of God, and it amounts to a failure of ordinary human imagination. That diagnosis is exact and it locates the problem where the Qur'an locates it.

The Book answers this objection in several places by pointing at scale. If the one who created the heavens and the earth is asked whether He can create their like, the question answers itself — and the Qur'an puts it in precisely those terms in Yā Sīn. The argument is one of proportion. A person who can accept the larger act and balk at the smaller one is not applying a consistent standard; he is applying an imaginative one, in which what he can picture seems possible and what he cannot seems absurd. Al-Rāzī's phrase for this is the failure of ordinary imagination, and it is the correct description of an objection that rests on never having seen something happen.

**Dust Is the Qur'an's Own Word for the Beginning**

There is an irony in the term they chose. *Turāb*, dust or earth, is the substance the Qur'an repeatedly names as what the human being was made from in the first place.

Their objection therefore runs: how can we be brought out when we are dust? — and the Book's account of their origin gives the answer inside the question. They began as the material they are afraid of becoming. Whatever happened at the start is being denied as impossible at the end, using the same noun for both. The Qur'an does not point this out in this verse, and it does not need to; the vocabulary is shared and the audience knew both claims. What the chapter has already put in place, three verses earlier, is the principle that resolves it: the one who originates the creation is the one who brings it back, and the second act is not the harder of the two.

**Why This Objection Recurs Across the Qur'an**

The same question appears in nearly identical wording in ar-Raʿd, al-Isrāʾ, al-Muʾminūn, aṣ-Ṣāffāt and al-Wāqiʿah. Five chapters, one sentence, barely altered — which tells us the Qur'an was answering a fixed formulation rather than a shifting set of doubts.

A repeated objection usually indicates a settled talking point, something a community has reduced to a single line that closes the conversation. The Meccan version had all the marks of one: short, concrete, framed as a question, and apparently unanswerable by observation. The Qur'an's treatment of it is correspondingly consistent. It never disputes the premise that bodies decompose, and it never offers a mechanism for reassembly. What it does, every time, is relocate the question from what has been observed to who is being asked, which is the only place the argument can actually be settled. The objection is about the limits of nature; the reply is about who set them. That is why none of the answers in the Qur'an is technical. Each one asks the questioner to compare the difficulty of the second act with the difficulty of the first, and the comparison always runs the wrong way for the objector.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:68

> We have already been promised this, as well as our forefathers earlier. This is nothing but ancient fables!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Objection**

Their speech continues, and the argument shifts from the physical to the historical. The first verse said resurrection is incredible; this one says the claim is old. We were promised this, and our fathers before us, and nothing came of it.

The move is a familiar one and it is not stupid. A prediction that has been repeated across generations without arriving loses force with each repetition, and the Meccans are pointing at a track record. Prophets before Muḥammad ﷺ had warned of the same accounting; the warnings are in the stories the chapter has just told. Nobody's grandfather rose. What the objection does not consider is whether a promise with no stated date can be falsified by the passage of time, which is the flaw the chapter will exploit shortly.

**"Ancient Fables"**

The dismissal is *asāṭīr al-awwalīn*, the tales of the ancients. The phrase appears repeatedly in the Qur'an as the standard Meccan verdict on the recitation, and it is worth taking seriously as a classification rather than an insult.

Every culture has a category for stories that are told, valued, and not believed — legends, old wives' tales, the things people say. Filing the Qur'an in that category solves the problem of what to do with a text that is manifestly impressive and inconveniently demanding. It concedes the artistry and removes the obligation. The move is identical in structure to the one Pharaoh's court made in verse 13 of this chapter, when they called the signs *pure magic*: assign the phenomenon to a known and harmless category, and the question of its source disappears. Two peoples, separated by a thousand years, reaching for the same manoeuvre.
