Neither of them examined the classification before applying it, which is what makes the two cases parallel rather than merely similar. A label adopted because it settles a problem is not a judgment about evidence.

**The Charge Is Self-Undermining**

Calling the material ancient fables sits badly with the objection in the previous verse. There they argued from observation that bodies do not come back. Here they argue that the claim is merely a repeated story. These are different kinds of complaint, and the second weakens the first.

If the teaching is only old storytelling, why is it being repeated by a man they have known for forty years, in a form they cannot match, with content about peoples whose ruins they pass on the trade road? And if the objection is genuinely empirical, the age of the claim is irrelevant — a true statement does not become false by being made often. The Qur'an lets both halves of their speech stand next to each other without comment. Its reply, in the next verse, addresses neither the metaphysics nor the literary classification, and instead sends them to look at something.

**Inherited Objections as Well as Inherited Religion**

Notice what they are actually reporting: their fathers heard this and dismissed it, and they are dismissing it too. The continuity they claim is a continuity of refusal.

That is the same appeal the Qur'an records elsewhere when it has them say they found their fathers on a way and are following their footsteps. What this verse adds is that the inheritance includes the counter-argument. A community passes down not only its practices but its ready answers, and a ready answer that has survived several generations feels like wisdom rather than habit. The chapter has already shown the alternative in Sheba, where a queen inherited sun worship and, when a claim reached her, tested it rather than reaching for what her court already said. The difference between her and this audience is not intelligence or evidence. It is whether the inherited reply gets examined.

**Why the Qur'an Preserves the Insult**

The Book could have summarised this objection. Instead it records the mockery verbatim, including the dismissive tone, and it does so in a text its own community would recite for centuries.

There is a confidence in that decision worth naming. A movement anxious about its standing suppresses the jibes made against it; this one preserves them in scripture, in the mouths of named opponents, with the rhetorical force intact. The same chapter recorded a prophet fleeing from a staff and a king threatening a bird. What the practice produces is a text that cannot be accused of hiding the case against it, and the answer it then gives is correspondingly plain. The Prophet is told to respond with a single instruction, and it is not an argument.

**What "Promised" Concedes**

Their complaint contains an admission. They say they *were promised* this, and their fathers before them — which acknowledges that the warning has a history, was delivered to earlier generations, and has been consistent.

That is a considerable thing to grant while dismissing the content. A teaching invented recently by one man in Mecca would not have reached their forefathers, and they are not claiming it did. They are claiming that a long-standing, repeated promise has not been kept. The Qur'an's reply to that complaint is distributed across the chapter rather than stated here: the promise has no date attached, the peoples who treated the delay as a refutation are the ones whose ruins now stand on the trade roads, and the delay itself is described elsewhere as a space given for return. Their own phrasing establishes the continuity of the message, which is one of the things the chapter has been arguing. An objection that concedes its opponent's main historical claim is not a strong objection. The objection is given in full before it is answered, which is how this chapter consistently handles the positions it rejects.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:69

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Travel throughout the land and see the fate of the wicked.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply Is an Instruction, Not an Argument**

They called the message ancient fables. The answer is: go and look. *Sīrū fī al-arḍ fa-anẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-mujrimīn* — travel in the land and see how the guilty ended.

Nothing in the reply engages the philosophical objection about dust and bodies at all. The reply changes the mode of the dispute from assertion to inspection, which is the move the whole chapter has been preparing. An-Naml has twice already issued this command about a specific people: at Pharaoh's court in verse 14 and at Thamūd in verse 51. Here the same imperative is generalised and attached to a journey. What was a rhetorical instruction in those two places becomes a practical one here.

**Travel Was Available to This Audience**

The command is not abstract for the Quraysh. They were a merchant community whose wealth came from two annual caravans, north to Syria and south to Yemen, and the Qur'an names those journeys in another chapter as the defining feature of their livelihood.

Both routes passed ruins. The northern road went by the rock-cut façades of Thamūd's settlements and near the region of Lūṭ's destroyed cities; the southern road went toward Sheba, whose great dam and irrigated gardens the Qur'an describes as lost. The people being told to travel and look were already travelling, twice a year, past the evidence in question. That is what makes the instruction sharp rather than pious. It asks them to notice something they have been riding past for their entire commercial lives, and to draw from it the conclusion the chapter has already drawn twice.

**Looking With More Than the Eyes**

Al-Qurṭubī records the reading that *fa-anẓurū* here means to observe with the heart and with insight rather than merely with the eye. The gloss is not a spiritualising of a plain command; it identifies a real requirement.

Caravans had passed those ruins for centuries, and the passing produced no conclusions. Seeing a collapsed city and drawing an inference from it are separate operations, and the second requires a willingness to treat the ruin as a case rather than as scenery. This is the same restriction verse 52 attached to the empty houses of Thamūd, where the sign was said to be for a people who know. The evidence is public and the reading of it is not automatic, which is why the Qur'an issues an imperative about looking rather than simply stating what is there.

**"The Guilty" Rather Than "the Disbelievers"**

The word for those whose end is to be examined is *al-mujrimīn*, the criminals or the guilty, from a root that means to commit a serious offence. It is not the ordinary word for those who reject faith.

The choice keeps the argument on the ground the ruins can support. What a traveller sees at these sites is the outcome of conduct — nations that oppressed, cheated, plotted and refused, and then ended. The chapter's own catalogue supports the term: Pharaoh's court called corruptors, Thamūd's nine who worked corruption and never set anything right, the people of Lūṭ who tried to expel a household for being clean. Naming them by their crimes rather than their creed makes the lesson legible to anyone, and it also makes the warning harder to evade. A Meccan could dispute a theological category. He could not dispute that the men who lived in those houses are gone.

**A Different Kind of Evidence Than the Previous Section**

The six questions that preceded this passage drew on nature — rain, mountains, rivers, the guidance of stars. This verse turns to history, and the shift is a widening of the case rather than a change of subject.

Both kinds of evidence are called *āyāt* in the Qur'an, and both are public, checkable and unmiraculous. Nothing in either category requires the audience to accept a report about something they did not witness. What the two have in common is that they answer the charge of *ancient fables* in the only way that charge can be answered: by pointing at things that are not stories. A ruin on a trade road is not a tale told by old men. The Prophet's own position while all of this is being said is addressed in the next verse.

**A Command Repeated Across the Book**

This instruction is not unique to an-Naml. The Qur'an issues it in similar terms in Yūsuf, ar-Rūm, Fāṭir, Ghāfir and Muḥammad, each time inviting the audience to move through the land and examine how earlier peoples ended.

The frequency establishes it as a standing method rather than a local retort. What the Book is proposing is that history functions as a body of evidence with a legible pattern, and that the pattern is available to anyone willing to travel and observe. That is a strong claim and it accepts a risk: a reader who looks and finds no pattern has been handed a refutation. The Qur'an makes the offer anyway, repeatedly, which is not the behaviour of a text expecting the inspection to fail. It is also worth noting what the command does not ask for. Nobody is told to consult a scholar, accept a report, or believe a story. They are told to go. The evidence is placed outside the argument entirely, in terrain that belongs to nobody, and the audience is invited to reach it under their own power. That is an unusual way to conduct a religious dispute, and it is the way this chapter has conducted its case from the opening verse, where the Book called its own sentences signs rather than sentences.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:70

> Do not grieve for them, nor be distressed by their schemes.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Turns to the Messenger's Own State**

After verses of argument directed at the opposition, this one is addressed to the man delivering it, and it concerns how he feels. Do not grieve over them, and do not be constricted by what they plot.

The placement of it says something about what had been happening in Mecca. A command not to grieve is issued to someone who is grieving, and the Qur'an gives the Prophet this instruction repeatedly across several chapters. The pattern indicates a persistent condition rather than a passing mood. He was distressed by the refusal of his own people, and the Book addresses that distress directly rather than treating it as something unworthy of mention in scripture.

**Grief for Them, Not About Them**

The Arabic is *lā taḥzan ʿalayhim* — do not grieve *over* them. The preposition matters. He is not being told to stop being upset by their behaviour; he is being told to stop mourning for them.

That is a different emotion and a more specific one. The men mocking him were his kin, his neighbours, his clan. He had grown up among them and knew what was coming to people who persisted, having just recited the fate of Thamūd and Lūṭ's people. What he is described as feeling is the sorrow of someone watching people he cares about walk toward something. The Qur'an elsewhere puts this in stronger terms, telling him he might destroy himself with grief over their refusal to believe — an image of a man being consumed by anxiety about others. The instruction here is a limit on that, not a rebuke for having it.

**"Distressed" Means Constricted**

The second half uses *ḍayq*, from a root meaning narrowness and contraction. The image is physical: a space closing in, a chest tightening. Arabic uses the same root for a cramped room and for the feeling described here.

That is an accurate word for the effect of sustained hostility. Being plotted against does not primarily produce fear; it produces a narrowing, in which the range of what feels possible contracts and every interaction is examined for threat. The Qur'an chooses the word that names the sensation rather than a general term for worry, which is characteristic of how it handles interior states throughout. And what it identifies as the cause is precise — *mimmā yamkurūn*, from what they plot, the same verb used of Thamūd's nine conspirators twenty verses earlier. The Prophet was facing plots of the same kind, and the chapter has just shown the reader how one such plot ended.

**Consolation Grounded in What Preceded It**

This verse works because of its position. Immediately before it, the audience was told to travel and see how the guilty ended. Immediately after that instruction, the Prophet is told not to be crushed by the scheming of people currently in that category.

The connection is left implicit and it is the whole argument. He has just recited an account of nine men who swore an oath, made a plan, and were answered by a plan they could not perceive. Being told not to feel constricted by plots, one verse later, is not a request to suppress a reaction; it is a conclusion drawn from evidence just presented. The chapter does the consoling by means of the history it has spent sixty verses assembling, which is more durable than reassurance would be.

**The Qur'an Records a Prophet's Emotional Life**

Set this verse alongside the others in this chapter that report interior states. Mūsā ran from a staff in fear. Sulaymān smiled at an ant and worried that his own blessings were a test. Now Muḥammad ﷺ is grieving and constricted, and the Book says so.

None of these is presented as a failing, and none is edited out. The pattern is consistent enough to count as a position: the men God chose are shown feeling what people feel, and the feeling is addressed rather than denied. That has a practical consequence for how the text has been read. A community whose founder is recorded as needing to be told not to grieve has scriptural warrant for its own grief, which is a different inheritance from one whose exemplars are depicted as serene. What the mockers said next, and how the Prophet was instructed to answer, follows immediately.

**Two Burdens, Two Different Sources**

The verse names two distinct pressures and they do not come from the same place. Grief comes from caring about people who are refusing; constriction comes from being the target of their plotting. One is the cost of compassion, the other the cost of opposition.

Separating them is useful because they call for different consolations. The grief is answered by the fact that guidance is not the messenger's to grant — the Qur'an tells him elsewhere that he cannot guide whom he loves but God guides whom He wills. The constriction is answered by the chapter's account of what happens to plots, which the reader has just watched fail at Thamūd. Neither answer denies that the feeling is warranted. What the verse does is set a limit on how far each should be allowed to go, addressed to a man who by every indication was exceeding it. The instruction is brief because it is corrective rather than explanatory; the explanation has already been given at length. A man who has just recited the history of four nations does not need to be told again why plots fail. He needs to be told that the conclusion applies to his own situation, and that is what this single sentence does.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:71

> They ask ˹the believers˺, “When will this threat come to pass, if what you say is true?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Demand for a Date**

The objection narrows to a single question: when. Not whether, not how, but the timing, and the demand is framed with a challenge attached — *in kuntum ṣādiqīn*, if you are truthful.

The question appears in this form throughout the Qur'an, addressed to several prophets by several peoples, always about the same promise. What it refers to is the end of the world and the judgment that follows, which the chapter's previous verses have been arguing for. The demand has a surface reasonableness. A claim about the future that never specifies a date can seem to be protecting itself, and asking for a date is the natural way to test it. That is what makes the question effective as rhetoric and useless as inquiry.

**The Condition Turned Around**

The challenge clause is the same one the Prophet was instructed to use against them seven verses earlier, when he was told to say: show me your proof, if what you say is true. Now they return the identical phrase.

The symmetry is unlikely to be accidental in a chapter this carefully constructed. Both sides are invoking the same standard of honesty, and the difference lies in what each is being asked to produce. He was asked for a *burhān*, a demonstrative proof for a claim about who deserves worship, which is a thing arguments can supply. They are asking for a date, which is a thing the Qur'an has just said nobody in the heavens or the earth possesses. The two demands look parallel and are not. One requests something the position should be able to deliver; the other requests something the position has explicitly denied having.

**Why the Question Cannot Be Answered**

Verse 65 had already closed this off. Nobody in the heavens or the earth knows the unseen except God, and the resurrection was named there as the specific case. The demand in this verse asks for exactly what was just declared unavailable.

That makes the question a trap rather than an inquiry, and the questioners presumably knew it. If the Prophet names a date he either proves himself false when it passes or contradicts what he has already said. If he declines, he can be presented as evasive. The Qur'an's reply, in the next verse, does neither. It gives a partial answer that concedes the ignorance and removes the comfort the ignorance was supposed to provide, which is the only move available that is both honest and effective.

**What the Question Is Actually For**

Nobody asking this wants a date in order to prepare. The function of the demand is to convert a warning into a claim that can be falsified by waiting, and waiting is something the questioners were going to do anyway.

This connects to what Ṣāliḥ confronted at Thamūd. His people were hastening the punishment, calling for it, and he asked why they were hurrying toward the bad outcome when the good one was available. The Meccans here are performing a more sophisticated version of the same act: demanding the arrival of the thing they claim not to believe in, and treating each day of its absence as evidence. The chapter has already recorded both peoples using time as an argument. Both are spending the interval that was given for something else.

**The Address Is to the Believers**

The question is put to a group — the Arabic verb is plural — and it is aimed at the whole community rather than at the Prophet alone. That detail is worth registering.

By this stage the Muslims in Mecca were a visible minority with a doctrine their neighbours found absurd, and the demand for a date would have been thrown at them in markets and households rather than delivered formally to their leader. What the verse records is the texture of daily pressure: the recurring taunt, phrased as a question, that a person has no good answer to. The reply supplied in the next verse is short enough to be used in exactly such an exchange, and it is instructive that the Qur'an gives them something to say rather than telling them to endure the mockery in silence.

**The Same Demand Across Several Chapters**

This question recurs in Yūnus, al-Anbiyāʾ, Sabaʾ, Yā Sīn and al-Mulk in almost identical wording. Five other chapters carry it, and in each the reply is a variation on what an-Naml gives here: a refusal to name a time, coupled with a statement that the thing is nearer than assumed.

The consistency of both the question and the answer is informative. It indicates that this was the settled shape of the dispute in Mecca over a period of years — not an argument that developed, but a challenge repeated and a reply repeated. It also means the Qur'an never broke its own rule under pressure. Across every instance of the demand, no date is ever given, no approximation offered, and no sign specified by which the timing could be calculated. A text produced under that much provocation, by an author who wanted to convince, would have been sorely tempted at some point. This one holds the line in all six places.

**A Threat and a Promise Are the Same Event**

The translation renders the disputed thing as *this threat*, and the Arabic *waʿd* is the ordinary word for a promise. Arabic marks the negative sense with a related form, *waʿīd*, and the choice of the neutral word here is worth noticing.

To the questioners it is a threat, and they are treating it as one. To the believers listening it is a promise, since the same day that brings the reckoning brings what the chapter's second verse called good news. One event, two names, and which name a person uses is determined entirely by where he expects to stand. That is why the question is asked with a sneer rather than with anxiety. A man genuinely uncertain about which side of the promise he would fall on would not demand its arrival, and the mockery is itself a report of what the questioners assume about themselves.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:72

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Perhaps some of what you seek to hasten is close at hand.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Answer That Refuses the Question and Still Answers**

They demanded a date. The reply gives none, and yet it is not an evasion: perhaps some of what you are hurrying toward is right behind you.

Three hedges are built into one short sentence — *ʿasā*, perhaps; *baʿḍu*, some of; and a verb of nearness rather than a time. Nothing is promised, nothing is scheduled, and nothing is denied. What the sentence does is remove the assumption their question was resting on. They asked when, expecting either a falsifiable date or an admission of ignorance; they are given the possibility that the answer is soon, which is the one response that makes the delay uncomfortable instead of reassuring.

**"Close Behind You"**

The verb is *radifa*, and al-Ṭabarī glosses it as having come near. Al-Qurṭubī adds the root's more specific sense: to follow directly in someone's footsteps, the way a second rider sits behind the first on the same mount.

That image is exact and it is unnerving. What follows in your footsteps is not visible from where you stand, is not in front of you where it could be watched, and is closer than anything you can see. The word does not say the punishment is coming; it says it may already be behind them, moving at their own pace. A demand for a date assumes the threatened event is somewhere ahead on a road, at a measurable distance. The reply places it in the one position that cannot be measured at all.

**"Some of What You Seek to Hasten"**

The partitive is doing real work. Not all of it — *some* of what you are hurrying toward. The Qur'an is distinguishing between two things the questioners had merged.

They asked about the resurrection, a single event at the end of everything. The answer speaks of a portion arriving sooner, which implies that the accounting comes in instalments and that the final one is not the only one. This is consistent with the chapter's own histories: Pharaoh's army drowned, Thamūd's houses were emptied, Lūṭ's cities were buried, and none of those was the Day of Judgment. Al-Rāzī and others took the near portion here as the defeat the Meccan leadership suffered at Badr, which came within a few years of this chapter's revelation. That identification is a commentator's inference rather than the verse's claim, and it should be marked as such, though the structure of the sentence plainly allows for it.

**Why "Perhaps" Is the Honest Word**

*ʿAsā* in the Qur'an, spoken by God, is not the hedge of someone uncertain. Classical commentators generally read the particle in divine speech as indicating something realised, softened in expression rather than in content.

But the softening is not empty here, and the reason returns to verse 65. The Prophet has been told, and has publicly said, that nobody knows the unseen. A flat prediction in his mouth would contradict that statement four verses later. *ʿAsā* is therefore the precise word: it lets him convey the nearness without claiming the knowledge, and it keeps the two verses consistent. The Qur'an is careful about this in a way a careless text would not be, and the care is itself evidence of how seriously the limit announced in verse 65 was meant.

**The Interval Is the Point**

Strip the exchange to its logic and the two sides are arguing about the same gap in opposite directions. They treat the time before the promised reckoning as evidence that it is not coming. The reply treats it as the space in which the question stops mattering.

What the chapter has been saying about that interval is consistent. Ṣāliḥ asked his people why they were hastening the bad outcome when forgiveness was available in the meantime. The hoopoe's report led to a letter rather than an army, because there was time for an argument. Sheba's queen used her interval to test, consult and travel. Nothing in the chapter suggests that the delay is meaningless; the delay is where everything the chapter cares about happens. A person demanding a date has already decided the interval is empty, and the answer he receives tells him it may be shorter than he assumes.

**What the Commentators Identified as the Near Portion**

Al-Rāzī and others took the part described as close behind them to be the defeat the Meccan leadership suffered at Badr, which came within a few years of this chapter's revelation. The identification is common in the tafsīr literature, where warnings of impending punishment in the Meccan sūrahs are frequently read this way.

The reading has something in its favour beyond hindsight. The verse specifies *some* of what they were hurrying toward, which invites exactly this kind of partial fulfilment, and the men who pressed the question hardest were largely the men who died at Badr. But its status is clear, and it is not a transmitted report. No ḥadīth attaches Badr to this verse; the connection is an inference drawn backwards from a known outcome, and the tafsīr literature applies the same inference to Meccan warning-verses generally, which is a sign that it is a habit of reading rather than a finding about this sentence. That does not make it wrong. It makes it an interpretation, and one that the verse's deliberate vagueness neither supports nor excludes. What the text commits to is only the structure — a portion, near, unnamed.

**Answering a Taunt Without Adopting Its Terms**

The reply is a model of how the chapter handles hostile questions. It does not accept the premise that a date is owed, does not concede that the absence of a date is a weakness, and does not counter-attack.

What it does is supply a possibility the questioner had not budgeted for. Their challenge assumed two outcomes, a date they could test or an admission they could mock. The answer produces a third, and the third is the one that costs them something to hear. Sulaymān used the same technique with the envoys from Sheba, refusing the gift rather than negotiating over it, and the queen used it herself when she declined both war and surrender and sent a gift instead. An-Naml is full of people who decline the options they are offered and construct a better one, and the reply here is the last instance of it in the chapter.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:73

> Surely your Lord is ever Bountiful to humanity, but most of them are ungrateful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bounty Stated Immediately After a Threat**

The previous verse told the mockers that part of what they were hurrying toward might be right behind them. This one says God is bountiful to mankind. The juxtaposition is abrupt and it is the point.

What connects the two is the interval. If some portion of the reckoning is close and has not yet arrived, then the time still available is itself the bounty being described. The Qur'an makes this link explicit elsewhere, saying that if God were to take people to task for what they have earned He would not leave a creature on the earth, and that He defers them to a stated term. Read against verse 72, this verse identifies the delay as generosity rather than as delay. The threat and the favour are the same fact described from two sides.

**"Toward Mankind" — Not Toward the Believers**

The recipient named is *al-nās*, humanity at large. Not the faithful, not the grateful, not those who acknowledge Him. The bounty is described as reaching everyone, including the people who have just been mocking the promise.

That universality is a settled feature of how the Qur'an describes divine provision. Rain falls on the fields of those who deny; the man who sets up partners eats from gardens that God's water grew; the interval before the reckoning is given to the obstinate and the sincere alike. The chapter's fourth verse had God Himself named as the one who made evil deeds attractive to those who deny the Hereafter, which is a statement of how the moral order works rather than an exception to it — even that is a law operating within a world where the deniers are fed. Two divine names in the chapter's first half, Self-Sufficient and Most Generous, were paired for exactly this reason: He does not need the response and gives anyway.

**Ingratitude as the Standard Condition**

The second half is a demographic claim: *wa lākinna aktharahum lā yashkurūn* — but most of them are not grateful. The Qur'an repeats this observation in a dozen places, always with the same word for the majority.

The consistency indicates it is meant as a description rather than a complaint. What makes ingratitude the default is not wickedness but familiarity: a benefit that arrives every day stops registering as a benefit, and nothing in ordinary experience corrects the drift. The chapter has already shown the alternative in a man far better positioned to be ungrateful than most. Sulaymān, given a share of all things, asked to be marshalled into thankfulness, as though gratitude were a formation that breaks without discipline. He identified in himself exactly the tendency this verse names in the majority, and he treated it as something requiring active management rather than assuming his own good character would supply it.

**Why Ingratitude Is Grouped With Disbelief**

The root of *yashkurūn* is *sh-k-r*, thanks, and its opposite in the Qur'an is *kufr* — the same word the Book uses for disbelief. The overlap is not loose usage.

*Kufr* at root means to cover over, and the image behind it is concealment of something that is there. A farmer covering a seed with soil uses the same root in classical Arabic. Applied to gifts, it names the act of covering over a benefit received so that it no longer shows; applied to God, it names covering over Him. Sulaymān's speech at the arrival of the throne used both terms in a single sentence, asking whether he would be grateful or *akfur*. The chapter therefore has a precise vocabulary at work: the deniers of verse 4 and the ungrateful of this verse are described with one root, because concealing a giver and concealing a gift are the same operation performed on different objects.

**A Verse Placed Between Warning and Surveillance**

Look at the neighbours. Verse 72 warns that the reckoning may be near. Verse 74 says God knows what their hearts conceal and what they disclose. Between the threat and the surveillance sits a statement about generosity.

The placement prevents the passage from reading as intimidation. A God who watches and warns and gives nothing would produce fear; the sequence here inserts the bounty in the middle, so that the knowing and the warning are attributes of someone already described as generous to the very people being warned. This is the chapter's method in miniature, and it matches what it did at the start: verse 2 called the Book guidance and good news, and only in verse 4 turned to those who refuse. Threat is never the first thing said in this chapter, and it is never the only thing said.

**Bounty Described as Continuous**

The construction is *la-dhū faḍlin ʿalā al-nās* — possessed of bounty toward mankind, with the emphatic particle attached. It is a description of a standing characteristic rather than a report of particular gifts.

The Qur'an uses the same phrase in a handful of other places, always in the same form, and always without listing what the bounty consists of. That vagueness is functional. A list would invite comparison and complaint, since people's portions differ enormously and always have. What the phrase asserts instead is the disposition behind whatever any given person has received, which is a claim that survives the unevenness of the distribution. The chapter has already built the specifics in its questions section: rain, gardens, rivers, mountains, guidance through darkness, an answer to the desperate. This verse names the source of all of it in four words and moves on to the response it does not receive. Stated this way, the claim can be tested against a person's own experience rather than only against the histories the chapter has recounted.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:74

> And surely your Lord knows what their hearts conceal and what they reveal.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Recurring Claim, Stated Plainly**

This assertion has appeared three times in an-Naml now. The hoopoe used it against Sheba's idolatry, describing God as knowing what is concealed and what is disclosed. Verse 50 demonstrated it in narrative, with nine conspirators inside a plan they could not see. Here it is stated flatly about the present audience.

The repetition tracks the chapter's structure. First the principle is voiced by a character, then it is shown operating in history, and now it is applied to the people listening. That is the same movement the whole sūrah makes — argument, demonstration, application — and this verse is where the third stage arrives for this particular claim. Nothing new is being asserted. What changes is who is being described.

**"What Their Breasts Conceal"**

The Arabic locates the concealment in the *ṣudūr*, the chests, and the Qur'an uses this term dozens of times for the seat of hidden thought and intention. What a person keeps in the chest is what nobody else can reach.

The physical metaphor is worth taking seriously because of what it implies. A chest is a container with a lid; things are kept in it deliberately, and keeping them there takes effort. The Qur'an's language for the inner life is consistently of this kind — things are held, covered, sealed, or brought out — rather than the language of a transparent interior that simply happens to be invisible. That framing makes concealment an act rather than a condition, which is why the verse pairs it with disclosure. Both are things people do, and the claim is that the two are equally available to God.

**Concealment Is Where Sincerity Is Decided**

Why does the Qur'an return to this attribute so often? Because the gap between what is held and what is shown is where the moral reality of a person is located.

Public conduct can be produced by pressure, habit or calculation, and a community can be full of people performing correctly for reasons that have nothing to do with belief. The Qur'an addresses this repeatedly, warning about those who say with their mouths what is not in their hearts. What this verse asserts is that the performance is not the record. It also cuts in the other direction, and the chapter has shown that side too — Sheba's queen worshipped the sun in public while, on one reading of verse 42, already knowing something she had not yet declared. The attribute is not only a threat.

**Placed Immediately After a Statement of Bounty**

The previous verse said God is bountiful to mankind and most are ungrateful. This one says He knows what is hidden and what is shown. The order matters.

Ingratitude is often an interior matter. A person can receive a benefit, say the right thing about it, and privately regard it as his due — and no observer could tell the difference between him and someone genuinely thankful. Verse 73 names the failure; verse 74 says it is visible. Together they close the gap that gratitude most easily slips through. Sulaymān's reaction to the throne is the counter-example the chapter has already supplied: his first words on receiving an extraordinary gift concerned whether he would be thankful, which is a man examining exactly the interior this verse describes.

**Knowledge Without a Verdict Attached**

The verse states what God knows and stops. No punishment is named, no reward, no consequence at all. That restraint is characteristic of how the Qur'an deploys this attribute.

A statement of omniscience does not need a threat attached to do its work, and adding one would narrow it. As it stands, the sentence applies equally to the mockers demanding a date, the believers enduring them, and every reader since. What each of those parties has hidden differs, and the verse addresses all of them with the same words. The Qur'an makes a nearly identical statement in Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ, and in both places the effect is the same: a fact is asserted, and the hearer supplies the application from his own knowledge of what he is holding. The next verse widens the claim from human interiors to everything hidden anywhere.

**Knowing Both, Not Only the Hidden**

The verse names two things and not one: what they conceal *and* what they reveal. A claim about knowing secrets alone would have been the expected form, and the addition of the second term changes the sense.

What it rules out is a picture in which the public life is unimportant and only the interior counts. Both are known, and both are therefore part of the record. That matters for a religion with an extensive outward practice — prayer at fixed times, alms at fixed rates, obligations discharged in front of witnesses. The chapter's third verse marked the believers by exactly such visible acts. If only the hidden were weighed, those would be theatre; if only the visible, the hypocrite would be safe. The pairing keeps both on the ledger, which is the arrangement the chapter's opening description of the believers already assumed.

**An Attribute That Requires No Enforcement**

There is a practical consequence to this claim that the Qur'an relies on throughout its ethics. A community that genuinely holds it needs less policing, because the most effective constraint on conduct is not the likelihood of being caught but the conviction that there is nothing to catch.

Islamic law developed with famously high evidential standards for its severest penalties — requirements so demanding that conviction was rare by design. That is workable only alongside a belief of the kind this verse states. Where the record is complete regardless of what any court establishes, the legal system is not the primary mechanism of restraint and does not need to be. The chapter has already illustrated the failure case in Thamūd's nine, who calculated precisely what their local system could prove and arranged their testimony accordingly. They had understood the law and missed the verse.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:75

> For there is nothing hidden in the heavens or the earth without being ˹written˺ in a perfect Record.

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Human Secrets to Everything Concealed**

The previous verse said God knows what hearts conceal. This one removes the restriction to hearts. Nothing hidden anywhere in the heavens or the earth falls outside a written record.

The widening is deliberate and it changes the scale of the claim. What was an assertion about the transparency of human intention becomes an assertion about the structure of reality: concealment is a relation between creatures and things, not a property of the things themselves. Something can be hidden from everyone and still be recorded. That is a stronger statement than omniscience alone, because it adds the element of writing — the hidden is not merely known but entered.

**The Word for Hidden**

The verse says *ghā'ibah*, from the same root as *ghayb*, the unseen category the chapter discussed at verse 65. The connection is direct and the two verses should be read together.

There the point was a limit: nobody in the heavens or the earth knows the unseen except God, and the resurrection was named as the case in hand. Here the same category is described from the other side — everything in it is written down. Put together, the two verses describe a single arrangement. There is a domain creatures cannot reach, and it is not chaotic or unformed; it is registered. The ignorance announced in the earlier verse is therefore not a statement about the world being partly indeterminate. It is a statement about who has access to a record that exists.

**"A Perfect Record"**

The phrase is *kitābin mubīn*, a clear or manifest Book. Al-Ṭabarī identifies it with what the Qur'an elsewhere calls the Mother of the Book — the archetypal record mentioned in Āl ʿImrān and az-Zukhruf, and connected to the Preserved Tablet described at the end of al-Burūj.

The adjective is the chapter's own. Verse 1 called the Qur'an *al-kitāb al-mubīn*, the clear Book, and here the same two words name something else. The overlap is not confusion; it is a claim about relation. What is recited in Arabic and what is written in the heavenly register are given one description, and the reader who has come from the chapter's opening will hear the echo. A Book called clear at the start of the sūrah is matched by a Book called clear at its close, and the sūrah's whole argument runs between them.

**Why Writing Rather Than Knowing**

The verse could have said God knows everything hidden, which is what verse 74 said about hearts. It says instead that the hidden is in a record, and the shift from knowing to writing carries an implication.

Knowledge can be complete and still private. A written record is the form knowledge takes when it is going to be produced — entered, kept, and brought out later. The Qur'an uses this image throughout its account of the judgment, where deeds are described as recorded, books are handed to their owners, and nothing small or large is left uncounted. What this verse establishes is the existence of the register before any of that machinery is described. The accounting the mockers of verse 71 were demanding a date for is, on this claim, already documented; only the session has not been convened.

**The Argument Against a Private Life**

Draw the last four verses together and they make one case. God is bountiful to all. He knows what the chest conceals. Nothing hidden anywhere is outside a written record. And the reckoning may be closer than the questioners assume.

That sequence removes, step by step, the conditions a person needs in order to live one way in public and another in private. Thamūd's nine had precisely that assumption when they swore an oath at night and drafted an alibi; the chapter showed what became of it. What makes this section's version of the argument different is that it is addressed to people who have not yet done anything of the sort. The nine were shown after the fact. These verses are put to an audience before, which is the position every reader occupies. The chapter now turns from what is recorded to what has been revealed, and to the disputes it was sent to settle.

**Nothing Small Enough to Escape**

The negative construction is absolute: *mā min ghā'ibatin... illā fī kitābin mubīn* — there is no hidden thing except that it is in a clear Book. Arabic builds this with a double negative that admits no exceptions, and the Qur'an uses the same pattern elsewhere when it says no leaf falls without His knowledge.

The absoluteness is what gives the claim its practical weight. A record that captured only significant matters would leave everyone free to calculate what counted, and the calculation would be made in their own favour. What this verse describes leaves no room for that estimate. It also has a consoling side that the surrounding polemic can obscure. A wrong done in private to someone with no power to protest is, on this claim, entered — which is precisely what the report about the supplication of the oppressed asserted from the other direction. The same comprehensiveness that threatens the plotter is the guarantee held by the person nobody was listening to.

**Two Books at the Chapter's Two Ends**

An-Naml opens by calling the Qur'an *the clear Book* and closes this section by giving the same title to a heavenly register. Between them the chapter has used the word *kitāb* twice more: for the letter Sulaymān sent to Sheba, and for the source of the knowledge held by the man who brought the throne.

Four books, then, in one chapter: a recitation, a diplomatic dispatch, a source of power, and a record of everything hidden. The recurrence is not likely to be accidental in a text this carefully arranged. What the four share is that each is a fixed, written thing that produces an effect at a distance — a letter that changes a kingdom, a knowledge that moves a throne, a record that will be produced at the reckoning, and a recitation that has outlasted everyone who first heard it. The chapter's first sentence and its last theological claim are held together by one word.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:76

> Indeed, this Quran clarifies for the Children of Israel most of what they differ over.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Different Audience Named**

Every verse since the chapter's narrative section ended has addressed the Meccan idolaters. This one turns to a community that was not in the room: the Children of Israel, the inheritors of an earlier scripture.

The shift is abrupt and it widens the chapter's claim considerably. A book that answers Arabian paganism is a local corrective; a book that settles disputes internal to the older revealed traditions is claiming a different kind of authority. The verse makes that second claim explicitly, and the placement repays attention — immediately after the statement that everything hidden is in a clear record. A Book that resolves what people cannot resolve among themselves is being connected to a source that has the matter written down.

**The Verb Is About Narration**

The Arabic is *yaquṣṣu ʿalā banī isrāʾīl* — it recounts to the Children of Israel. The verb comes from the root behind *qaṣaṣ*, story or narrative, and it means to relate an account.

That choice of verb is more modest than a verb of adjudication would be, and more precise. The Qur'an does not say it issues rulings on their disputes; it says it recounts the matters to them. What settles a disagreement about what happened is a reliable account of what happened, and the Book presents itself here as supplying one. This is consistent with how the Qur'an handles the earlier traditions elsewhere, where it describes itself as confirming what came before and as a criterion for distinguishing within it, rather than as a replacement.

**What They Actually Differed Over**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī identify the disputes as internal quarrels among the Israelites, pursued to the point of mutual denunciation. Al-Rāzī specifies matters connected to what he calls the distortion of the Torah and the Gospel; al-Qurṭubī adds disagreements over the obligations of the divine law.

Both categories are recognisable. Communities with a long scriptural history generate disputes about the text itself — what belongs, how it was transmitted, what was altered — and disputes about practice, which obligations remain binding and which were provisional. The Qur'an engages both kinds in other chapters, correcting the account of ʿĪsā, addressing what was permitted and then forbidden to the Israelites, and adjudicating the status of Ibrāhīm. This verse is the general statement of which those are instances. A broader reading also circulated among the commentators, taking the disputes as questions about the nature of God, which the Qur'an answers universally.

**"Most of What They Differ Over" — Not All**

The limitation is explicit and it is unusual. *Akthara alladhī hum fīhi yakhtalifūn* — most of what they differ about, not the whole of it.

That qualification is easy to pass over and hard to explain away, since an expansive claim would have been rhetorically stronger. What it indicates is a selection principle: the Qur'an addresses the disputes that matter for the relationship between a person and God, and leaves alone the ones that do not. Questions of ritual detail, historical minutiae and legal particulars internal to another community's law are not the Book's subject. The restriction is consistent with how the Qur'an actually behaves. It says a great deal about the identity of ʿĪsā and nothing about the genealogies that occupy so much of the earlier scripture.

**Why the Children of Israel Appear at This Point**

The placement can look arbitrary in a chapter aimed at Mecca, and it is not. An-Naml has just spent fifty verses recounting the histories of Mūsā, Dāwūd, Sulaymān, Ṣāliḥ and Lūṭ — four of whom belong to the Israelite tradition and its prehistory.

The chapter has therefore been doing, in narrative, exactly what this verse describes in summary. It told the story of Mūsā at the fire, of Sulaymān and the queen, of the household of Lūṭ, and in each case it gave an account that differs in emphasis and sometimes in substance from what circulated elsewhere. Verse 9 of this chapter set the divine self-disclosure at the fire beside its biblical counterpart; verse 44 recorded a queen's submission that no other tradition preserves. This verse names what the chapter has been doing and identifies its second audience after the fact.

**A Claim That Could Be Checked**

The assertion is unusual for its period in one respect: it was verifiable by the people it concerned. Medina held substantial Jewish communities with scholars, texts and a long tradition of internal debate, and within a decade of this chapter's revelation the Prophet would be living among them.

A book claiming to resolve their disputes is inviting exactly the audience best equipped to test the claim, and the Qur'an keeps making it rather than quietly dropping it once contact was established. The later Medinan chapters engage the Israelite tradition at far greater length and in greater detail than the Meccan ones — the covenant, the calf, the sabbath, the prophets, the dietary restrictions. That is the behaviour of a text growing more confident about the claim under conditions where it could be contested, not less.

**Clarification Is Not the Same as Judgment**

The verse says the Qur'an recounts the disputed matters. It does not say the disputes end. Two verses later the chapter will state that the actual judgment between the parties belongs to God and happens in the Hereafter.

Reading the two together prevents a natural misunderstanding. A community holding a book that clarifies could easily conclude that it also holds the verdict, and is entitled to enforce it. The chapter separates the functions and gives the second one to nobody in this world. What is available now is a clear account; what is not available now is the decision about who was right. That distinction governs how the whole section should be read, and it is the reason the passage ends with an instruction to the Prophet to rely on God rather than an instruction to settle anything.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:77

> And it is truly a guide and mercy for the believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Returns to Its Opening Words**

This is almost the second verse of the sūrah repeated. There the Book was called *a guide and good news for the believers*; here it is *a guide and mercy for the believers*. One word has changed.

The repetition is structural. An-Naml opened by describing the Qur'an, spent seventy verses on narrative and argument, and now returns to the same description as the argument closes. What sits between the two statements is the demonstration — four peoples, six questions, a queen who submitted and three nations that did not. The claim made at the start is restated at the end, and everything in between was the case for it.

**Mercy in Place of Good News**

The substituted word repays attention. *Bushrā* is an announcement about the future: something good is coming. *Raḥmah* is a present quality of the thing itself: the Book is mercy, now, in the hands of whoever holds it.

The change tracks where the chapter has arrived. Verse 2 was addressed to people at the start of a text, being told what the Book promises. Verse 77 comes after the accounts of Thamūd's emptied houses, the rain that fell on Lūṭ's people, and Pharaoh's court refusing what it knew. A reader who has been through that material is not in a position to hear about good news in the abstract; what he has just been shown is what happens to communities without a guide. Against that, the Book's presence is itself the mercy. The pairing appears elsewhere in the Qur'an for both this revelation and the Torah, always in the same order.

**Guidance Named Twice**

The one element that does not change across the two verses is *hudan*, guidance. It is the first thing said about the Book at the chapter's opening and the first thing said about it here.

That constancy identifies the primary function. Everything else the Qur'an is called in this chapter — clear, a criterion between disputing parties, good news, mercy — describes an aspect or an effect. Guidance is the thing itself. The chapter's whole vocabulary has been organised around this word: the deniers of verse 4 wander blindly, Sheba's people are left unguided, the queen's test in verse 41 is framed as whether she will be guided, and verse 63 asked who guides travellers through the darkness of land and sea. A Book described as guidance is being placed in the same category as the stars a caravan steers by.

**Restricted to the Believers Again**

As in verse 2, the benefit is specified for *al-mu'minīn*. The Book is mercy for those who believe, which raises the same question the earlier verse raised and answers it the same way.

Nothing here says the Qur'an was sent only to believers, and the chapter has just addressed most of its recent verses to people who reject it. What the restriction names is who actually receives the benefit, not who is offered it. Medicine is described by whom it heals. The seventy-five verses between this statement and its twin have supplied the evidence: the same signs that guided some were called sorcery by others, the same interval that let Sheba's queen investigate was spent by Thamūd demanding their own destruction. The material was identical in each case. What differed was the reception.

**The Argument Has Come Full Circle**

Set verses 1–3 beside verses 76–77 and the chapter's frame is visible. It opens: these are the verses of the clear Book, a guide and good news for the believers, those who pray, give and are certain of the Hereafter. It closes this section: this Qur'an recounts to the Children of Israel most of what they dispute, and it is a guide and mercy for the believers.

Both ends describe the same object and name the same beneficiaries. What the closing pair adds is the second audience and the substituted attribute — the Book now addresses a scriptural community as well as a pagan one, and it is mercy as well as news. That widening is the chapter's net movement. It began with an Arabian audience and two letters of the alphabet, and it ends with a claim to adjudicate between traditions. The judgment itself, and who will deliver it, is the subject of the next verse.

**A Pairing Used of the Torah Too**

The two words appear together in several other chapters, and in some of those places they describe the Book given to Mūsā rather than the Qur'an. Al-Anʿām applies the phrase to the scripture of Mūsā twice in three verses; al-Aḥqāf does the same.

That shared description carries a claim about continuity. The Qur'an is not presenting itself as a new kind of thing but as the latest instance of a recognised kind, and it uses the identical vocabulary for its predecessor. The placement here reinforces the point, since the previous verse has just addressed the Children of Israel directly. A book that says it clarifies their disputes, and then describes itself with the same two words their own scripture is given, is making an argument about lineage rather than replacement. What follows in the next verse — that God will judge between the parties — assumes the same framework.

**What "Mercy" Means in Practice**

The abstraction can slide past without landing, so it helps to ask what the chapter has actually shown mercy to consist of. In an-Naml it is consistently information arriving in time to be acted on.

Sheba received a letter before an army. Lūṭ's household was extracted before the rain. Ṣāliḥ's people were given a warning they spent on plotting. In each case what was offered was notice — the interval between the situation and its consequence, filled with an account of what was happening. That is precisely what a book is. Calling the Qur'an mercy, in a chapter organised around those four histories, is not a pious epithet but a summary of the pattern the narratives have established. The mercy is that the message came first.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:78

> Your Lord will certainly judge between them by His justice, for He is the Almighty, All-Knowing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict Is Deferred, Not Cancelled**

The previous verses described a Book that recounts to a disputing community most of what they differ over. This one says who will actually settle the matter, and when: *your Lord* will judge between them.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both read the judgment as occurring in the Hereafter — God will decide there who was right. That timing is the substance of the verse. The Qur'an has just claimed to clarify the disputes, and it now distinguishes clarification from adjudication. Making the truth available and enforcing a verdict are two different acts, and only the first has happened. The disputes will continue in this world, and the Book's role is to inform them rather than to end them.

**Not the Prophet's Job**

Al-Rāzī draws the consequence explicitly: carrying out this judgment is not the Prophet's duty. The verse assigns it elsewhere, and the assignment is a limitation on the office.

That limit matters given what the chapter has been describing. A man told that his recitation settles the disputes of an ancient scriptural community might reasonably conclude that he is authorised to impose the settlement. The verse forecloses it in the same breath as the claim. He conveys; God judges. This is consistent with the instruction two verses earlier not to grieve over the refusers, and with the Qur'an's repeated statements that the messenger's responsibility is delivery rather than result. Nothing in an-Naml gives the Prophet a verdict to enforce.

**"By His Justice"**

The Arabic is *bi-ḥukmih*, by His judgment or His decision, which the translation renders as His justice. The phrase asserts that the criterion belongs to God rather than to any of the disputing parties.

That is the specific difficulty with long-running religious disputes: each side judges by a standard the other does not accept, and there is no neutral position available inside the argument. Communities that have quarrelled for centuries are not usually short of arguments; they are short of an arbiter both sides recognise. The verse names one, and locates the session after the point at which anyone can still be arguing. The Qur'an makes this promise in a dozen other places, telling the Prophet in several of them that God will inform people of what they differed about — the same formula, applied to Jews and Christians, to sects within them, and to the pagans of Mecca alike.

**Two Names Fitted to Judgment**

The verse closes with *al-ʿazīz al-ʿalīm*, the Almighty and the All-Knowing. The pairing is chosen for the task described, and the chapter has used the same technique before.

A judge needs two things: the knowledge to determine what actually happened, and the power to impose the finding. Human courts fail at one or the other constantly — they reach correct verdicts they cannot enforce, or enforce verdicts reached on incomplete information. The two names answer both failures in order. Note also the echo. These are the same two attributes named at the fire in verse 9, when God identified Himself to Mūsā as the Almighty, the All-Wise, and in verse 6, where the Qur'an was described as coming from the All-Wise, All-Knowing. The chapter's beginning and end are held together by the same small set of names.

**The Chapter's Fourth Deferral**

An-Naml has now declined four times to bring matters to a conclusion in this world. The Hour's timing was withheld at verse 65. The date of the reckoning was refused at verse 72. The Prophet was told not to grieve over the refusers at verse 70. Now the adjudication of religious dispute is placed beyond death.

Together these establish the position the chapter leaves its reader in. Nothing will be settled here; the evidence is available and the verdict is not. That is an uncomfortable arrangement and the chapter does not soften it. What it offers instead is the record of communities that were in exactly the same position and what they did with it — Sheba's queen, who investigated, and three nations that did not. The verse that follows tells the Prophet what to do while the judgment is pending.

**"Between Them" — Between Whom?**

The pronoun has no obvious antecedent in the immediate verse, and the commentators take it from the previous one: the Children of Israel and their internal disputes. The judgment is between the parties to that disagreement.

But the chapter has other disputing parties in view, and the phrasing is broad enough to hold them. The Meccans demanding a date, the believers enduring the demand, and the older scriptural communities are all in the passage's field of vision, and all of them are in disagreement about the same set of questions. The Qur'an's other statements of this promise are similarly unrestricted. What they have in common is a refusal to distribute the verdicts in advance. Nobody in the dispute is told they have already won it, which is an unusual position for a text written from inside one of the parties.

**Judgment as Disclosure**

Several of the parallel verses phrase the promise not as a sentence being passed but as information being released: God will inform them of what they differed about. That framing suits a chapter preoccupied with what is hidden and what is recorded.

Verse 75 said everything concealed is in a clear Book. This verse says the parties will be judged. Between them sits an implication the Qur'an makes explicit elsewhere — the judgment consists partly in the record being opened. Disputes about what really happened end when the account is produced, and the chapter has already established that an account exists. The people quarrelling are not waiting for an opinion. They are waiting for a document.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:79

> So put your trust in Allah, for you are surely upon the ˹Path of˺ clear truth.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Instruction That Follows a Deferral**

The previous verse placed the verdict in the Hereafter. This one says what to do in the meantime: *tawakkal ʿalā Allāh* — rely on God. The connective *fa* at the start makes it a conclusion drawn from what preceded.

The logic is worth tracing. If the judgment is not yours to deliver, and the timing is not yours to know, and the outcome is not yours to secure, then the appropriate posture is reliance. Trust here is not a mood cultivated for its own sake; it is what remains once the things outside a person's control have been correctly identified. The Qur'an commands *tawakkul* in many places, and it is almost always attached to a situation in which the addressee has done what he can and cannot do more.

**Trust Is Not Passivity**

The word could be misread as an instruction to stop working, and the chapter itself blocks that reading. Everything an-Naml admires involves action. Sulaymān verified a report, wrote a letter, and altered a throne. The queen consulted, tested and travelled. The hoopoe flew two thousand kilometres and came back with evidence.

None of that is quietism, and none of it is presented as a failure of trust. What the Qur'an pairs *tawakkul* with elsewhere makes the balance explicit: the Prophet is told to consult his companions on the matter and then, having resolved, to rely on God. Consultation first, decision second, reliance third — in that order. Reliance is what a person does about the part of the outcome that was never his, and it becomes an excuse only when it is applied to the part that was.

**"You Are Upon Clear Truth"**

The second half gives the ground for the first. *Innaka ʿalā al-ḥaqq al-mubīn* — you are upon the manifest truth. The preposition is spatial: he is *on* it, standing on it, as one stands on solid ground.

The commentators note that the phrase can also be rendered as following it or being based upon it, and all three senses share the image of support from below. That is the reason reliance is possible. A man is told to trust not because trusting is a virtue in itself but because what he is standing on will hold. The adjective is the chapter's own signature word, *mubīn*, clear — the same one applied to the Book in verse 1, to the sorcery charge in verse 13, to the favour Sulaymān named in verse 16, and to the record of hidden things in verse 75. Each time it marks something unambiguous.

**Consolation Addressed to a Specific Pressure**

This verse belongs with verse 70, where the Prophet was told not to grieve and not to feel constricted by plots. The two together form the chapter's treatment of his position, and they work differently.

Verse 70 named the feelings and set a limit on them. This one supplies what replaces them. A man told only to stop grieving has been given a prohibition; a man told to rely on God because he stands on clear truth has been given something to do instead. The order is also right. The chapter first records the distress, then argues at length — six questions, a command to travel and look, a statement about the record of hidden things — and only then instructs him to trust. The reliance is placed after the evidence rather than in place of it.

**What He Is Not Told**

Notice what the instruction omits. He is not promised success, not told his people will believe, not given a timeline, and not assured of protection. The chapter has already withheld all of that.

What he is given is an assessment of his position — you are on clear truth — and a direction for his confidence. That is a narrower consolation than a promise of victory and a more durable one, because it does not depend on how the following years turn out. The next verses will state plainly that some of the people he is addressing cannot be reached at all, comparing them to the dead and the deaf. Being told to rely on God immediately before being told that his audience includes people beyond persuasion is the sequence the chapter has chosen, and the two verses are meant to be held together.

**Reliance Recommended Across the Book**

The command appears in many other chapters and usually in the same position: after a difficulty has been described and before it has been resolved. Yaʿqūb sends his sons to Egypt with precautions and then says the decision rests with God. The believers under threat are told that God suffices them. Mūsā tells his people to rely if they have submitted.

The pattern in every case is the same three-part structure — a real danger, an action taken, and reliance for the remainder. None of these passages uses *tawakkul* to replace the action, and several of them describe the precaution in some detail before naming the trust. That consistency is the best available guide to what the word means in the Qur'an. It is the disposition a person holds toward the part of the outcome that was never within his reach, and the Book is careful to show the reach being used first.

**The Root of the Word**

*Tawakkul* comes from *w-k-l*, to appoint an agent or to entrust a matter to someone else. The related noun *wakīl* means a representative who acts on another's behalf, and it is used in ordinary Arabic for a legal proxy.

That commercial and legal background sharpens the sense. A man who appoints an agent has not stopped caring about the outcome; he has transferred the handling of it to someone better placed, and the transfer is deliberate and specific. What the Qur'an asks for is that structure applied to the matters no human agent could take on — the timing of the Hour, the response of a hostile city, the verdict on ancient disputes. The Prophet is being told to appoint the one party who actually has jurisdiction over those things, which is the same division of responsibility the previous verse established.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:80

> You certainly cannot make the dead hear ˹the truth˺. Nor can you make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs and walk away.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Limit Stated Without Softening**

The Prophet is told something blunt about his own effectiveness: there are people he cannot reach. Not people who are difficult, not people who need more time — people in the condition of the dead and the deaf.

This follows directly on the instruction to trust God, and the placement is deliberate. A man who has just been told to rely on God is now told what the reliance is for. He is not being promised that persistence will convert his audience; he is being informed that some of it is beyond his capacity, which is exactly the situation in which reliance becomes the only sane response. The Qur'an says the same thing to him elsewhere in almost identical words, and the repetition suggests it needed saying more than once.

**Two Images, Two Different Failures**

The verse gives two comparisons and they are not interchangeable. The dead cannot hear because they have no capacity left. The deaf described here cannot hear because they are deaf *and* walking away.

That second clause is doing the work. A deaf man standing still might yet be reached by gesture, by writing, by someone catching his eye. The image the verse constructs removes even that: the hearing is gone and the back is turned. What is being described is not a disability but a combination of incapacity and motion, and the motion is the part the person is responsible for. Al-Ṭabarī's reading of similar passages treats the deafness as acquired through refusal, which fits the chapter's account in verse 4 of hearts and eyes turned away after an initial rejection.

**Figurative, and the Figure Is Precise**

Nobody in Mecca was literally deaf in the sense meant, and the Qur'an is not describing a medical condition. It is describing what happens to the faculty of reception when it is repeatedly overruled.

The comparison works because the outcome is genuinely indistinguishable from the outside. A man who cannot hear and a man who will not hear behave identically in the presence of speech, and no amount of increased volume improves either case. That is the practical information the verse is giving the Prophet. His problem is not that he has been insufficiently clear — the chapter has called his message *mubīn* four times — and adjusting his delivery will not help. There is a category of listener for whom clarity is not the operative variable.

**The Speaker Is Not at Fault**

The verse's grammar keeps the responsibility away from the Prophet. He *cannot* make them hear; the incapacity is in them and the emphatic particle *innaka* marks the statement as a correction of something he may have believed.

That correction matters for how he was to understand the preceding years. A messenger watching his own clan refuse him has an obvious temptation to search his own conduct for the failure, and the chapter has already recorded him grieving. What this verse supplies is a diagnosis that locates the problem correctly. It is the same service Ṣāliḥ performed for his people in reverse, when they blamed their misfortunes on his presence and he told them they were being tested. Both cases involve someone attributing a cause wrongly and being corrected.

**Hearing as the Chapter's Central Faculty**

An-Naml has been about listening from its first pages. A prophet was called from a fire and heard a voice. A king was given the language of birds and overheard an ant. A hoopoe reported what he had seen and was heard out. A queen listened to a letter, then to her council, then to a correction about a floor.

Against all of that, this verse names the failure case. Everything the chapter admires is a variety of successful hearing, usually across a gap that should have made it impossible — a human hearing an insect, a king hearing a subordinate contradict him, a foreign monarch hearing a claim about her own error. The people described here are separated from a man speaking their own language in their own city, and the distance is unbridgeable. The chapter's next verse adds a third image to the two given here and then names the one group that can be reached.

**Why the Comparison Is to the Dead First**

The order of the two images is not arbitrary. The dead come first, and the dead are the more absolute case, which is an unusual way to build a comparison — the expected rhetorical move is to escalate.

Starting with the strongest image and then adding the qualified one has a specific effect. It establishes the outer limit immediately, so that the second image is read as a description of how a living person arrives at that condition. The deaf man walking away is the process; the dead man is where the process ends. The Qur'an uses death as a figure for unresponsiveness elsewhere and pairs it with life as a figure for faith, describing the believer as someone who was dead and whom God gave life. This verse names the state before that occurs, and the next verse will name the only people in whom the hearing works.

**A Statement About Audiences, Not About Fate**

One caution belongs here. The verse describes a condition, and nothing in it identifies which individuals are in that condition or declares the state permanent.

The chapter's own narrative forbids the harder reading. Sheba's queen worshipped the sun, led a nation that did so, and changed. The Meccan audience of this verse included men who fought the Prophet for years and then submitted. A verse taken as a roster of the damned would have been falsified within the lifetime of the people who first heard it. What it supplies instead is an explanation the Prophet needed at a particular moment: the absence of response is not always a measure of the speaker. Read as consolation it is accurate; read as prophecy about named persons it is neither what the words say nor what the history bore out.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:81

> Nor can you lead the blind out of their misguidance. You can make none hear ˹the truth˺ except those who believe in Our revelations, ˹fully˺ submitting ˹to Allah˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Image Completes the Set**

The previous verse gave two figures for unresponsiveness, the dead and the deaf. This one adds the blind, and with it the series is finished: no life, no hearing, no sight.

The three together cover every channel by which a message could arrive. The Qur'an uses this triad often, sometimes as a list — deaf, dumb, blind — and the repetition indicates a settled way of describing the condition. What makes the version here distinctive is the verb attached to the third term. The Prophet cannot *guide* the blind away from their error, which is a stronger claim than that they cannot see. A blind man can be led. The verse says this one cannot, because the blindness in question is not of the eye.

**Where the Blindness Is Located**

Another chapter states the point directly: it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts. That verse is the Qur'an's own gloss on the figure, and it removes any ambiguity about what organ is meant.

Relocating the blindness to the heart explains why guidance fails. A man blind in the eye still wants to reach the destination and will accept a hand; the problem is only in the seeing. A man blind in the heart has no sense that there is a destination, and the offer of a hand is unintelligible to him. This chapter established the mechanism at verse 4, where those who do not believe in the Hereafter have their deeds made attractive to them so that they wander blindly. The blindness there was an outcome of a prior refusal, not a starting condition, and the same is implied here.

**The Positive Clause Is the Point**

After three negations the verse turns: *in tusmiʿu illā man yu'minu bi-āyātinā* — you can make none hear except those who believe in Our signs. The whole passage has been building to this restriction, and it identifies who the Prophet is actually speaking to.

The formulation runs against ordinary expectation. One would think belief follows from hearing, not that hearing is available only to those who already believe. What the verse describes is a receptivity that precedes the argument and determines whether the argument can land at all. The chapter has shown both cases in narrative. Sheba's queen heard a letter from a foreign king, found it worth acting on, and eventually said she had wronged herself; Pharaoh's court saw nine signs, recognised them in their own souls, and called them sorcery. The material presented to each was not the deficient variable.

**"Submitting" as the Second Condition**

Belief is not the only qualifier. Those who hear are described as *muslimūn* — submitting, surrendered. The pairing of belief with submission adds something to the description.

A person can accept that a claim is true and decline to act on it, and the Qur'an is attentive to that gap. Pharaoh's court is the chapter's own example: they were certain in their souls and rejected all the same. Assent without surrender is precisely the position the chapter has been documenting in its opponents. The verse therefore names two things — the inner recognition and the practical yielding that follows — and describes hearing as belonging only to those in whom both are present. The word is also the one Sheba's queen used of herself, twice, when she declared her submission along with Sulaymān.

**Al-Rāzī on What This Does for the Prophet**

Al-Rāzī reads verses 80 and 81 as directed at the Prophet's own condition rather than at the deniers. His argument is that hope of getting something from people weakens one's ability to oppose them, and that absolving the Prophet of responsibility for their belief strengthens his capacity to declare the truth.

The observation is shrewd and it is about the psychology of a person who depends on an audience. A preacher who believes the outcome rests on his performance will begin, without deciding to, adjusting the message toward what his listeners can accept. Removing the outcome from his account removes the pressure that produces the adjustment. On al-Rāzī's reading the passage is not a verdict on Mecca at all but an instruction to the man addressing it, and the instruction to rely on God two verses earlier is what these verses exist to make possible.

**Sealed Hearts and the Question of Responsibility**

Al-Ṭabarī explains the passage in terms of sealing: those whose hearts God has sealed cannot be made to understand, and those God has made deaf cannot then be made to hear. That formulation raises the obvious difficulty about responsibility, and it deserves a direct answer rather than a deflection.
