The Qur'an's own sequencing supplies one. Wherever the sealing is described, it follows a refusal rather than preceding it. Al-Baqarah says God has sealed their hearts and then explains, in the surrounding verses, that they had first chosen error over guidance. This chapter's fourth verse works the same way: those who do not believe in the Hereafter have their deeds made attractive to them, with the disbelief stated first and the consequence second. The sealing describes what a settled refusal does to the faculty that refused, which is a familiar enough phenomenon at a human scale. Capacities that go unused close, and they close by a law rather than by an arbitrary decree.

**Why the Chapter Needs This Verse Here**

There is a structural reason this passage sits where it does, between the command to trust God and the description of the final signs. It marks the end of the persuasive section.

An-Naml has argued for eighty verses — with narrative, with questions about rain and rivers and the guidance of travellers, with an instruction to go and inspect the ruins. This verse states the limit of what argument accomplishes and identifies the audience in whom it works. Everything after this point in the chapter is no longer addressed to the possibility of convincing the unwilling. It describes what happens at the end, and the shift in the chapter's mode is announced here.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:82

> And when the decree ˹of the Hour˺ comes to pass against them, We will bring forth for them a beast from the earth, telling them that the people had no sure faith in Our revelations.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sign at the End of the Argument**

The chapter has spent a long section saying that some people cannot be reached by speech. This verse describes what comes when the time for speech has run out: a creature brought out of the earth to state what the situation is.

The placement is exact. Verses 80 and 81 established that the deaf and the blind cannot be made to hear; this verse says that at the end a sign will be produced which does not depend on being heard. It marks and declares rather than persuades. What the chapter has been arguing — that the interval is for deciding and the decision must be made inside it — reaches its terminus here, at the point where the evidence becomes unavoidable and therefore useless.

**"When the Word Comes Upon Them"**

The phrase is *idhā waqaʿa al-qawlu ʿalayhim*, and al-Ṭabarī connects it to similar language elsewhere about a word or decree falling due. The sense is of a sentence that has been pronounced and now takes effect.

Others among the commentators read it as the point at which the divine wrath becomes necessary against them. Both readings agree on the structure: something already determined arrives at its appointed moment. The Qur'an uses the same construction in several chapters, always for the transition from warning to execution. What the verse adds to that familiar formula is the creature, which appears in the Qur'an only here and which the tradition identifies as one of the portents preceding the end.

**What "Beast" Means**

*Dābbah* is a broad word. Its root sense covers anything that moves along the ground, and the Qur'an uses it for animals generally, for creatures in the heavens and the earth, and in one place for a worm. It can mean a riding animal, an insect, or a sea creature.

The vagueness is in the Arabic itself, not in the translation. What the verse specifies is only that it is brought out *from the earth* and that it addresses people about the state of their belief. Everything beyond that — size, shape, place of emergence — comes from reports rather than from the text, and the reports do not agree with each other. This is one of the few places where the Qur'an introduces a concrete future event and then declines to describe it, and the restraint is itself a datum.

**The Commentators' Descriptions and al-Rāzī's Caution**

The tafsīr literature carries a large and colourful body of material. Some give the creature a length of sixty or seventy cubits. Others say it emerges carrying the staff of Mūsā and the seal of Sulaymān, marking believers with the staff and stamping deniers with the seal so that each is recognisable on sight. One composite description assembles it from the parts of a dozen animals — a bull's head, an elephant's trunk, a stag's horns, a camel's legs. There is disagreement, too, about where it appears, with Mecca, Minā, Yemen and the desert all named.

Al-Rāzī's response to this abundance is the soundest position available. Since the Qur'an gives no indication of any of these details, a report should be accepted only so far as it can be authenticated from the Prophet, and otherwise disregarded. The methodological point is worth keeping: the density of a tradition is not evidence for it, and a text that says little about something was probably not intending to say more. Applying his test resolves the question cleanly. There *is* authenticated material about this creature, and it is strikingly thin — the report naming it among three signs after which faith no longer benefits is sound, recorded from Abū Hurayrah at **Jāmiʿ at-Tirmidhī 3072** and graded *ṣaḥīḥ*, and it says nothing whatever about the creature's appearance. The cubits, the composite anatomy, the staff and the seal and the marked faces belong to a separate stratum, traceable largely to ath-Thaʿlabī and the storytellers, with no prophetic chain behind any of it. What survives the test is a sign, a function, and an announcement.

**A Reading Difference in the Verb**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note an alternative vocalisation. Read as *tukallimuhum* the creature speaks to them; read as *taklimuhum* it wounds them. The consonantal text supports either.

The variant matters more than it looks. On the first reading the event is an announcement — the creature declares that people had no certainty about the signs. On the second it is a physical marking, which fits the reports about the staff and the seal. Most commentators, including az-Zamakhsharī, take the declaration as the creature's own speech. The chapter's own concerns favour that reading too, since an-Naml is a sūrah in which animals speak and are understood: an ant warns her colony, a hoopoe delivers a report, and the last creature in the chapter announces a verdict.

**The Content of the Announcement**

What the creature says is short and specific: *anna al-nāsa kānū bi-āyātinā lā yūqinūn* — that people had no certainty about Our signs. Not that they disbelieved, and not that they disobeyed. That they were not certain.

The word is *yūqinūn*, from *yaqīn*, and it takes the chapter back to its third verse, where the believers were described as being certain about the Hereafter. Certainty is the quality the sūrah named first and returns to last. It also connects to Pharaoh's court in verse 14, whose souls were certain while their tongues denied — the one case in the chapter where certainty existed and made no difference. The final sign in the Qur'anic chapter about signs announces that the problem was never the availability of evidence.

**A Threshold After Which Belief Does Not Count**

A well-known report names three events after which no soul's faith will benefit it if it did not believe before: the sun rising from the west, the appearance of the Dajjāl, and the beast of the earth. The creature of this verse is one of the three.

That classification tells us what the sign is for. It does not open a further opportunity; it closes the one that was open. The logic matches what the chapter has been saying about certainty. Belief under conditions that make disbelief impossible is not the thing the Qur'an has been asking for, and verse 3 defined the believers as those who are certain about the Hereafter while it remains unseen. When the evidence becomes overwhelming the quality that was being tested no longer exists to be tested, which is why the arrival of this sign functions as a terminus rather than as a final appeal.

**A Sign for a Time Without Correction**

One report cited by al-Ṭabarī connects the creature's emergence to a condition of the community rather than to a date: it comes when no one any longer enjoins what is right and forbids what is wrong.

That is a diagnostic rather than a calendar, and it fits the verse's refusal to give a time. What the condition describes is a society in which the ordinary internal mechanism of correction has stopped operating — not a society full of wrongdoing, which every society is, but one in which nobody speaks against it. The chapter has shown the mechanism working elsewhere. An ant warned her colony, a hoopoe contradicted a king, a councillor advised a queen, and Ṣāliḥ addressed his people directly. The sign arrives when nothing of that kind is left, which is to say when speech has already failed.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:83

> ˹Watch for˺ the Day We will gather from every faith-community a group of those who denied Our revelations, and they will be driven in ranks.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Selection, Not a Sweep**

The verse describes a gathering, and the striking thing about it is how narrow the selection is. From every community a *fawj* is assembled — a contingent, a party — consisting of those who denied the signs.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes the resurrection as universal, with everyone raised and everyone present. What this verse depicts is something different and more specific: a delegation drawn out of each nation for a particular purpose. The scene that follows in the next verse makes the purpose clear. They are brought forward to be questioned, one group at a time, and the questioning is about what they did with the evidence they were given. This is a procedure, not a stampede.

**"From Every Community"**

*Min kulli ummah* — from every nation. No community is exempt from producing such a contingent, and the phrase includes the ones that received prophets and the ones the chapter has just been discussing.

That universality does two things. It rules out the idea that denial was a peculiarity of Arabian paganism, which would let the Meccan audience treat the whole passage as a description of themselves and nobody else. It also rules out the inverse comfort, that membership in a community with a scripture provides cover. The previous verses addressed the Children of Israel and their disputes; this one says that every nation, the scriptural ones included, contributes to the group being assembled. The chapter has already made the point in narrative by pairing Thamūd with Lūṭ's people — one sent a prophet from among them, one visited by messengers — and giving both the same outcome.

**"Driven in Ranks"**

The verb is *yūzaʿūn*, and readers of this chapter have met it before. It was used in verse 17 for Sulaymān's hosts of jinn, men and birds, who were marshalled into ordered ranks before him.

Al-Ṭabarī glosses it in both places as corralling or grouping together, holding the front back so the rear can catch up. The reuse of a rare word across one chapter is unlikely to be accidental, and the effect is a deliberate parallel with the difference left visible. Sulaymān's ranks were an army assembled in glory, marching under a king who thanked God for his share of all things. These ranks are assembled for interrogation. The same formation, the same word, and two entirely different meanings depending on who is doing the marshalling and where the column is headed.

**Order as the Characteristic of the Scene**

The image is not chaotic. Nothing in the verse suggests panic or confusion; the deniers are collected, sorted by community, and moved in formation. That orderliness is worth pausing on.

Descriptions of judgment in popular imagination tend toward tumult, and the Qur'an does use such imagery elsewhere — mountains moved, seas boiling, people scattered like moths. This verse does the opposite. It depicts an administrative process conducted with precision, which is in keeping with what the chapter has just said about a record in which everything hidden is written. A proceeding based on a complete document does not need to be frantic. The calm of the description is itself an argument about the quality of the information behind it.

**"Watch for the Day"**

The translation supplies an imperative that the Arabic carries through its particle: *wa yawma naḥshuru* — and the Day We gather. The construction points forward to an appointed time and asks the hearer to hold it in view.

That is what the chapter has been asking since verse 3, where the believers were identified as those certain about the Hereafter. Between then and now the sūrah has described four nations that did not hold the day in view, an idolatrous queen who came to, and a Meccan audience demanding its date. This verse gives them a picture of the date they asked about. They wanted to know when; they are shown what, and the what is a scene in which they are among those being counted out of their own nation and put in line.

**A Scene Built for the People Who Demanded a Date**

Twelve verses earlier the Meccans asked when the promise would arrive. The chapter has answered them in stages: perhaps part of it is close behind you, then a statement about the record of hidden things, and now a picture of the occasion itself.

What the sequence does is replace the question. They wanted a position on a calendar, which the chapter has consistently refused to supply. What they are given is a description of their own place in the event, which is information of a more useful kind. A man told the date of a trial learns something about his schedule; a man shown himself in the dock learns something about his situation. The chapter has used this substitution before, answering the demand for a proof with a demand for theirs, and answering the request for timing with a statement about nearness.

**The Contingent Is Drawn From Their Own People**

There is a detail in the phrasing that would have landed sharply on the first audience. The group is taken *from* a community — pulled out of it, separated from the rest of their nation.

Tribal Mecca understood identity as collective. A man's standing, protection and fate ran with his clan, and the worst thing that could happen to him was to be cut off from it. The chapter has already played on this at verse 56, where Lūṭ's opponents proposed expelling his household from the town, and at verse 57, where the rescue was of a household as a unit. This verse describes a separation that runs the other way and that no clan can prevent. Belonging, which was the ultimate security in that society, does not determine which contingent a person is counted into.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:84

> When they ˹finally˺ come before their Lord, He will ask ˹them˺, “Did you deny My revelations without ˹even˺ comprehending them? Or what ˹exactly˺ did you do?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Charge Is Not Denial**

The question put to the assembled contingents is precise, and it is easy to misread. They are not asked why they denied. They are asked whether they denied *without encompassing the signs in knowledge*.

The grammatical weight falls on the second clause. Denial after investigation would be a different charge and would meet a different question; what is put to them is that they rejected without having examined. Al-Ṭabarī takes the phrase to mean they did not know or recognise the signs as they deserved to be known. The offence being identified is therefore procedural before it is doctrinal: a verdict reached without the evidence being looked at.

**"Encompassed in Knowledge"**

The verb is *aḥāṭa*, to encompass or surround, and this chapter has used it once before at a memorable moment. The hoopoe returned to Sulaymān and said *aḥaṭtu bi-mā lam tuḥiṭ bih* — I have encompassed what you have not encompassed.

That earlier use sets the standard the question here applies. A bird flew a long distance, observed a nation, identified its religion and its ruler, and returned with a report it described as certain news. The chapter presents that as the model of how a claim is established. Against it, the contingents at the judgment are asked whether they encompassed anything at all before rejecting. The word is the same and the comparison is unflattering. A hoopoe did the work; they did not.

**The Second Question**

After the charge comes an odd addition: *ammā dhā kuntum taʿmalūn* — or what was it that you used to do? The question is left open, and its openness is the point.

It functions as an invitation to supply an alternative account. If you did not reject out of ignorance, then tell us what you were doing. This is how a competent interrogation proceeds — the charge is stated and the accused is given room to answer it. What makes the moment devastating is not the severity of the question but its fairness. Nobody is being convicted by assertion. They are being asked to describe their own conduct, and the following verse records that they cannot speak.

**A Question That Assumes an Answer Existed**

There is something conditional in the phrasing that is worth drawing out. The question implies that encompassing the signs in knowledge *was* possible — that had they investigated, they would have found something.

The chapter has supported that implication throughout. Sheba's queen investigated and arrived somewhere. Pharaoh's court examined the nine signs closely enough to be certain of them in their souls. The Meccans were told to travel and look at the ruins, an instruction that assumes the looking would be informative. None of the chapter's evidence is presented as accessible only to the elect. The question at the judgment is answerable only because the inquiry was available, and the failure it names is a failure to use what was there.

**Speech Restored at the Worst Moment**

For the whole of this passage the deniers have been described as unable to hear. Now they are addressed directly by God and asked a question that requires an answer.

The reversal is pointed. In the world they had hearing they did not use; at the judgment they are given a hearing they cannot use, since what is asked of them is a defence they do not have. The chapter's account of the queen of Sheba ran the other way: she was spoken to by a letter, replied, was spoken to again, and ended by speaking for herself in the words of submission. Dialogue in an-Naml is consistently the mark of those who are getting somewhere. This exchange has one speaker and, as the next verse states, no reply.

**The Question Presumes a Standard of Inquiry**

Read the charge closely and it contains a claim about what people owe to evidence. To reject a thing is permissible; to reject it without having encompassed it in knowledge is what is being put to them as an offence.

That standard is high and it is applied at the judgment to ordinary people, not to scholars. The chapter has already illustrated what meeting it looks like, and none of the examples involve special training. Sheba's queen consulted her council, sent a probe, and travelled to see for herself. The hoopoe flew and observed. Ṣāliḥ's people were asked to wait and did not. What these have in common is the willingness to spend effort before reaching a conclusion, which is a disposition rather than a qualification. The question at the judgment assumes the disposition was available to everyone being asked.

**No Excuse Is Pre-empted**

Something the verse does not do is anticipate the defence. It does not say they had no excuse, does not list the warnings they received, and does not rehearse the evidence against them.

The restraint is consistent with the procedural character of the scene. A charge is put, an opening is left for a reply, and the text waits. Compare how the chapter handled Thamūd's conspirators: the plot was described, the counter-plot named, and the outcome given, with no speech assigned to the nine at all. Here the accused are addressed and given the floor. The difference is that this is the judgment rather than the world, and the judgment is where the parties are heard. That the hearing produces nothing is the subject of the verse that follows.

**Deniers, Not Disbelievers**

The charge names an act rather than an identity: *kadhdhabtum*, you denied, from a verb meaning to call something a lie. The assembled group was described in the previous verse by the same root.

This matters for how the passage should be read. The Qur'an has words for disbelief and for associating partners with God, and it does not use them here. What is under examination is a specific thing people did with specific evidence — they were shown signs and pronounced them false. The chapter's opponents did this in so many words. Pharaoh's court called the nine signs manifest sorcery; the Meccans called the account of the Hereafter tales of the ancients. The judgment scene asks about that pronouncement and about the inquiry that should have preceded it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:85

> And the decree ˹of torment˺ will be justified against them for their wrongdoing, leaving them speechless.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Falls After the Question**

Verse 84 put a question to the assembled deniers. This verse records what follows: the word falls against them, and they do not speak.

The order of events is the substance of the passage. The charge was stated, an opportunity to answer was given, and only then does the decree take effect. That sequence is what distinguishes a judgment from a punishment. The Qur'an could have described the outcome without the intervening question and lost nothing in severity; including it establishes that the verdict was reached through a procedure in which the accused were heard. What the verse reports is that they had nothing to say when the moment came.

**"For Their Having Done Wrong"**

The cause is named: *bimā ẓalamū*, because they did wrong. The chapter used this exact phrase once before, at verse 52, where the emptied houses of Thamūd stood as a consequence of the same thing.

The repetition ties the two scenes together. What happened to a nation in history and what happens to the contingents at the judgment are given the same explanation in the same words, which is the chapter's method throughout — the pattern visible in the ruins is the pattern that operates at the end. Al-Ṭabarī identifies the wrong specifically as their denial of the truth and rejection of what was revealed. *Ẓulm* in Qur'anic usage means putting something where it does not belong, and the chapter has already applied it to the self: Sulaymān's confession and the queen's both used it of themselves.

**They Do Not Speak**

*Fa-hum lā yanṭiqūn* — and they do not speak. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as an inability to defend themselves: nothing remains that could be offered as a case.

The silence is not imposed by force. Nothing in the verse describes them being prevented; the natural reading is that the question of verse 84 admitted no answer. A man asked whether he condemned something without examining it, when he did, has only two options, and confession is not going to help him. The verse describes the position where argument has run out. Elsewhere the Qur'an depicts the deniers at the judgment talking a great deal — blaming their leaders, asking to be returned, disputing among themselves. Here, at the point where the specific charge is put, there is nothing.

**The Verb Chosen for the Silence**

*Nuṭq* is not the general word for speech. Its sense is articulate utterance, the forming of intelligible speech, and the chapter opened one of its most famous scenes with the related noun.

When Sulaymān said he had been taught *manṭiq al-ṭayr*, the speech of birds, it was this root. The chapter that began with a king given the power to understand articulate utterance in creatures that supposedly have none ends by describing human beings who have lost it. An ant addressed her colony in intelligible speech and was understood; a bird made a report and was believed; and the deniers, at the scene where speech would finally matter, produce none. The word is rare enough in the Qur'an that the echo across a single chapter is unlikely to be coincidence.

**Silence as Part of the Penalty**

Being unable to speak to God is treated in the Qur'an as a punishment in its own right, not merely as a symptom of having no defence. Other passages state plainly that God will not speak to certain people on the Day of Resurrection, and the commentators discuss this as a form of deprivation.

What is being withheld, on that reading, is relation. The whole of an-Naml has been a chapter of addresses: God speaking from a fire, a prophet praying, a queen writing to her council, a letter read aloud, six questions put to an audience. Every one of those is an exchange, and the chapter's positive figures are the ones who answer. To be present before God and unable to produce a word is to be outside the only conversation that the chapter has treated as mattering. The next verse turns back to the living and to a sign they can still consider.

**The Decree Is Described as Deserved**

The grammar attaches the falling word to a cause, and the causal particle is doing more than transition. The sentence is constructed so that the penalty and its ground arrive in the same clause.

That construction appears throughout the chapter's accounts of destroyed nations and it is always the same shape: an outcome, then the reason, stated as a fact about what the people did rather than as a justification offered on God's behalf. Thamūd's houses were emptied because they wronged. Lūṭ's people received a rain described as evil for those who had been warned. Nothing in any of these passages argues that the punishment was fair; the fairness is presented as evident from the pairing. The chapter's confidence on this point is worth registering, since a text anxious about the charge of arbitrariness would have argued rather than asserted.

**What the Scene Leaves the Living Reader**

This is the last verse of the judgment sequence, and the chapter does not linger over the punishment itself. There is no description of what happens next — no fire, no chains, no duration. The account stops at the silence.

That is a choice, and the Qur'an is capable of the other approach; other chapters describe the outcome in considerable physical detail. Ending here keeps the emphasis where the passage has had it from verse 83: on a procedure, a question, and an answer that was not available. The reader is left holding the question rather than the penalty, and the question — did you reject without examining — is one he can still do something about. The verse immediately following turns to the night and the day, which is evidence available to anyone reading, tonight.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:86

> Do they not see that We made the night for them to rest in and the day bright? Surely in this are signs for those who believe.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Back to the Ordinary After the Judgment**

Three verses have described a gathering, an interrogation and a silence. This one asks about last night. The descent in scale is deliberate and it is the chapter's habit.

An-Naml keeps returning from the extraordinary to the available. It showed a throne moved in the blink of an eye and then asked who sends the winds. It described the destruction of Thamūd and then pointed at their houses, still standing on the trade road. The pattern here is the same: after the terror of the judgment scene, the evidence offered is the alternation a person has experienced every day of his life and never once had to seek out.

**The Question Form Returns**

*A lam yaraw* — have they not seen? The chapter used a sequence of such questions in verses 60 to 64, each beginning with the same challenge and each ending by asking whether there is a god besides God.

That series stopped at verse 64 and the chapter turned to the Hour, the mockers, the record and the judgment. Now the interrogative returns, which signals that the argument from creation has not been abandoned but resumed. What has changed is the ending. The earlier questions closed by asking whether another god could do this; this one closes by saying there are signs here for people who believe. The rhetorical pressure has shifted from disputing about deities to identifying who actually reads the evidence.

**Night Described by Its Purpose**

The verse does not say God made the night dark. It says He made it *li-yaskunū fīh* — for them to be still in, to rest in. The night is defined by what it is for rather than by what it is.

That framing is characteristic of how the Qur'an handles natural phenomena, and it makes a claim that goes beyond description. Darkness is a physical fact; rest is a fit between the fact and a creature's need. The word *sakan* and its relatives carry the sense of settling, dwelling, and quiet — the Qur'an uses the same root for the tranquillity between spouses and for a dwelling place. What the verse asserts is that the planet's rotation and the human requirement for sleep are matched, and that the match is the sign rather than either element alone.

**Day Described the Same Way**

The second half is parallel: the day *mubṣiran*, which the translation renders as bright. The Arabic is more pointed than that. The form is active — the day is made *seeing*, or more exactly made such that seeing happens in it.

Arabic grammarians treat this as attributing to the time what belongs to the people in it, a recognised figure. The effect is to put the emphasis again on fit rather than on fact. Light is not described as a property of the sky; it is described in terms of the faculty it serves. Set the two halves together and the verse has named a physical alternation and, for each phase, the human capacity it answers. The argument is not that the world is impressive. It is that the world and its inhabitants appear to have been made for each other.

**Why This Evidence Is Hard to Argue With**

There is a reason the Qur'an returns to the day and night so often, in a dozen chapters and in several forms — the passing of one into the other, their alternation, the withdrawal of daylight. The evidence is inexhaustible because nobody can stop receiving it.

A man can avoid a ruined city and decline to travel. He can dismiss a report, refuse a letter, and walk away from a speaker. He cannot avoid the night. Whatever position he takes about the chapter's claims, he will sleep tonight and wake tomorrow, and the sign will have been delivered again without his cooperation. That is why the chapter places this verse after the judgment scene rather than before it: the reader who has just been asked whether he examined the signs is given one he cannot claim not to have encountered.

**"For a People Who Believe"**

The closing restriction is the same one the chapter has used repeatedly. The signs are for those who believe — not because the phenomenon is hidden from anyone else, but because it registers as a sign only for those prepared to read it that way.

This is the third time the chapter has attached a qualifier of this kind to its evidence. Verse 52 said the ruins are a sign for a people who know. Verse 86 says the night and day are signs for a people who believe. The recurring structure carries the chapter's central claim about evidence: the material is public and the reading is not automatic. Every person in Mecca had seen the ruins and every one of them had slept through a night. The chapter has never argued that the deniers were short of data.

**A Sign With No Rarity Value**

One more feature of this choice of evidence deserves attention. It is completely unremarkable, and the chapter has just spent seventy verses on the remarkable — a talking ant, a throne transported, a staff that moved, a nation destroyed overnight.

Choosing the daily alternation immediately after that material makes a point about what miracles are for. The extraordinary events in an-Naml all served to get somebody's attention at a particular moment; none of them is offered to the chapter's readers as evidence they can inspect. What is offered instead is the most ordinary thing there is. A text that thought wonders were the real argument would not have ended its case with the sunset.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:87

> And ˹beware of˺ the Day the Trumpet will be blown, and all those in the heavens and all those on the earth will be horrified ˹to the point of death˺, except those Allah wills ˹to spare˺. And all will come before Him, fully humbled.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sound That Ends the World**

The trumpet appears in many chapters of the Qur'an as the signal that opens the final sequence. Tradition identifies the one who blows it as the angel Isrāfīl, and the Book treats the blast as the hinge between this order and what follows.

Commentators divide over how many blasts there are. Al-Ṭabarī transmits an account of three — this terrifying one, a second causing all to swoon, and a third bringing the resurrection. Al-Qurṭubī reports the view that there are only two, on the grounds that the blast described here is the same as the one in az-Zumar, which causes those in the heavens and the earth to swoon. The disagreement rests on whether two nearly identical descriptions in different chapters name one event or two, and neither side can settle it from the text alone.

**What the Trumpet Might Be**

Most take *ṣūr* to mean a horn, and the word is used that way in Arabic. Al-Rāzī records two other possibilities and both are worth hearing.

On one, the image is military: a trumpeter mustering an army, with the raising of the dead pictured as troops being called into formation. That reading fits this chapter unusually well, since the previous judgment scene described the deniers being driven in ranks using the word the sūrah applied to Sulaymān's marshalled hosts. On the other, *ṣūr* is read as *ṣuwar*, the plural of *ṣūrah*, form — so that what is blown is spirit into bodies. Al-Rāzī reports both without deciding, and the second is the more speculative, depending as it does on an alternative vocalisation.

**"Horrified" — and an Alternative Sense**

The verb is *faziʿa*, ordinarily to be terrified or startled. Al-Qurṭubī notes that it can also mean to respond to a summons with haste, which would describe the resurrected coming quickly out of the graves rather than cowering.

The two senses are not as far apart as they look. Both describe a violent reaction to a sudden sound; they differ over whether the reaction is recoil or movement toward. The verse's second half may favour the first, since those who come to God are described as humbled rather than eager. But the ambiguity is genuine, and a word that means both fright and immediate response is well chosen for an event that is simultaneously a catastrophe and a summons.

**The Exception**

*Illā man shā'a Allāh* — except whom God wills. The verse names an exempt group and does not identify it, and the commentators have filled the gap in several ways.

Al-Ṭabarī reports the view that they are those slain in God's path, whom another chapter describes as alive with their Lord and provided for, and therefore already beyond the terror that strikes the living. Al-Qurṭubī records the view that certain angels are meant, sometimes specified as Jibrīl, Mīkāʾīl, Isrāfīl and the Angel of Death; some extend the exemption to prophets and the saints. None of these is stated in the verse, and the disagreement is of a particular kind. These are not competing reports from the Prophet; they are inferences, each reasoning from some other verse to a group that ought to qualify. The martyr identification is the strongest of them because it argues from scripture — those slain in God's path are elsewhere described as already alive with their Lord, so the terror that strikes the living would not reach them. That is an argument, not a transmission, and it should be received as one. The chapter itself resolves the question differently and better two verses later, by naming a criterion instead of a list. What the text commits to is only that the terror is not universal and that the exemption is God's to grant.

**"All Will Come Before Him, Fully Humbled"**

The final clause changes the register. After the blast and the terror comes an arrival, and everyone who arrives is described by one word: *dākhirīn*, abased, humbled, small.

The Qur'an makes a related point elsewhere by saying each of them will come to Him on the Day of Resurrection alone. Both descriptions strip away what people arrive with. The chapter has been full of persons defined by their retinues — a king with hosts of jinn, men and birds; a queen with a council and an army; a Meccan aristocracy with clans behind it. The verse describes a moment in which none of that accompanies anyone. The one figure in an-Naml who arrived at this posture voluntarily was Sheba's queen, who came a great distance and said she had wronged herself.

**Terror as the Qur'an Describes It**

Other chapters fill in what this Day does to people, and the descriptions are consistently about the severing of attachments. A guilty man would ransom himself with his children. Hearts tremble. A man flees from his brother.

None of these is about physical pain; each is about the collapse of the relationships a person's life was built on. That emphasis clarifies what the terror consists of. It is not primarily fear of a penalty, which has not yet been assigned at this point in the sequence, but the exposure of a self with nothing left attached to it. An-Naml has consistently treated relation as the thing that matters — who is heard, who answers, who is spoken to. The trumpet verse describes the moment when every relation except one has ended.

**A Warning Placed After an Invitation**

Notice the arrangement. The previous verse pointed at the night and the day and called them signs. This one describes the blast. The chapter alternates between the gentle evidence and the severe outcome rather than grouping them.

That alternation runs through the whole sūrah. Verse 2 promised guidance and good news, verse 4 described those who wander blindly. Verse 73 called God bountiful, verse 74 said He knows what is concealed. Here the pattern appears one last time, and in the same order: the invitation first, the warning second. What follows in the next verses is the return, with the mountains moving and the deeds weighed, before the chapter ends on a command to recite.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:88

> Now you see the mountains, thinking they are firmly fixed, but they are travelling ˹just˺ like clouds. ˹That is˺ the design of Allah, Who has perfected everything. Surely He is All-Aware of what you do.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Most Contested Verse**

Few verses in an-Naml have produced more disagreement, and the disagreement is not about the words but about when the scene takes place. Is the mountains' movement a present fact we fail to perceive, or an event at the end of the world?

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both report the first reading: the mountains move now, and we do not see it because large objects viewed from a distance appear stationary even when travelling fast. Al-Rāzī also records the second, connecting the verse to the many passages describing mountains scattered, levelled or turned to carded wool at the Hour. The surrounding verses support the eschatological reading, since the trumpet has just sounded. The verb tense and the address to a present "you" support the other. Both camps are working from the same four words.

**The Argument From Perception**

The first reading deserves to be taken seriously on its own terms rather than treated as a curiosity. Its logic is about the reliability of the senses, and the commentators state it plainly: a very large thing seen from very far away looks still even when it is moving quickly.

That is a genuine observation, available to anyone who has watched a ship on the horizon or a cloud bank that seems fixed until one looks away and back. The verse builds the illusion into its own wording — *taḥsabuhā jāmidah*, you *suppose* they are fixed. The supposition is named as a supposition. What the reading then proposes is a general lesson about inference: the most solid-seeming thing in a person's world may be in motion, and his confidence that it is not is a product of his vantage point rather than of the thing itself.

**The Argument From Context**

The competing reading has the stronger contextual case. The previous verse describes the trumpet and the terror; the following verses describe the weighing of deeds. A verse about geology sitting between them would be an interruption.

On this reading the mountains are seen to move at the Hour, as many other chapters describe, and the present-tense verb functions as a vivid future — a common enough device in Arabic and in the Qur'an particularly, where scenes of the judgment are frequently narrated as though unfolding now. The word *tamurru*, passing, and the comparison to clouds match the imagery of those other passages closely. Nothing forces a choice between the two readings, and the classical commentators generally reported both.

**A Third Reading: The World Itself**

Al-Qurṭubī records a further possibility that reframes the image. The passing away may refer to the earth as a whole — the world, believed to be solid, is in fact as ephemeral as a cloud.

This reading treats the mountains as the Qur'an's chosen instance of apparent permanence rather than as its subject. They are the obvious candidate: an-Naml itself, at verse 61, described God as placing firm mountains on the earth, and the word used there, *rawāsī*, means anchors. Naming them now as clouds inverts the chapter's own earlier image. What was set down to stabilise is shown to be in motion, and if the anchors are moving, nothing in the visible world is what it appeared.

**Three Allegorical Readings, and Whose They Are**

Al-Qurṭubī closes his treatment by reporting, on the authority of al-Māwardī, that the verse has been taken as a similitude, and he gives three versions of what it is a similitude for. The attributions can be stated precisely.

The first is Sahl ibn ʿAbdullāh's: the world itself is the mountain, and the man looking at it supposes it fixed while it is taking its share of passing away, like a cloud. The second applies the image to faith — you suppose it settled in the heart while its work is ascending. The third takes it of the soul at death, departing the body and rising toward the Throne. All three use the perceptual argument in the same way, by insisting that the appearance of stillness is a statement about the observer rather than the observed, and the first is the most exact use of it. A popular anecdote attaches this verse to Junayd al-Baghdādī, who is said to have recited it when asked why he showed none of the visible ecstasies of his contemporaries. The reading is apt — a mountain in motion looks still, and composure is no evidence of stillness — but the story is not in al-Qurṭubī's discussion and does not belong to this chain. It circulates in the later Sufi literature and should not be attributed to the commentary.

**"Who Has Perfected Everything"**

The verb is *atqana*. Al-Ṭabarī glosses it as tying down or making firm; al-Rāzī takes it in this context as God tying things together according to His wisdom.

Both senses turn on fastening, and the word's placement is deliberate. The sentence has just said that the firmest things are moving, and it immediately describes God as the one who makes things firm. The apparent contradiction resolves into the chapter's point: firmness is not a property things have in themselves but a relation they stand in to their maker. *Itqān* in later Arabic came to mean craftsmanship, doing a thing thoroughly and well, and the word retains that sense here. What the verse claims is not that the world is stable but that it is made properly.

**The Closing Clause Returns to Conduct**

After the cosmology comes a sentence about behaviour: He is aware of what you do. The transition is abrupt and it is the chapter's signature move.

An-Naml never leaves a description of the universe standing alone. The six questions about rain and rivers ended by asking about other gods. The statement that everything hidden is recorded led into a scene of judgment. Here the mountains dissolve into clouds and the verse closes by noting that your actions are observed. The scale collapses from planetary to personal in a single clause, and the reader is left with the implication the chapter has been pressing since verse 3: the vast thing being described is the setting in which small things are being counted.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:89

> Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded with what is better, and they will be secure from the horror on that Day.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Arriving With Something**

The verb is *jā'a bi* — to come bringing. The image is of a person arriving at the judgment carrying what he has, and the previous verses have already established the setting: a summons, a terror, and a gathering at which everyone comes humbled.

That framing tells us what a deed is in this passage. It is not a mark on a record maintained elsewhere but something the person brings with him, the way a traveller brings goods. The chapter has used arrival imagery repeatedly — envoys arriving with a gift, a queen arriving at a palace, contingents brought before their Lord. Here the whole of a life is compressed into what a person is holding when he gets there.

**What the Good Deed Is**

Al-Qurṭubī identifies the *ḥasanah* specifically: testifying that there is no god but God, and believing in Him. He supports it with a report that this testimony is the best of good things.

The identification narrows the verse considerably and it fits the chapter. An-Naml's entire argument has been about the object of worship — the hoopoe's report concerned a nation prostrating to the sun, the six questions each ended by asking whether there is a god besides God, and the queen's turning point was the sentence in which she submitted with Sulaymān. If the sūrah has been driving at one deed, it is this one. The reading is not the only available one, since *ḥasanah* in Arabic covers any good act, but al-Qurṭubī's is well anchored in the chapter's own concerns.

**"Better Than It"**

The reward is described only comparatively: *khayrun minhā*, better than it. Nothing is specified, and the commentators have offered several candidates for what the improvement consists of.

Al-Rāzī names knowledge of God in this world and the vision of God in the Hereafter. Al-Qurṭubī mentions the divine contentment, and also the arithmetic reading, in which what is better refers to the multiplication the Qur'an describes elsewhere — a good deed returned tenfold. Al-Ṭabarī offers the simplest and most contextual answer: what is better is precisely the security from terror named in the second half of this verse. That last reading has the advantage of not requiring anything from outside the sentence.

**Security as the Named Reward**

Whatever else is included, the verse states one benefit outright: *hum min fazaʿin yawma'idhin āminūn* — they are secure from the terror of that Day. The word for terror is the one used two verses earlier for the reaction to the trumpet.

The connection is direct. Verse 87 said everyone in the heavens and the earth would be terrified except whom God wills, and left the exception unidentified. This verse supplies a criterion. The commentators who guessed at martyrs or named angels were filling a gap that the chapter closes three verses later with a general rule rather than a list of persons. Whoever comes bringing a good deed is secure — which is a more useful answer than a roster, because it is a category a reader can join.

**Security Rather Than Reward**

Consider what kind of benefit is named first. Not a garden, not rivers, not companions — the imagery the Qur'an uses elsewhere at length. The first thing said about the outcome is the absence of fear.

That choice suits the passage it sits in. The preceding verses have described a sound that terrifies everyone alive, mountains dissolving, and a crowd arriving abased. Against that, the most valuable thing available is not an addition but a subtraction. The chapter made the same move with Lūṭ's household, whose deliverance was described simply as being brought out before the rain. An-Naml's picture of salvation is consistently extraction from a situation rather than elevation above one.

**A Hint About Punishment's Limits**

Al-Rāzī draws a further inference from the verse, and it touches a long-running theological dispute. If whoever brings a good deed receives what is better, then people of faith, even if punished in the Hereafter, will not be punished forever.

The reasoning runs through the comparative. A permanent penalty could not be described as receiving something better than the good one brought, so the good deed must retain some effect. Others resisted the inference, holding that the verse describes those whose record qualifies them outright. The status of the claim is worth being exact about, because it differs from the other disputed items in this chapter. This is not a transmitted report that might or might not have a chain; it is a piece of reasoning, and reasoning can be assessed directly. Al-Rāzī's inference is valid if *ḥasanah* here covers the testimony of faith, which al-Qurṭubī's reading of the previous clause grants, and if the reward is not conditional on the record as a whole, which the verse leaves open. So the argument holds on premises the verse permits without asserting. What an-Naml supplies is that permission, not the conclusion, and the wider question is settled — if at all — from the many other passages that speak to it.

**The Verse Pairs With the Next**

This sentence and the one after it are a matched set, and neither is complete alone. One deed brought, one outcome; another deed brought, another outcome. The symmetry is exact in structure and deliberately unbalanced in tone.

The good case is stated in seventeen Arabic words with a named benefit and an unspecified improvement. The evil case, in the next verse, is stated more briefly and ends with a question rather than a description. That asymmetry is consistent throughout the Qur'an's treatment of the two outcomes, where the return for good is described as multiplied and the return for evil as equivalent. The chapter's own statement of the principle arrives in the following verse's closing question.

**Whoever — With No Qualifier**

The pronoun is open: *man jā'a bi'l-ḥasanah*, whoever comes with a good deed. No nation is named, no lineage, no prior standing. The rule is stated in the most general terms Arabic allows.

That generality matters at this point in the chapter. The judgment scene of verses 83 and 84 emphasised that contingents are drawn from every community, which removed the protection of belonging. This verse removes the same barrier from the other direction: if membership does not condemn, neither does it save, and what counts is what a person arrives holding. Sheba's queen is the chapter's proof that the category is open. She came from a nation that worshipped the sun, ruled it, and ended by declaring her submission to the Lord of the worlds along with Sulaymān.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:90

> And whoever comes with an evil deed will be hurled face-first into the Fire. Are you rewarded except for what you used to do?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Matching Half**

This verse completes the pair begun in the previous one. The same construction opens it — whoever comes bringing — and the object is reversed. One arrives with a good deed, one with an evil deed, and the two outcomes are set beside each other.

The parallel makes the difference visible. The good deed received something better than itself; the evil deed receives exactly itself, as the closing question states. Nothing is added on the negative side. That asymmetry is the Qur'an's consistent position on the two ledgers, stated elsewhere as a tenfold return for good and a like-for-like return for wrong, and this verse is where an-Naml records it.

**What the Evil Deed Is**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both identify the *sayyi'ah* here as *shirk* — associating partners with God. The identification is the mirror of the good deed in the previous verse, which they took as the testimony of God's oneness.

Reading the pair this way makes the two verses a summary of the chapter rather than a general statement about ethics. An-Naml opened by describing believers who pray, give alms and are certain of the Hereafter, and it has spent its length on one question: who is worshipped. Sheba prostrated to the sun. Pharaoh claimed a status he did not have. Thamūd and Lūṭ's people rejected the messengers sent to them. The final assessment scene sorts by the same criterion the whole sūrah has been applying.

**"Face-First"**

The phrase is *kubbat wujūhuhum fī al-nār* — their faces cast down into the Fire. The Qur'an uses similar imagery in several chapters, describing the guilty gathered upon their faces or dragged upon them.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note that in Arabic idiom the face, like the head or the neck, can stand for the whole person, so that "their faces" may mean simply "they." On that reading the violence of the image is a figure of speech rather than a posture. The other reading takes the detail literally, matching the passages elsewhere that describe the guilty cast down inverted. Either way the connotation is the same in Arabic as in English: the face is where dignity resides, and to be put on it is to be humiliated rather than merely harmed.

**The Face in This Chapter**

There is a reason the detail lands where it does. The chapter has used the face for status throughout, and the contrast with its earlier scenes is sharp.

Sheba's queen entered a palace and was told to look at a floor; Sulaymān's response to the arrival of her throne was to lower himself in gratitude. The chapter's whole moral vocabulary has been about who bows and to what. A person who declined to lower his face voluntarily is described here as arriving at the same position involuntarily. That is not a flourish added for effect; it is the logic the sūrah has been building since the hoopoe reported a nation prostrating to the wrong object.

**The Closing Question**

The verse ends by turning to the reader: *hal tujzawna illā mā kuntum taʿmalūn* — are you repaid for anything other than what you used to do? The address shifts to the second person, and the shift is abrupt.

Up to this point the verse has described other people in the third person. The question breaks that frame and puts it to whoever is listening. Al-Rāzī records the alternative that the question is spoken to those being cast down, as they are cast down, which would keep it inside the scene. Both readings are available and the ambiguity may be intended: a question addressed to the condemned is overheard by the living, and its force is the same in either position.

**Nothing Is Added to the Penalty**

The question's content is a claim about justice and it is defensive in form. It anticipates an objection — that the outcome is excessive — and answers it by pointing out the exact correspondence between what was done and what is received.

The chapter has made this argument before in narrative. Thamūd's nine plotted and were destroyed by a plot; the word for their scheming and for what met it is identical. Lūṭ's people had been warned and received what the chapter calls the rain of those who were warned. In each case the penalty was described in the vocabulary of the offence. The closing question here states the principle those scenes illustrated: the return is the act, given back.

**The End of the Argument**

With this verse the chapter's case is complete. It has presented the Book, told four histories, asked six questions about the natural world, answered the demand for a date, described the record, staged the judgment, and now stated the rule by which the sorting is done.

What remains is three verses of instruction to the Prophet — what he was commanded, what he is to recite, and what to say to those who refuse. The argument does not end with a summary or an appeal. It ends with a man being told his job and a statement that the signs will yet be shown. That is a characteristic close for a chapter which has insisted throughout that evidence is available, the reading of it is the reader's own work, and the verdict belongs to someone else.

**A Rule, Not a Threat**

The second half of the verse is phrased as a question rather than as a warning, and the difference in register is deliberate. Threats describe what someone will do to you; this asks you to check an equation.

The Qur'an uses interrogatives throughout an-Naml for exactly this purpose, and the chapter has been full of them: is there a god besides God, have they not travelled, do they not see, did you deny without knowledge. Each puts the burden of the answer on the hearer. Ending the assessment scene the same way keeps the pattern intact to the last. A reader is not being told what will happen to him. He is being asked whether he can name any basis for the outcome other than his own conduct, and the question is rhetorical because there is none available.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:91

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I have only been commanded to worship the Lord of this city ˹of Mecca˺, Who has made it sacred, and to Him belongs everything. And I am commanded to be one of those who ˹fully˺ submit ˹to Him˺,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Closes With a Job Description**

After the judgment, the mountains and the weighing of deeds, the sūrah's last movement is a statement of what the Prophet was told to do. Not what he achieved, not what he was promised — what he was commanded.

The construction is restrictive: *innamā umirtu*, I have only been commanded. The particle limits the claim. He is not announcing a programme, a mission or a set of demands on his audience; he is reporting the contents of an instruction he received, and the instruction contains two items, worship and submission, with a third added in the next verse. Everything in the chapter's eighty-nine preceding verses is presented, at the end, as flowing from an order given to one man.

**"The Lord of This City"**

God could have been named in any number of ways here, and the chapter has used many — the Almighty, the All-Knowing, the Lord of the Mighty Throne, the Lord of the worlds. The closing statement chooses a local title: the Lord of this city.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that the reference is to Mecca, while adding the necessary qualification that God is Lord of all lands. The specificity is chosen for the audience. This is the home of the idolaters being addressed, and the phrase reminds them of what they have by virtue of living there — a place where they are safe from attack in a peninsula where safety was scarce. The argument is the same one al-Quraysh makes at greater length elsewhere, pointing at the security and provision of the sanctuary and asking its beneficiaries to worship its Lord.

**"Who Has Made It Sacred"**

*Ḥarramahā* — He made it forbidden, inviolable. The word is the root behind *ḥaram*, the sacred precinct, and it names a status the Meccans already recognised and enforced.

The commentators describe what the sanctity involved. Certain acts were prohibited during the pilgrimage; the place served as a refuge for those seeking sanctuary; its trees were not to be cut and its game not hunted. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note that the Arabs of the time observed these prohibitions, out of a persisting recognition of the sanctity of the Kaʿbah as the House built by Ibrāhīm. That is the crucial point for the verse's rhetoric. The Prophet is not asking them to accept a new claim about their city. He is pointing at an institution they already maintained and naming the one who established it.

**The Argument the Naming Makes**

Put the pieces together and a compressed argument emerges. You observe this place as inviolable. Its inviolability is not something you invented and not something you can revoke. The one who gave it that status is the one I have been commanded to worship.

This is the same method the chapter used in its six questions at verses 60 to 64. Each of those started from something the audience already accepted — that rain falls, that rivers run, that the desperate are sometimes answered — and asked who was responsible. None of them required the audience to concede a disputed premise first. The closing statement applies the technique to the most local fact available, the ground under their feet, and it is the last argument the sūrah makes.

**"To Him Belongs Everything"**

Immediately after the local title comes a universal one. He is the Lord of this city, *wa lahu kullu shay'* — and to Him belongs everything.

The pairing prevents exactly the misunderstanding the first phrase invites. Meccan religion was territorial in the ordinary ancient way, with deities attached to places and tribes holding particular gods. A claim about the Lord of this city, standing alone, would have been intelligible within that system and would have changed nothing. The second clause removes it from the system. The one whose sanctuary this is owns everything else too, which means the city is not His domain but an instance of it. The chapter made the same move with Sheba's throne, where the hoopoe's report of a mighty throne was answered by the mention of a greater one.

**Submission Named as a Command**

The second item in the instruction is that he be among the *muslimīn*, those who submit. It is put as a command rather than as a description, which is worth attending to.

The word has appeared twice before in this chapter, both times at decisive moments. Sulaymān's letter to Sheba ended by summoning her to come as submitters. Her declaration at the palace floor ended with her saying she submitted, with Sulaymān, to the Lord of the worlds. Now at the chapter's close the Prophet is commanded to be one of the same company. The term links the three figures across the sūrah — a king, a foreign queen and the man reciting — and puts them in the same category rather than in a hierarchy. He is not commanded to lead the submitters. He is commanded to be among them.

**Ending With Obedience Rather Than Authority**

There is a shape to this that the chapter has earned. An-Naml is full of commanding figures: a king with armies of jinn and men, a queen with a council, a court that sentenced a prophet. Its closing verse gives its own protagonist no command to issue.

What he is given is an account of orders received, and both of them concern his own conduct rather than anyone else's. He must worship; he must be among those who submit. The audience is told this rather than told to do anything themselves. That indirection is deliberate and it is consistent with what the previous verses established — he cannot make the deaf hear, the judgment is not his to deliver, and grieving over the refusal is to be set aside. A messenger whose responsibility has been narrowed that far has, at the end, only his own obedience to report.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:92

> and to recite the Quran.” Then whoever chooses to be guided, it is only for their own good. But whoever chooses to stray, say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I am only a warner.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Command**

The previous verse listed two instructions — worship and submission — and this one completes the set with a third: *wa an atluwa al-Qur'ān*, and to recite the Qur'an. The three together are the whole of what the Prophet reports being ordered.

The brevity of the list is its point. Nothing about building a community, defeating opponents, or securing acceptance appears in it. Two of the three items concern his relationship to God and the third concerns his relationship to the text. The chapter that opened by calling these the verses of the clear Book closes by identifying the reciting of that Book as the whole of what its reciter was told to do with it.

**Reciting, Not Enforcing**

The verb is *tilāwah*, reading aloud or reciting, and the absent verbs matter as much as the present one. He is not commanded to explain, to defend, to convince or to impose. He is commanded to say the words.

That narrowness follows directly from what verses 80 and 81 established. If there are people who cannot be made to hear, then the effectiveness of the delivery is not a variable the deliverer controls, and defining his duty by an outcome would make it undischargeable. Defining it by the act makes it something he can complete every day. Al-Rāzī's reading of the earlier verses, that relieving the Prophet of responsibility for belief strengthens his ability to declare the truth, has its practical expression here.

**"Whoever Is Guided Is Guided for Himself"**

The clause states a principle the Qur'an repeats in several chapters: the benefit of guidance accrues to the person guided, not to God and not to the messenger.

The form of words is precise — *fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsih*, he is guided only for his own soul. Related verses make the negative side explicit, saying that whoever is grateful is grateful for his own benefit and that God is beyond need of him. What this removes is any sense that acceptance is a favour done to the one calling. A preacher who believes otherwise will inevitably start bargaining, and the chapter has already shown what bargaining looks like: Sheba's queen sent a gift to see whether Sulaymān could be bought, and he refused it on precisely this ground, saying what God had given him was better than what she offered.

**The Asymmetry in the Sentence**

Look at how the two halves are built. For the guided, a statement of fact in the third person. For the astray, an instruction to the Prophet: *say, I am only a warner.*

The imbalance is informative. The guided need no reply from him at all; their case is described and closed. The straying require a response, and the response he is given is not an argument but a declaration of his own limits. This is the last of several places where the chapter hands him something to say in a difficult exchange — verse 64 gave him a demand for proof, verse 65 a statement of what nobody knows, verse 72 an answer to the demand for a date. The sūrah equips him for conversations rather than for victories.

**"Only a Warner"**

*Innamā anā min al-mundhirīn* — I am only one of the warners. The restrictive particle appears for the third time in two verses, and the description occurs throughout the Qur'an.

A warner has a defined and limited office: he states what is coming and the matter passes out of his hands. That framing has been at work in this chapter's histories. Ṣāliḥ warned Thamūd and then watched them plot. Lūṭ's people were described as those who had been warned, and the rain that fell on them was named accordingly. Neither prophet compelled anyone. The word also carries a note the English can lose — a warner is someone acting in your interest, which is why the same root gives the Qur'an its description of a community that receives notice before consequence.

**One of the Warners**

The phrasing includes a detail worth drawing out. He does not say *I am a warner* but *I am one of the warners*, placing himself within a company.

This matches the previous verse, where he was commanded to be among those who submit rather than to be their head. Twice in two sentences the Prophet is located inside a group rather than above one, and the chapter has spent fifty verses populating that group: Mūsā at the fire, Dāwūd and Sulaymān given knowledge, Ṣāliḥ sent to his brothers, Lūṭ addressing his people. The claim being made at the chapter's close is one of continuity with them, which is the same claim verse 76 made about the Book and the disputes of the Children of Israel.

**Guidance and Straying Both Described as Choices**

The translation renders the two clauses as choosing to be guided and choosing to stray, and the Arabic verbs support that reading. Both are in forms that describe the person's own action rather than something done to him.

That matters given what the chapter said at verse 4, where deeds were made attractive to those who do not believe in the Hereafter so that they wander blindly. Read alone, that verse could suggest the outcome was arranged in advance. Read with this one, the sequence is clear: the disbelief comes first and the wandering follows it. An-Naml puts the two statements eighty-eight verses apart and does not reconcile them explicitly, but the closing verse's grammar returns the initiative to the person. The chapter's last word on the matter leaves him holding it.

## Sūrah an-Naml 27:93

> And say, “All praise is for Allah! He will show you His signs, and you will recognize them. And your Lord is never unaware of what you do.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Instruction Is Praise**

After ninety-two verses of argument, narrative, warning and judgment, the chapter's final command to its Prophet is to say *al-ḥamdu li-Llāh*. Praise, not a summary and not an appeal.

The placement is deliberate and it completes a frame. Praise has appeared at the pivot of this chapter's greatest scenes: Dāwūd and Sulaymān said it when given knowledge, and Sulaymān said it again in a different form when the throne arrived. Both times it followed a gift. Here it follows something else — a long refusal by an audience that has not accepted the message. The chapter ends by putting the same words in the mouth of a man who has been given no visible success, which says something about what praise is anchored to.

**"He Will Show You His Signs"**

The promise is future and it is addressed to the audience rather than to the Prophet: *sayurīkum āyātih*, He will show you His signs. The chapter has been pointing at signs from its first verse, and now it says more are coming.

Al-Ṭabarī reads this as signs both within the soul and in the created order, and the reading connects to well-known verses elsewhere — the promise that God will show them His signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear that it is the truth, and the statement that there are signs on the earth for those with certainty, and within your own selves. Taken this way the closing line is an invitation with no deadline. The evidence is not finished; the audience has not seen the last of it.

**The Other Reading: Signs Too Late**

Al-Rāzī records a harder interpretation. The sentence may be a warning rather than an invitation: they will indeed see God's signs, but only after it is too late, when the signs are the ones that accompany the end of the world.

Both readings fit the grammar, and the chapter supports each. It has just described a trumpet, mountains moving like clouds, and a creature brought out of the earth to announce that people had no certainty — signs of exactly the kind al-Rāzī means. But it has also, in the preceding verse, spoken of whoever chooses to be guided, which assumes the option is open. The ambiguity may be the point. A sentence that reads as promise to one listener and as threat to another is well suited to close a chapter whose argument has been that the same evidence lands differently depending on who receives it.

**"And You Will Recognize Them"**

The second clause is the one that decides the tone, and it is not softened on either reading: *fa-taʿrifūnahā*, and you will know them. Whatever the signs turn out to be, they will not be ambiguous when they come.

This is the chapter's answer to the charge it levelled at the judgment. Verse 84 asked the deniers whether they rejected the signs without encompassing them in knowledge. This verse promises knowledge — recognition, the thing they lacked. What it does not promise is that the recognition will arrive in time to be useful, and the chapter has already described the case where it does not: a creature announcing the failure of certainty, and a contingent standing silent before a question they cannot answer.

**Not Unaware**

The final clause is *wa mā rabbuka bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn* — your Lord is not heedless of what you do. The chapter has said versions of this four times, and it ends on it.

Verse 74 said God knows what breasts conceal and what they disclose. Verse 75 said nothing hidden anywhere is outside a clear record. Verse 88 closed with the statement that He is aware of what you do. Now the last sentence of the sūrah says it once more, and in the negative form, which is the strongest of the four. A positive claim about knowledge can be heard as general; denying heedlessness answers the specific hope that something has gone unnoticed. The chapter's opponents included nine men who swore a night oath believing themselves unobserved, and its close forecloses on the assumption they were operating under.

**A Chapter That Ends Without Resolution**

Nothing is concluded here. No one converts, no punishment falls, the Meccans are not answered, and the judgment stays in the future. The chapter simply stops, with a man told to praise God and an audience told they will see more.

That is unusual and it is consistent with everything the sūrah has done. It refused to name the Hour, refused a date for the reckoning, placed the adjudication of religious disputes in the Hereafter, and told the Prophet his responsibility ended at the recitation. A chapter organised around deferral could hardly finish with a resolution. What it offers instead is the posture to hold while waiting, and the posture is the one the verse names first.

**The Ant and the Last Verse**

It is worth returning, at the end, to the incident the chapter is named for. An ant saw an army approaching and warned her colony to get inside. Sulaymān heard her, smiled, and asked to be marshalled into gratitude for the favour.

Almost nothing else in an-Naml is as small as that scene, and the chapter takes its title from it rather than from the queen, the throne, or the judgment. What the ant did was notice something coming and speak about it in time. What Sulaymān did was hear a warning not addressed to him and respond with thanks. Between them they perform the two acts this final verse asks for — recognising signs and saying *al-ḥamdu li-Llāh* — and the chapter that closes with both commands was named, from the beginning, after the creature who managed them first.
